Fundamentals / Physics / Waves and optics

Waves and optics

Follow a wave from its source through interfaces and apertures, then use interference and diffraction to explain what an image or a spectrum can resolve.

Chapters, problem sheets and the applied study in reading order. For a paper copy, use your browser’s print command. Open the worked solutions you want to include before printing.

Read the subject in depth

Each volume develops its arguments in sequence, with worked problems and links to experiments.

  • Optical fields, instruments and observations

    Trace an optical field through a physical instrument. Calculate the signal a detector measures and test the approximations on which the interpretation rests.

Advanced study across the subject

Each sequence connects the course to detailed chapters in neighbouring subjects. The complete course PDF includes their derivations, examples and exercises.

  1. A wave in space, matter and a guide

    Rebuild a propagating mode from the field equations. Track vector polarisation and flux through an interface, then separate material from geometric dispersion.

  2. What a detector samples

    Compare radiation, scattering and the measured diffraction pattern. Examine how aperture sampling, transfer calibration and finite coherence alter the observable.

  3. Mechanical and thermal effects of light

    Calculate how a beam can translate a bead, couple resonances or exchange energy with a moving boundary. Test each proposal against a measured signal.

How the course fits together

  1. Chapters 1–4

    A local force balance on a string gives a wave speed. Superpose travelling waves to satisfy interfaces and make packets. In the short-wavelength limit their phase fronts lead to rays, focal lengths and images.

  2. Chapters 5–8

    Restore the information that ray tracing discards. Polarisation describes a vector field, coherence determines which fields can interfere, and an aperture maps a wavefront to a diffraction pattern that limits image detail.

  3. Chapters 9–12

    Repeated optical paths select frequencies in an interferometer. Many illuminated grooves resolve nearby wavelengths. Beams and widely separated receivers offer two different ways to infer spatial structure from wave phase.

Chapter 1 · Travelling waves and energy on a string

Derive wave speed from the local force balance and determine how much average power a transverse travelling wave carries.

From first principles

Derive the wave speed from transverse force balance

Select a short string segment of unperturbed length \(dx\) and mass \(\mu\,dx\), where \(\mu\) is mass per unit length. Assume a flexible string with uniform tension \(T\), small slopes and negligible damping. Its neighbours pull tangentially at either end. For small angles the transverse component is approximately \(T\) times the slope. Oppositely directed pulls cancel when the two ends have equal slopes, while a change in slope produces a net transverse force.

\[dF_y=T\left[\partial_xy(x+dx,t)-\partial_xy(x,t)\right]\simeq T\,\partial_x^2y\,dx .\]

Newton's second law equates this force to the mass times transverse acceleration. Divide by segment length and compare with the derivatives of a profile translated at speed \(c\).

\[\partial_t^2y=\frac T\mu\,\partial_x^2y,\]
\[y=f(x-ct)\ \Longrightarrow\ \partial_t^2y=c^2f'',\]
\[\partial_x^2y=f'' .\]

A freely travelling shape therefore moves at \(c=\sqrt{T/\mu}\). Extra tension supplies a larger force for the same curvature. More mass per unit length reduces the acceleration produced by that force.

Background · Wavenumber
\[k=2\pi/\lambda\]

The wavenumber gives phase change per unit distance in a homogeneous medium. Wavelength must be specified in the medium in which propagation is calculated.

Derivation and applications

Calculate the energy and power of a travelling pulse

A single pulse has no unique frequency, but a measurement of its slope can still determine the total energy it carries. Model a long string with constant tension \(T\), line density \(\mu\), negligible bending stiffness and small transverse slope. Choose a right-moving displacement \(y(x,t)=f(x-ct)\), with \(c=\sqrt{T/\mu}\). Stretching a segment increases its length by approximately \((\partial_xy)^2dx/2\), while its transverse velocity is \(\partial_ty=-c\partial_xy\). The kinetic and elastic energies per unit unperturbed length are consequently equal.

\[u=\tfrac12\mu(\partial_ty)^2+\tfrac12T(\partial_xy)^2=T[f'(x-ct)]^2.\]

The left portion of the string does work on the right portion through its transverse force at a cut. With positive power defined towards increasing \(x\), multiply that force by the velocity of the point at the cut. The result is nonnegative even as the two slopes of the passing pulse have opposite signs.

\[P=-T(\partial_xy)(\partial_ty)=cT[f'(x-ct)]^2=cu .\]

Consider an isolated Gaussian displacement with peak height \(A\) and spatial scale \(s\),

\[f(\xi)=A\exp[-\xi^2/(2s^2)].\]

Its slope is \(-\xi f/s^2\). Integrating over the entire string using the second moment of a Gaussian gives

\[E=T\frac{A^2}{s^4}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\xi^2 e^{-\xi^2/s^2}d\xi=\frac{\sqrt\pi\,TA^2}{2s}.\]

A broader pulse of the same height has gentler slopes and stores less energy. The displacement maximum itself has zero instantaneous power because its slope vanishes. Two power peaks pass a fixed sensor on either side of that maximum.

Worked example

Budget the energy of a laboratory pulse

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A string under 40.0 N tension carries a Gaussian pulse of height 4.00 mm with \(s=50.0\) mm. Its energy is \((\sqrt\pi/2)(40.0)(0.00400)^2/0.0500=11.3\) mJ. The largest slope is \((A/s)e^{-1/2}=0.0485\), supporting the small-slope approximation. If the string absorbs the pulse completely at its far end, the absorber receives the full 11.3 mJ.

Second application

Reconstruct pulse width from two power maxima

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

On a second string the pulse speed is 25.0 m/s. Since \(P\) is proportional to \(\xi^2e^{-\xi^2/s^2}\), differentiating gives maxima at \(\xi=\pm s\). Two measured power peaks 6.00 ms apart therefore imply \(s=c\Delta t/2=75.0\) mm. For \(A=3.00\) mm and \(T=18.0\) N the predicted peak power is \(cT(A/s)^2/e=0.265\) W, an independent amplitude check.

Try it · String pulse

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Choose the displacement and the approximation

Strike one end of a stretched rope. A crest moves down its length, yet a marked point on the rope moves mainly up and down. Distinguishing the motion of a disturbance from the motion of the material is the starting point for predicting wave speed. Consider a uniform flexible string under tension \(F_T\) with mass per unit length \(\mu\). Its transverse displacement from a straight equilibrium line is \(y(x,t)\). Assume slopes \(|\partial_xy|\ll1\), negligible bending stiffness and negligible damping.

These assumptions have measurable consequences. To first order a segment of horizontal length \(dx\) retains that length and has mass \(\mu\,dx\). The tension magnitude can be treated as its equilibrium value, while the directions at the two ends vary with the local slope. For larger slopes the tension can change as the string stretches, and longitudinal motion need no longer decouple from transverse displacement.

Balance forces on one small segment

At the two ends of a short segment the vertical tension components have opposite signs. Expand the difference of the slopes to first order in the segment length. Newton’s law then reads

\[\begin{aligned} \mu\,dx\,\partial_t^2 y &= F_T\,[\partial_x y(x+dx,t)-\partial_x y(x,t)]\\ &= F_T\,dx\,\partial_x^2 y+O(dx^2). \end{aligned}\]

Dividing by the segment mass and taking the limit gives the wave equation with speed \(c\),

\[\partial_t^2 y=c^2\partial_x^2 y,\]
\[c=\sqrt{\frac{F_T}{\mu}}.\]

The equation predicts that a local curvature accelerates material towards straightening the string. Any twice-differentiable shape \(g(x-ct)\) travels right at speed \(c\) because its second time derivative is \(c^2g''\). A second independent shape \(h(x+ct)\) travels left. Their superposition solves the same linear equation, and initial displacement alone generally does not specify how the disturbance splits. An initial velocity field supplies the second piece of information.

For example, lifting a narrow region and releasing the whole string from rest produces opposite-going copies of half the initial displacement on a long uniform string. Launching the same shape exclusively to the right requires \(y_t=-cy_x\) at release. A sequence of still images can reveal the direction of motion, but a single displacement profile usually cannot.

Read a frequency from moving wavefronts

A sinusoidal displacement at a fixed point repeats every source period \(T_s\). A microphone in motion does not stay at that point between observations. To relate its measured frequency to the source clock, choose the positive axis along the sound ray, from the source towards the receiver. Let \(c\) be the sound speed in still air and \(v_s,v_r\) the signed source and receiver velocities in that same air frame. Assume steady subsonic motion along the ray and negligible reflection.

At emission of the next crest, the first crest has travelled \(cT_s\) and the emitter has moved \(v_sT_s\). The separation of the crests ahead of the emitter is consequently \(\lambda=(c-v_s)T_s\). The receiver intercepts them at relative speed \(c-v_r\). Dividing the distance swept per second by the spacing gives

\[\frac{f_r}{f_s}=\frac{c-v_r}{c-v_s}.\]

A speaker on a test trolley emits 680 Hz while travelling at 15 m/s towards a microphone moving towards it at 5 m/s. Take \(c=340\) m/s. The crest spacing is \(325/680=0.478\) m, and the microphone sweeps through the crests at 345 m/s. It registers \(721.85\) Hz. Holding the microphone still instead gives \(711.38\) Hz. The two instruments approach one another in either experiment, but moving the emitter changes wavelength while moving only the microphone leaves it fixed.

Two checks prevent sign errors. If both instruments travel at the same speed the ratio is one. For velocities small compared with \(c\), expansion to first order gives

\[\Delta f/f_s\simeq(v_s-v_r)/c.\]

Separate dependence on the two air-frame velocities reappears at second order. The distinction matters in a wind tunnel, where ground speed and air-frame speed differ. The derivation uses a material medium and cannot be used unchanged for light in vacuum.

Practise on sheet 1 · Recover a source speed from two microphones. Further reading · University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign · Physics 406, Acoustical Physics of Music · Lecture III, part 2 · Doppler effect for a moving source and observer.

Locate the energy being transmitted

Each segment stores kinetic energy \(\mu y_t^2dx/2\). Stretching it adds length \([\sqrt{1+y_x^2}-1]dx\simeq y_x^2dx/2\), costing potential energy \(F_Ty_x^2dx/2\) at the assumed constant tension. Defining their sum per length as \(u\), direct differentiation and substitution of the wave equation yield

\[\partial_tu=\partial_x(F_Ty_ty_x).\]

Thus the positive-direction power is \(P=-F_Ty_ty_x\) and \(\partial_tu+\partial_xP=0\).

For a right-moving wave \(y_t=-cy_x\), so the power is positive and equals \(cu\). A left-moving wave carries power in the opposite direction. The signs come from the mechanical work done across a cut. In a standing wave the two powers need not add constructively even where the displacement amplitude is large. An average amplitude alone therefore measures power only once the propagation conditions have been established.

Compare two launches

Let \(F_T=100\) N, \(\mu=0.010\) kg/m and frequency \(f=20.0\) Hz. The wave speed is 100 m/s and wavelength is 5.0 m. A sinusoidal right-moving displacement of peak amplitude \(A=3.0\) mm has peak transverse material speed \(2\pi f A=0.377\) m/s. The largest slope is \(2\pi A/\lambda=0.00377\), supporting the small-slope approximation.

The mean power is

\[\mu c(2\pi f A)^2/2=0.0711\,\mathrm{W}.\]

Doubling the amplitude at the same frequency quadruples mean power while keeping the propagation speed fixed. If the tension is doubled at fixed amplitude and frequency, the speed and wavelength both increase by \(\sqrt2\) and the mean power increases by \(\sqrt2\). These predictions separate the energy required to maintain a sinusoidal drive from the arrival time of a signal along the string.

The two field measurements needed for instantaneous power are the transverse velocity and the local slope. Measuring displacement alone at one point gives velocity after differentiation, but not the spatial slope. A second nearby displacement probe can approximate that slope if its spacing is short compared with the wavelength. Standing waves illustrate why this matters: a point may move rapidly while the time-averaged transport across it is zero because counterpropagating components carry equal powers.

Hear the initial shape in the harmonics of a string

On a finite uniform string with fixed ends the boundary values must vanish at \(x=0,L\) at every time. Separating the wave equation gives spatial eigenfunctions \(\sin(n\pi x/L)\) and angular frequencies \(n\pi c/L\). If the initial transverse velocity is zero, each excited mode starts in its cosine time phase. To determine its amplitude multiply the initial displacement \(f(x)\) by the corresponding sine and integrate. Orthogonality gives

\[y(x,t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}b_n\sin(n\pi x/L)\cos(n\pi ct/L),\]
\[b_n=\frac2L\int_0^L f(x)\sin(n\pi x/L)\,dx .\]

Pluck the midpoint upward by height \(h\ll L\) and release the resulting triangle from rest. Its slopes are \(2h/L\) and \(-2h/L\). Integrating the coefficient by parts makes the endpoint term vanish because \(f\) vanishes at both ends. The two remaining constant-slope integrals give

\[b_n=8h\sin(n\pi/2)/(n^2\pi^2).\]

Reflection symmetry suppresses every even mode. The negative coefficient for the third mode fixes its phase relative to the fundamental. Its energy remains positive.

Take \(L=0.600\) m, \(c=120\) m/s and \(h=3.00\) mm. The first and third frequencies are 100 and 300 Hz and the amplitudes are \(+2.43\) and \(-0.270\) mm. Energy in a mode is proportional to \(n^2b_n^2\) because its maximum spatial slope scales with \(n\). The third mode therefore has one ninth of the first mode's energy, even though its displacement amplitude magnitude is one ninth and its amplitude squared is only one eighty-first. A pickup at an individual mode node may suppress the measured line even when the mode is present in the string.

Practise on sheet 1 · Predict which note disappears for an off-centre pluck. Further reading · Massachusetts Institute of Technology · 8.03SC, The Physics of Waves · §6.2.1 The String with Fixed Ends; §6.2.4 Plucking a String.

Reconstruct two outgoing pulses from initial data

A long uniform string can be started by specifying both its displacement and its velocity. Assume small slopes, constant tension and observations made before reflections return from the ends. The one-dimensional wave equation factors into left- and right-travelling pieces, so write \(y(x,t)=F(x-ct)+G(x+ct)\). At the release time the measured shape is \(f(x)=F(x)+G(x)\) and the transverse velocity is \(g(x)=-cF'(x)+cG'(x)\). Differentiating the shape equation and solving this pair gives

\[F'=(f'-g/c)/2,\]
\[G'=(f'+g/c)/2.\]

Integrate those derivatives and evaluate the two pieces at their respective moving arguments. Constants cancel when the prescribed initial shape is restored, yielding the solution

\[y(x,t)=\frac{f(x-ct)+f(x+ct)}2+\frac1{2c}\int_{x-ct}^{x+ct}g(s)\,ds .\]

The interval in this formula contains every initial point whose signal can have reached the observation point by time \(t\). A localized shape released from rest splits into two half-height copies. A localized velocity impulse produces a different response because the integral accumulates the initial velocity over a growing interval. Measuring only a release photograph cannot distinguish these preparations. A right-travelling shape alone requires the specific initial velocity \(g=-cf'\), consistent with the local slope-to-velocity relation of a travelling wave.

Strike a short region of the string

A string has \(c=50.0\) m/s and line density \(\mu=0.0200\) kg/m. Initially it is straight. Impart transverse velocity \(g_0=0.400\) m/s over the interval \(-a<x<a\) with \(a=0.100\) m, and zero elsewhere. This rectangular velocity profile idealizes a short, distributed tap. At the centre, while \(ct<a\), the integration interval lies inside the struck region and \(y(0,t)=g_0 t\). At 1.00 ms the centre has moved 0.400 mm. After \(a/c=2.00\) ms the entire initial velocity region lies inside the interval, and the centre takes the constant displacement \(g_0a/c=0.800\) mm.

At that later stage the centre is at rest and locally straight while two wavefront regions move away in opposite directions. The energy that initially resided in the moving patch was \(\mu(2a)g_0^2/2=0.320\) mJ, with half propagating in each direction by symmetry. A permanent shift at a fixed point of an infinite ideal string costs no local curvature energy after the fronts pass. Finite fixed ends eventually send return waves, invalidating the infinite-string prediction at late times. An array of displacement sensors would see a linear rise followed by a local plateau, while a velocity probe would record a finite-time pulse and return to zero.

The example also predicts what a position probe placed outside the struck region records before any boundary reflection. Its signal begins when the nearer edge of the initial interval enters the backwards propagation cone. It reaches the plateau when the farther edge enters. At a point 0.500 m from centre, those events occur at 8.00 and 12.0 ms. The local rise lasts 4.00 ms, twice the centre rise time, but only one travelling front contributes there. A second sensor equally far on the opposite side should give the same record, providing an immediate symmetry check on the applied tap.

Does it matter which instrument is moving?

Compare two acoustic measurements at the same closing speed in still air. Move the source in the first experiment and move the microphone in the second. Their rates of approach agree but their crest spacings differ.

Chapter 2 · Impedance, reflection and standing waves

Derive amplitude and energy reflection at a string junction and recover the resonance conditions of a finite resonator.

From first principles

Match displacement and force at a string joint

Join two long strings under the same tension \(T\), with linear densities \(\mu_1,\mu_2\). Assume small transverse displacements and a massless joint with no external force. A sinusoidal wave arrives from string one. Write \(A,B,C\) for complex incident, reflected and transmitted displacement amplitudes at the join. Because the strings remain attached, their endpoint positions agree at each instant.

\[A+B=C .\]

The transverse components of the two tensions must balance. A right-going wave has slope amplitude \(ikA\), whereas reversing its direction changes the sign of that slope. With \(k_j=\omega/c_j\), the force equality becomes

\[Tk_1(A-B)=Tk_2C,\]
\[Z_j=T/c_j=\sqrt{T\mu_j}.\]

Eliminate \(C\) using continuity and solve for the two wave amplitudes. The sign of the reflection records a possible reversal of displacement.

\[\frac BA=\frac{Z_1-Z_2}{Z_1+Z_2},\]
\[\frac CA=\frac{2Z_1}{Z_1+Z_2}.\]

A matched joint gives zero reflection. Equal-frequency incident and reflected waves overlap to produce fixed interference minima and maxima. Two reflecting endpoints select a standing pattern when the round trip reproduces the original phase.

Derivation and applications

Scatter a string wave from a small bead

Two strings with identical impedance can reflect a wave at their joint if the joint has inertia. Attach a bead of mass \(M\) between two long uniform portions of a string under tension \(T\). Assume a pointlike bead, small transverse motion and negligible damping. Write displacements proportional to \(e^{-i\omega t}\), with incident, reflected and transmitted amplitudes \(A\), \(B\) and \(C\). Continuity of the string displacement gives \(C=A+B\). Newton's law for the bead requires the difference in transverse tension components to accelerate this common displacement.

\[-M\omega^2 C=T\,[\partial_xy(0^+)-\partial_xy(0^-)]_{\rm amplitude}=ikT[C-(A-B)]=2ikTB .\]

The reflected wave has the opposite spatial phase gradient, which produces the second copy of \(B\) in the tension jump. Introduce the travelling-wave impedance \(Z=T/c\) and dimensionless mass parameter \(s=\omega M/(2Z)\). Using \(k=\omega/c\), the junction equation gives \(B=isC\). Eliminate \(C\) through displacement continuity to obtain

\[r=\frac BA=\frac{is}{1-is},\]
\[t=\frac CA=\frac{1}{1-is}.\]

For identical strings the mean incident and outgoing powers carry the same impedance and frequency factors. Squaring the amplitude ratios gives the energy fractions and explicitly accounts for the entire incident power.

\[\mathcal R=\frac{s^2}{1+s^2},\]
\[\mathcal T=\frac1{1+s^2},\]
\[\mathcal R+\mathcal T=1 .\]

For slowly oscillating signals the bead follows the string and becomes almost transparent. At high frequency the transmitted amplitude tends to zero and \(r\) approaches \(-1\), the displacement reversal of a fixed endpoint.

Worked example

Predict the power passing a marker bead

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

With \(Z=0.500\) kg/s, \(M=5.00\) g and \(\omega=200\) rad/s, the junction has \(s=1.00\). Each outgoing string carries half of the incident power. The transmitted displacement magnitude is \(1/\sqrt{2}=0.707\) of the incident value, and \(t=(1+i)/2\) in the selected phase convention. An incident 12.0 mW wave therefore delivers 6.00 mW beyond the bead.

Second application

Weigh an attached marker by reflection

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Another string has \(Z=0.300\) kg/s. At 30.0 Hz its reflected power fraction is 0.200. The inversion \(s=\sqrt{\mathcal R/(1-\mathcal R)}\) gives 0.500. Its inferred bead mass is

\[2Zs/(2\pi f)=1.59\,\mathrm{g}.\]

Doubling the frequency should bring \(s\) to unity and the reflected power to 50.0 percent, a check independent of the calibrated incident displacement.

Try it · Bead scattering

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Make the joint part of the model

A travelling disturbance reaches the junction between a light string and a heavy one held at the same tension. The animation of the junction might show an inverted reflected crest. To tell which sign and amplitude are physically possible, isolate the joint. We assume it has negligible mass, that the strings are tied without slip and that it experiences no externally applied transverse force. The displacement must be continuous and the transverse components of tension must balance.

Place the junction at \(x=0\) and let positive \(x\) point into the transmitted string. Use a harmonic time convention \(e^{-i\omega t}\). In the first string write the complex displacement as \(A_i e^{ik_1x}+A_r e^{-ik_1x}\), and in the second as \(A_t e^{ik_2x}\). Since the joint experiences one oscillation frequency, wavelength changes with the respective speed. A change of wavelength alone says nothing about the reflected power.

Match motion and transverse force

At the joint the displacement condition gives

\[A_i+A_r=A_t.\]

The force condition gives

\[F_{T1}k_1(A_i-A_r)=F_{T2}k_2 A_t.\]

Defining the string impedance as \(Z_j=F_{Tj}/c_j=\sqrt{F_{Tj}\mu_j}\) converts the second equation into \(Z_1(A_i-A_r)=Z_2 A_t\). Under the equal static tensions assumed here, different impedances arise from different linear mass densities. Eliminating the transmitted amplitude gives

\[r=\frac{A_r}{A_i}=\frac{Z_1-Z_2}{Z_1+Z_2},\]
\[t=\frac{A_t}{A_i}=\frac{2Z_1}{Z_1+Z_2}.\]

The sign of \(r\) describes displacement relative to the incident displacement at the junction. For \(Z_2\gg Z_1\) the junction approaches a fixed end, \(r\to-1\). For \(Z_2\ll Z_1\) it approaches a free end, \(r\to+1\). These limiting experiments check the force signs independently.

Background · Which sign changes at a free end?

A free end of an ideal stretched string carries zero transverse force, hence zero slope. For harmonic displacement \(y=(A e^{ikx}+B e^{-ikx})e^{-i\omega t}\), differentiating reverses the sign of the reflected term. If the end lies at \(x=0\), zero slope requires \(B=A\). Holding the same end at zero displacement instead gives \(B=-A\).

\[y(0)=2Ae^{-i\omega t}\quad\text{(free end)},\]
\[\partial_xy(0)=2ikAe^{-i\omega t}\quad\text{(fixed end)}.\]

Either end returns all incident power. A sensor measuring string displacement sees an antinode at the free end and a node at the fixed end. A force sensor responds to slope and gives the opposite pattern. State the measured quantity when assigning a phase reversal to a reflected wave.

Compare amplitude and power

The mean power of a sinusoidal travelling wave is \(Z\omega^2|A|^2/2\). Thus the reflected power fraction is \(|r|^2\) and the transmitted power fraction is \((Z_2/Z_1)|t|^2\). Adding the two fractions gives one for the ideal lossless junction. A transmitted displacement larger than the incoming displacement is consistent with energy conservation when the second medium has sufficiently small impedance.

Suppose both strings have tension 80 N, while \(\mu_1=0.0050\) and \(\mu_2=0.020\) kg/m. The ratio of impedances is two, so \(r=-1/3\) and \(t=2/3\). Of 0.90 W entering, 0.10 W is reflected and 0.80 W travels onwards. Reversing the incident direction makes \(r=+1/3\) and \(t=4/3\) while leaving the reflected fraction at one ninth. A sensor that records only displacement peaks could wrongly interpret the reverse experiment as amplification.

Build a resonator using two reflections

Put reflecting ends a distance \(L\) apart. After a round trip a monochromatic wave acquires propagation phase \(2kL\) and the phases of both reflection coefficients. A repeated wave pattern requires their sum to be an integer multiple of \(2\pi\). With two fixed ends, the two sign reversals add a complete cycle and \(k_n=n\pi/L\), \(n=1,2,\ldots\). With one fixed and one free end there is only one reversal, giving \(k_n=(2n-1)\pi/(2L)\). The boundary conditions, rather than the length alone, determine whether an antinode or a node sits at an end.

For a 0.60 m string with speed 120 m/s the fixed–fixed fundamental is 100 Hz. The fixed–free fundamental is 50 Hz. Replacing one ideal end by a partially transmitting attachment also leaks energy each round trip. One then observes a finite resonance width and a decaying signal after switching off the drive. A resonance frequency tests phase closure, whereas the decay time tests energy escape and internal loss.

A pulse experiment can also distinguish a fixed end from a free end without measuring force. At a fixed end the incoming and reflected displacements must cancel, so an upward pulse returns downward. At a free end the transverse force is zero, requiring slopes to cancel; the displacement returns with the original sign. In both cases ideal reflection conserves energy. A negative displacement reflection coefficient records a phase reversal, even though the reflected energy remains positive.

Terminate a string with a spring and a damper

A fixed or free termination gives only two extreme reflection phases. Connect the end of a uniform string to a linear spring of stiffness \(k_s\) and a viscous damper \(b\) acting in parallel against ground. The endpoint is massless, and the incoming transverse displacement is harmonic. Use temporal phasors \(e^{i\omega t}\) and let the string extend from negative \(x\) to its terminal at zero. Define the positive load force as the force the string supplies to the termination. The load force divided by endpoint velocity is \(Z_L=b+k_s/(i\omega)\). A travelling string wave has characteristic force-to-velocity ratio \(Z_0=\sqrt{T\mu}\).

The incident and reflected endpoint velocities add, but their forces enter with opposite signs because their directions of transport differ. If \(r\) is the reflected-to-incident velocity or displacement amplitude, the boundary equation is

\[Z_0(1-r)=Z_L(1+r).\]

Thus

\[r=\frac{Z_0-Z_L}{Z_0+Z_L},\]
\[1-|r|^2=\frac{4Z_0\,\mathrm{Re}\,Z_L}{|Z_0+Z_L|^2}.\]

For a pure spring the reflected power fraction is one, although its phase varies continuously with frequency. The spring temporarily stores energy and returns it. A matched pure damper \(b=Z_0\) with no spring absorbs an incident wave completely. In the low-frequency limit a nonzero spring has a large impedance magnitude and its end approximates a fixed boundary. As frequency increases the spring contributes less force for the same velocity, and the damper determines the response.

Infer the absorbed power from an endpoint motion

Choose \(T=100\) N and \(\mu=0.0100\) kg/m, giving \(Z_0=1.00\) N s/m. Fit \(b=1.00\) N s/m and \(k_s=20.0\) N/m, and drive at \(\omega=20.0\) rad/s. The load is \(Z_L=1.00-i1.00\) N s/m, hence

\[r=i/(2-i)=(-1+2i)/5.\]

Its magnitude squared is \(0.200\). Of 10.0 mW incident power, 2.00 mW returns and 8.00 mW is dissipated.

The incident velocity amplitude is

\[\sqrt{2P_{\rm in}/Z_0}=0.141\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Total endpoint velocity amplitude is \(|1+r|\) times this, or \(0.126\) m/s. Independent damper heating is \(b|v_{\rm end}|^2/2=8.00\) mW as required. The endpoint displacement amplitude is

\[0.126/20.0=6.32\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

At a much higher frequency the same termination approaches the matched damper limit. A complex reflection measurement therefore separates reactive force from dissipative force even when an amplitude-only measurement happens to give the same reflected fraction for two different fixtures.

One can invert the experiment to estimate the fixture properties from reflected phase. Algebraically the boundary equation gives

\[Z_L=Z_0(1-r)/(1+r).\]

The real part estimates the damper coefficient, and multiplying the negative imaginary part by angular frequency estimates spring stiffness. Near a fixed boundary the measured reflection is close to minus one. The small denominator then makes the stiffness estimate sensitive to phase errors even when reflected power is measured accurately. Repeating the experiment near the fixture's spring–damper crossover gives a better-conditioned comparison between the two independently inferred components.

Repeating the inversion at two frequencies also tests whether the assumed spring and damper coefficients remain constant.

Infer a reflection from a standing-wave scan

Superpose an incident harmonic wave of unit amplitude and a returned wave of real amplitude \(r\). A probe that measures the oscillation amplitude at each point reports the modulus of their complex sum. Squaring first and adding intensities would erase the interference.

Problem sheet 1 · Pulses, junctions and resonances

Use the force and energy balances of Lectures 1 and 2 to interpret motion at a string junction.

Select the direction of a pulse

A string has tension 72 N and linear density 0.020 kg/m. At one instant its displacement is \(y=Ae^{-(x/w)^2}\) with \(A=2.0\) mm and \(w=0.10\) m. Specify the initial velocity field that sends the pulse only to the right. Find the material velocity at \(x=w\) and the time for its peak to travel 3.0 m.

Relate the initial velocity to the slope

The speed is

\[c=\sqrt{72/0.020}=60\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

A right-moving profile satisfies

\[y_t=-cy_x=2cAx\,e^{-(x/w)^2}/w^2.\]

At \(x=w\), the positive material velocity is

\[2(60)(0.0020)e^{-1}/0.10=0.883\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

The crest travels 3.0 m in 0.050 s. Setting all velocities to zero instead produces two pulses in opposite directions, each with half the original displacement profile.

Match a measured reflection

A small junction connects strings under equal tension. A displacement pulse reflects with amplitude \(-0.20\) times the incident amplitude. Find the ratio of impedances, the ratio of linear mass densities and the transmitted power fraction for a long sinusoidal train. Does the reflected displacement sign change when the source is moved to the other side?

Invert the matching equations

Solving \(r=(1-z)/(1+z)\) with \(z=Z_2/Z_1\) gives

\[z=(1-r)/(1+r)=1.50.\]

Equal tension implies

\[\mu_2/\mu_1=z^2=2.25.\]

Four percent of the incident power reflects, so 96% transmits. Reversing incidence makes \(r=+0.20\) and leaves the reflected power fraction at 0.04. A sign reversal records phase; a power measurement alone cannot distinguish positive and negative \(r\).

Identify the boundary by spectroscopy

A string of length 0.75 m has speed 90 m/s. A vibration record contains adjacent peaks at 90 and 150 Hz. Compare the fixed–fixed and fixed–free spectra and predict one lower-frequency peak. Find how the peak spacing changes if the tension is increased by 44% without changing density.

Combine the mode conditions with the speed law

For fixed–fixed ends the frequencies are multiples of 60 Hz, inconsistent with both readings. A fixed–free string has odd multiples of \(c/(4L)=30\) Hz, producing 30, 90 and 150 Hz. The predicted lower peak is 30 Hz if the drive and probe couple to it. Increasing tension by a factor of 1.44 multiplies speed and all frequencies by 1.20. The spacing between adjacent allowed modes rises from 60 to 72 Hz.

Predict which note disappears for an off-centre pluck

A string is released from a triangular displacement of height \(h\) at \(x=L/3\). Obtain its coefficients by integrating the two constant slopes after one integration by parts. Compare the second and first modal energies and identify the first missing harmonic.

Project the two sides onto the sine modes

For pluck position \(\alpha L\), the slopes are \(h/(\alpha L)\) and \(-h/[(1-\alpha)L]\). The coefficient is

\[b_n=2h\sin(n\pi\alpha)/[\pi^2n^2\alpha(1-\alpha)].\]

At \(\alpha=1/3\) the first two sines are equal, so

\[b_2/b_1=1/4,\]
\[E_2/E_1=4(1/4)^2=1/4.\]

The third harmonic is absent because its sine vanishes at the pluck point. Moving the pluck slightly off that point restores it.

Revisit chapter 1 · Hear the initial shape in the harmonics of a string.

Recover a source speed from two microphones

A trolley carries a tone generator down a straight track in still air at \(c=340\) m/s. A stationary microphone ahead of it measures 714 Hz, and a stationary microphone behind it measures 646 Hz during the same steady run. Recover the trolley speed and emitted frequency without assuming a small Doppler shift. Would averaging the two readings recover the source frequency exactly?

Worked solution

With speed \(v\) towards the front microphone, the measured frequencies are

\[f_+=f_sc/(c-v),\]
\[f_-=f_sc/(c+v).\]

Taking their difference divided by their sum eliminates the unknown tone frequency. Thus

\[v=c(f_+-f_-)/(f_++f_-)=17.0\,\mathrm{m/s}.\]

Adding the reciprocal frequencies gives

\[1/f_++1/f_-=2/f_s.\]

The emitted frequency is their harmonic mean, \(2f_+f_-/(f_++f_-)=678.3\) Hz. The arithmetic mean is 680 Hz, high by 1.7 Hz. Substitution with \(v/c=0.05\) gives 678.3/0.95 = 714 Hz and 678.3/1.05 = 646 Hz.

Revisit chapter 1 · Read a frequency from moving wavefronts.

Explore these relations

  • For the pulse direction, compare displacement and initial velocity and follow the separated travelling components. Open the live calculation.
  • For the boundary inference, compare the standing-wave mode sequence. Open the live calculation.
  • For the microphone observations, vary source speed and compare the approach and recession frequencies. Open the live calculation.

Chapter 3 · Wave packets and dispersion

Derive phase and group velocity from a pair of waves, then use a dispersion relation to predict how a finite packet moves and spreads.

From first principles

Derive the speed of an envelope from two nearby waves

Consider two small waves in a linear medium, with equal amplitudes and nearby wavenumbers \(k_1,k_2\). The medium supplies their frequencies through its dispersion relation \(\omega(k)\). Linearity allows the displacements to add. Let overbars denote arithmetic means and let \(\Delta k=k_2-k_1\), \(\Delta\omega=\omega_2-\omega_1\). The sum of the two cosines factors into a rapidly oscillating carrier and a slowly varying multiplier.

\[y=2A\cos[(\Delta k\,x-\Delta\omega\,t)/2]\cos(\bar kx-\bar\omega t).\]

To follow a particular carrier crest, keep its phase constant. To follow an envelope maximum, hold the phase of the first cosine fixed instead. Differentiating these two constant-phase conditions gives two speeds.

\[v_{\rm crest}=\bar\omega/\bar k,\]
\[v_{\rm envelope}=\Delta\omega/\Delta k .\]

As the two waves approach each other in wavenumber, the ratio of differences becomes a derivative.

\[v_p=\omega/k,\]
\[v_g=d\omega/dk .\]

A narrow packet moves approximately at this group velocity when nearby components sample nearly the same derivative. Curvature of \(\omega(k)\) makes different parts of its spectrum translate at slightly different speeds.

Background · Group velocity
\[v_g=d\omega/dk\]

This is the local slope of the dispersion curve. A sufficiently narrow-band packet with weak distortion can travel with its envelope at this velocity.

Derivation and applications

Predict the exact width of a Gaussian envelope

The range of group velocities estimates when a packet starts to spread. For a Gaussian input one can calculate its width at every time and fix the numerical factor in that estimate. Assume a linear, lossless medium, a narrow spectrum around \(k_0\) and negligible cubic and higher dispersion across that spectrum. At \(t=0\) take the complex envelope \(A(x,0)=A_0e^{-x^2/(2s^2)}\) with no initial spatial phase variation. Its wave-number spectrum is proportional to \(e^{-s^2q^2/2}\) for \(q=k-k_0\).

\[\omega(k_0+q)=\omega_0+v_gq+\tfrac12Dq^2,\]
\[D=\left.\frac{d^2\omega}{dk^2}\right|_{k_0}.\]

After removing the carrier, the linear phase translates the packet to \(\xi=x-v_gt\). The quadratic phase combines with its spectral amplitude to give a Gaussian with complex coefficient \(s^2+iDt\). Integrating each spatial Fourier component and fixing the prefactor by its initial value gives

\[A(\xi,t)=\frac{A_0s}{\sqrt{s^2+iDt}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{\xi^2}{2(s^2+iDt)}\right].\]

A square-law detector measures the modulus squared. The real part of the reciprocal complex width determines the new spatial scale \(w\), defined here as the distance from the centre to the \(1/e\) intensity point.

\[|A|^2=|A_0|^2\frac{s}{w}e^{-\xi^2/w^2},\]
\[w=s\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{Dt}{s^2}\right)^2}.\]

The product of peak intensity and width stays constant, as expected for lossless propagation. The sign of curvature affects the phase across the packet but drops out of this width measurement. A pre-existing spatial phase gradient varying across the envelope would change that conclusion about increasing width.

Worked example

Separate attenuation from geometric spreading

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A packet starts with \(s=0.500\) m in a medium with \(D=-0.100\) m²/s. At 5.00 s, \(Dt/s^2=-2.00\), so its width is 1.12 m. The peak intensity then equals \(1/\sqrt5=0.447\) of its initial value. A receiver following the envelope could see this reduction even with no dissipation. Integrating intensity across the packet tests that interpretation.

Second application

Infer local dispersion from a profile

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

For a second packet \(s=0.200\) m increases to 0.250 m after 0.600 s. Solving for curvature magnitude gives \(|D|=(s^2/t)\sqrt{(w/s)^2-1}=0.0500\) m²/s. An independently measured \(v_g=1.80\) m/s puts the centre 1.08 m from launch at this time. The centre displacement fixes the first derivative of the dispersion relation, while the width change fixes the magnitude of its second derivative.

Track a crest and an envelope separately

A burst contains many neighbouring frequencies. Individual crests can travel across its envelope as the burst moves past a detector. To see this with the simplest possible signal, superpose cosines with wave numbers \(k_0\pm\Delta k/2\) and angular frequencies \(\omega_0\pm\Delta\omega/2\). Applying the sum-to-product identity gives a rapid carrier \(\cos(k_0x-\omega_0t)\) multiplied by \(2\cos[(\Delta k\,x-\Delta\omega\,t)/2]\). A crest of the carrier moves at \(\omega_0/k_0\), while a maximum of the envelope moves at \(\Delta\omega/\Delta k\).

As the two wave numbers approach one another, the envelope velocity tends to \(v_g=d\omega/dk\). The phase velocity is \(v_p=\omega/k\). These coincide for a straight dispersion relation through the origin, as on the ideal nondispersive string. They can differ when restoring physics, geometry or material response introduces another length or time scale. Their distinction comes from which observable is followed.

Move a localised packet

Describe the packet as an integral of linear modes with complex amplitudes concentrated near \(k_0\). Write \(k=k_0+q\). The phase of each component is \(k_0x-\omega_0t+q(x-v_gt)-\omega''(k_0)q^2t/2\) to second order. The common first term is the carrier. Retaining only the linear term in \(q\) translates the original envelope without changing its shape, since all its Fourier components now depend on \(x-v_gt\).

The quadratic term changes the relative phases within the envelope. A packet spanning a wave-number width \(\Delta k\) samples a range of group velocities of order \(|\omega''|\Delta k\). After a time \(t\) the associated spatial separation is of order \(|\omega''|\Delta k\,t\). When this approaches the initial packet width, a single arrival time ceases to describe its full shape. This estimate concerns a narrow band and small enough bandwidth for the local expansion to describe the dispersion relation. Dissipation can also distort a pulse by attenuating frequencies unequally.

Apply the argument to surface gravity waves

For small-amplitude waves on deep water, neglect surface tension and viscosity. Incompressible, initially irrotational motion admits a velocity potential \(\Phi\) with \(\nabla^2\Phi=0\). A wave that decays rapidly with depth \(z<0\) has a potential proportional to \(e^{kz}e^{i(kx-\omega t)}\). At the surface the vertical velocity follows the elevation \(\eta\) through \(\eta_t=\Phi_z\), while the linearised pressure condition from Bernoulli gives

\[\Phi_t+g\eta=0.\]

Differentiating the latter and eliminating \(\eta\) gives \(\Phi_{tt}+g\Phi_z=0\) at \(z=0\). This yields

\[\omega^2=gk.\]

Consequently \(v_p=\sqrt{g/k}\) and \(v_g=v_p/2\). The disturbance penetrates through a depth of order \(1/k\). The assumption of deep water means bottom depth much greater than this scale. A finite-depth bottom alters the dispersion and therefore the time predicted for the arrival of a burst.

Predict an arrival and a change of width

Take a packet with carrier wavelength 4.0 m on deep water. Its \(k_0=1.571\) m⁻¹ gives \(\omega_0=3.93\) rad/s, \(v_p=2.50\) m/s and \(v_g=1.25\) m/s. An envelope maximum takes approximately 16.0 s to cover 20 m. A crest travels twice as fast, so it crosses the packet rather than remaining attached to the envelope maximum. Timing successive crests at one point would measure the carrier frequency, while timing envelope peaks at two points measures the group velocity.

For a spread \(\Delta k=0.20\) m⁻¹ the magnitude of the curvature is \(|\omega''|=v_g/(2k_0)=0.398\) m²/s. Over 16 s the different group velocities produce about 1.3 m of relative separation in this estimate. Comparing that with the initial envelope width is essential. Dispersion that is small in a broad pulse can be a major correction when the initial pulse is sharply localised.

An envelope measurement requires an operational timing definition. For a nearly undistorted narrowband packet, its maximum and its energy centroid travel almost together. Strong reshaping can move the maximum relative to the centroid, so arrival times defined by a threshold crossing can depend on amplitude as well as propagation. Saving the full waveform at two stations allows cross-correlation delays and centroid delays to be compared before assigning a group velocity.

Connect deep-water waves to shallow-water travel

When the water depth is not large compared with the wavelength, the bottom alters both crest speed and energy transport. For an inviscid, incompressible liquid of uniform depth \(h\), assume a small-amplitude plane surface wave and irrotational flow. A velocity potential \(\phi\) then satisfies Laplace's equation. Take the mean free surface at \(z=0\) and the flat impermeable bottom at \(z=-h\). Horizontal dependence \(e^{i(kx-\omega t)}\) makes the vertical solution proportional to \(\cosh k(z+h)\), since its derivative must vanish at the bottom.

The free surface elevation \(\eta\) follows fluid vertically, so \(\partial_t\eta=\partial_z\phi\) there. The linear dynamic condition from atmospheric pressure and gravity is

\[\partial_t\phi+g\eta=0.\]

Differentiate the second condition in time, eliminate \(\partial_t\eta\) with the first and insert the hyperbolic profile. The result and its group-velocity derivative are

\[\omega^2=gk\tanh(kh),\]
\[\frac{v_g}{v_p}=\frac12\left[1+\frac{2kh}{\sinh(2kh)}\right].\]

In deep water \(\tanh(kh)\to1\) and the group speed is half the phase speed. In shallow water \(\tanh(kh)\simeq kh\), giving \(\omega\simeq k\sqrt{gh}\). Both speeds then approach \(\sqrt{gh}\). The controlling small parameter is the depth measured against \(1/k\), so even the same tank can appear deep to short ripples and shallow to longer waves.

Use an arrival time to test the depth approximation

Take wavelength 4.00 m and tank depth 0.500 m. Then \(k=1.571\) m⁻¹, \(kh=0.785\) and \(\tanh(kh)=0.656\). The angular frequency is

\[\sqrt{9.81(1.571)(0.656)}=3.18\,\mathrm{rad/s}.\]

Phase speed is \(2.02\) m/s and the ratio formula gives \(v_g/v_p=[1+1.571/\sinh(1.571)]/2=0.841\), hence group speed \(1.70\) m/s. A narrow packet takes \(11.8\) s to travel 20.0 m. The shallow-water estimate would give \(2.21\) m/s and a 9.03 s arrival, a difference readily observable with two surface gauges.

The calculation holds the wavelength fixed when comparing model predictions. A wave entering a different depth from a steady source instead retains its frequency, and its wavenumber must be found again using the local dispersion relation. Keeping both wavelength and frequency fixed across a depth change would overconstrain the problem. If depth varies gradually, timing a packet across the tank involves integrating the inverse local group speed. Sharp bottom steps introduce reflection, so a single travel-time calculation cannot then predict the transmitted amplitude.

The shape of the subsurface motion gives another depth test independent of packet timing. For the potential derived above, horizontal velocity amplitude at the bottom is reduced relative to the free surface by \(1/\cosh(kh)\). At the stated depth this ratio is about \(0.755\): substantial horizontal motion remains even at the bottom. At much larger depths the ratio becomes exponentially small. Tracers placed at several depths therefore distinguish bottom influence directly, although viscous effects right at a real solid wall can alter the potential-flow velocity in a thin boundary layer.

The bottom must be flat on the scale of the wavelength for a single standing vertical profile to describe the whole observation region.

Can a pulse broaden without losing energy?

Propagate a transform-limited Gaussian envelope with second-order dispersion in a lossless medium. In retarded time, the intensity width increases by \(b=\sqrt{1+(z/L_D)^2}\). The peak must fall as \(1/b\) if the integrated pulse energy is conserved.

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Dispersive pulse compression and a detector's view of a tilted pulse · Optical fields, instruments and observations

An ultrashort pulse can broaden because its spectral components acquire different group delays or because different parts of its beam arrive at different times. Derive the two mechanisms separately, predict when added glass can shorten an already chirped pulse, and decide which measurements distinguish them.

Chapter 4 · Optical paths, lenses and images

Use stationary optical path length and the paraxial approximation to predict refraction, focal length and the image formed on a screen.

From first principles

Obtain refraction by varying the interface crossing point

In geometrical optics a ray between two specified points makes its travel time stationary against small changes of path. Take a flat interface between homogeneous transparent media with refractive indices \(n_1,n_2\), and light speed \(c/n_j\). Put the source a perpendicular distance \(h_1\) from the interface and the receiver a distance \(h_2\) beyond it. Their horizontal separation is \(D\). If the ray crosses at coordinate \(x\) measured from the source projection, its time is

\[t(x)=\frac{n_1}{c}\sqrt{x^2+h_1^2}+\frac{n_2}{c}\sqrt{(D-x)^2+h_2^2}.\]

Moving the crossing point increases one segment and decreases the other. At a stationary path their first-order contributions to travel time cancel.

\[c\,\frac{dt}{dx}=\frac{n_1x}{\sqrt{x^2+h_1^2}}-\frac{n_2(D-x)}{\sqrt{(D-x)^2+h_2^2}}=0 .\]

The geometric fractions are the sines of the ray angles to the surface normal. Hence \(n_1\sin\theta_1=n_2\sin\theta_2\). A curved optical surface applies this same local relation using its own normal at each crossing point, redirecting a family of rays towards an image.

Background · Optical path length
\[\mathcal L=\int n\,ds\]

Differences of this quantity determine phase delays in geometrical optics. For a pulse in a dispersive medium one must also account for group delay.

Derivation and applications

Calculate the apparent depth of a submerged point

A submerged mark appears closer to a flat water surface when viewed from air. How accurately does a single apparent depth describe rays reaching an eye with a wide pupil? Consider a point at depth \(h\) in a clear medium of refractive index \(n_1\). A plane boundary separates it from a second medium with index \(n_2\). Ignore diffraction and boundary curvature. A ray reaches the surface a horizontal distance \(x\) from the vertical through the mark. Its incidence angle \(\theta_1\) and exit angle \(\theta_2\) are measured from the normal.

\[x=h\tan\theta_1,\]
\[n_1\sin\theta_1=n_2\sin\theta_2 .\]

Extend the emerging straight ray backwards until it crosses the original vertical. The crossing lies at a depth \(h_{\rm app}\) which depends on the ray chosen. Eliminating the horizontal distance and then using Snell's law shows how the cosines correct the familiar small-angle result.

\[h_{\rm app}=\frac{x}{\tan\theta_2}=h\frac{\tan\theta_1}{\tan\theta_2}=h\frac{n_2}{n_1}\frac{\cos\theta_2}{\cos\theta_1}.\]

For a small aperture, both cosines approach unity. To see the first error term let \(m=n_1/n_2\) and expand around normal incidence, measuring angles in radians.

\[h_{\rm app}=\frac{h}{m}\left[1-\frac{m^2-1}{2}\theta_1^2+O(\theta_1^4)\right].\]

For observation from a lower-index medium, rays farther from the normal cross at smaller apparent depths. As incidence approaches its critical angle, the emerging ray grazes the surface and the inferred crossing approaches the interface. A wide bundle therefore has a range of apparent axial origins.

Worked example

Focus on a mark below water

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A mark lies 120 mm below water with \(n_1=4/3\) and \(n_2=1\). Near normal incidence the apparent depth is 90.0 mm. A ray entering the interface at 30.0° emerges with \(\sin\theta_2=2/3\), so \(\cos\theta_2=\sqrt5/3\). Its crossing is at \(90.0(0.7454/0.8660)=77.5\) mm. Restricting the rays admitted by the viewing optics therefore changes the focus of this mark.

Second application

Recover a refractive index with a narrow pupil

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

The lower surface of a 15.0 mm thick transparent slab appears 9.50 mm below its top surface in a near-normal air observation. The slab index is approximately \(15.0/9.50=1.58\). An internal incidence angle of 5.00° then gives a relative depth correction of \(-(1.58^2-1)(0.0873)^2/2=-0.0057\), or about 0.054 mm. This estimates the aperture-induced error against which the depth gauge resolution should be compared.

Try it · Apparent depth

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Travel time chooses a ray

A pulse travels from a point in air to a point in glass through a plane interface. A straight line minimises geometrical distance, but the pulse spends different times per metre in the two media. For a narrow-band wave in transparent, effectively nondispersive media, optical path length is \(\int n\,ds\). The ray connecting two fixed endpoints makes that integral stationary under small path variations. This description requires that the refractive index vary slowly on the scale of a wavelength within each material region and treats interfaces separately.

Let the crossing point slide horizontally by \(dx\). If the angles relative to the normal are \(\theta_1,\theta_2\), the length of the first segment changes by \(\sin\theta_1\,dx\) and that of the second by \(-\sin\theta_2\,dx\). Setting the first variation of optical path length to zero gives

\[n_1\sin\theta_1=n_2\sin\theta_2.\]

The same condition follows from tangential phase matching. Stationarity gives the ray direction; the reflected and transmitted powers require the wave boundary conditions as well.

Follow field amplitude through the critical angle

Snell’s law determines transmitted direction by matching the tangential phase across an interface. It does not determine how much energy is reflected. For light incident from isotropic nonmagnetic index \(n_1\) onto index \(n_2\), take the electric field perpendicular to the plane of incidence. Continuity of tangential electric and magnetic fields gives

\[1+r_s=t_s,\]
\[n_1\cos\theta_i(1-r_s)=n_2\cos\theta_t\,t_s.\]

All amplitudes refer to the same tangential electric direction. Eliminating \(t_s\) yields

\[r_s=\frac{n_1\cos\theta_i-n_2\cos\theta_t}{n_1\cos\theta_i+n_2\cos\theta_t},\]
\[T_s=\frac{n_2\cos\theta_t}{n_1\cos\theta_i}|t_s|^2\]

for propagating transmission in lossless media. The flux factor is essential: an electric amplitude larger than one need not imply transmitted power larger than the incoming power. At normal incidence from air into glass of index 1.50, \(r=-0.200\), \(t=0.800\), \(R=0.0400\) and \(T=0.960\).

Now send 500 nm light from the glass toward air at \(45^\circ\). Matching the tangential wavenumber gives

\[k_\parallel=k_0 n_1\sin\theta_i.\]

The outgoing normal wavenumber obeys \(k_z^2=k_0^2 n_2^2-k_\parallel^2\). Above the critical angle \(41.8^\circ\), choose \(k_z=i\kappa\) so fields decay into air. Here \(\kappa/k_0=\sqrt{1.50^2\sin^2 45^\circ-1}=0.354\), giving amplitude decay length \(1/\kappa=225\) nm and intensity decay length \(113\) nm. The reflection coefficient has unit magnitude and a phase shift. A single evanescent field in the lossless half-space carries no mean power normal to the interface even though its local electric amplitude is nonzero.

Practise on sheet 2 · Distinguish transmission amplitude, flux and penetration. Further reading · Delft University of Technology · Interactive Optics · Basic Electromagnetic and Wave Optics, “Properties of the Fresnel Coefficients” and “Total Internal Reflection and Evanescent Waves”.

Approximate a spherical refracting surface

Take light travelling from left to right. Put the surface vertex at zero and its centre of curvature at distance \(R\) to the right for a convex interface. A near-axis ray reaching height \(y\) from a real on-axis object a distance \(s\) to the left makes a small angle \(y/s\) to the axis. The normal through the centre makes angle approximately \(-y/R\). For a refracted ray directed to an image \(s'\) to the right, its angle is approximately \(-y/s'\). Small-angle Snell refraction applied to the signed differences between ray and normal gives

\[\frac{n_1}{s}+\frac{n_2}{s'}=\frac{n_2-n_1}{R}.\]

This sign convention takes positive object distance on the incoming side and positive image distance on the outgoing side. A negative solution for \(s'\) means that rays diverge after refraction but their backwards extensions intersect on the incoming side. The algebra therefore distinguishes a real screen image from a virtual image.

Combine two surfaces

For a thin lens in air, treat the image from the first surface as the object for the second. Since their vertices are taken at the same axial position, these intermediate signed distances have opposite signs. Adding the two surface equations eliminates the intermediate image term, leaving

\[\frac1s+\frac1{s'}=\frac1f,\]
\[\frac1f=(n-1)\left(\frac1{R_1}-\frac1{R_2}\right).\]

A symmetric biconvex glass lens has \(R_1>0\) and \(R_2<0\), hence positive focal length. Rays from a distant object then converge approximately one focal length beyond the lens. The formula assumes thin geometry and paraxial rays. Wide beams can generate spherical aberration, and index dispersion makes the focal length wavelength dependent. Even a perfect paraxial lens forms a diffraction spot when its pupil is finite.

Place a detector and interpret a virtual image

A lens has \(n=1.50\), \(R_1=+0.20\) m and \(R_2=-0.20\) m, giving \(f=0.20\) m. An object at \(s=0.60\) m gives \(1/s'=5.00-1.67=3.33\) m⁻¹, hence a real image at 0.30 m. The central undeviated ray gives transverse magnification \(-s'/s=-0.50\). A 12 mm tall object therefore produces a 6 mm inverted image. In practice a screen scan locates its sharpest plane, and the measured magnification independently checks the distance convention.

Move the object to 0.15 m. The same lens now gives \(s'=-0.60\) m and magnification \(+4.0\). The eye behind the lens sees rays diverging as if from an upright image on the object side, while a screen placed 0.60 m behind the lens finds no sharp real image. As the object passes through the focal plane, the sign changes through infinite image distance. This limiting case is readily checked using a collimated output beam.

A real image can be tested by moving a diffuse screen through its predicted location and recording the sharpness. For a virtual image, extend at least two emerging ray directions backwards to locate their apparent origin. An observer’s eye lens can still create a real retinal image from those diverging rays.

Treat two separated lenses as a single optical system

A compound objective can have an effective focal length different from its back focal distance. Work in air with paraxial rays and two centred thin lenses separated by distance \(d\). Describe a ray by height \(y\) and small angle \(u\) relative to the optical axis. Free propagation sends \((y,u)\) to \((y+du,u)\) and a lens of focal length \(f\) sends it to \((y,u-y/f)\). These operations are linear, so their matrices can be multiplied in the order the ray encounters them.

For the first lens \(f_1\), a spacing \(d\) and second lens \(f_2\), the ray immediately after the second lens is \(M(y,u)\) with

\[M=\begin{pmatrix}1-d/f_1&d\\-[1/f_1+1/f_2-d/(f_1f_2)]&1-d/f_2\end{pmatrix}.\]

For an initially parallel ray, its final angle is \(Cy\) where \(C\) is the lower-left entry. Define effective focal length \(f_{\rm eff}=-1/C\). Its ray height at the second lens is \(Ay\), where \(A=1-d/f_1\). After further distance \(s\) the height is \((A+sC)y\), so the back focal distance measured from the second lens is \(-A/C=A f_{\rm eff}\). Conflating the effective focal length with that physically measured clearance can place a detector incorrectly.

Locate the sensor in a compact two-lens assembly

Let \(f_1=100\) mm, \(f_2=-50.0\) mm and \(d=75.0\) mm. The total optical power is \(0.0100-0.0200+0.0150=0.00500\) mm⁻¹, giving \(f_{\rm eff}=200\) mm. The upper-left matrix entry is \(0.250\), so the back focal distance is only 50.0 mm. The sensor lies 125 mm behind the first lens even though the angular image scale corresponds to a 200 mm lens.

Trace a parallel ray entering 2.00 mm above the axis. The first lens sets its angle to \(-0.0200\) rad. Propagation over 75.0 mm reduces height to 0.500 mm. The diverging lens adds \(+0.0100\) rad, leaving \(-0.0100\) rad, so after another 50.0 mm the ray reaches the axis. If light instead arrives at a small field angle, focal-plane image displacement is \(f_{\rm eff}u\) in this ideal system: at \(u=1.00\) mrad that is 0.200 mm. In the zero-spacing limit powers add directly and the two focal distances coincide. When the total power instead tends to zero, a parallel incident beam exits parallel and no finite back focus exists.

For a further alignment check, reverse the propagation direction so that the diverging lens is encountered first. The equivalent focal length remains 200 mm because the expression for total power is symmetric in the two lenses. The back focal distance, however, now uses \(A=1-d/f_2=2.5\), giving 500 mm after the converging lens. A pair of lenses can therefore preserve its overall power when reversed while placing a collimated focus at a very different distance from the final glass surface. A measurement locating the two external focal points identifies this front–back asymmetry without having to inspect the lens curvatures.

Study the full derivation

From a slowly varying wave to a curved ray in a graded index · Optical fields, instruments and observations

A ray bends continuously in a glass whose index changes across its width. Derive where that ray equation comes from, predict the focusing length of a graded-index rod, and locate the points at which a smooth ray description ceases to determine an optical amplitude.

Problem sheet 2 · Packets and paraxial imaging

Use Lectures 3 and 4 to distinguish phase measurements, arrival-time measurements and image positions.

Extract two speeds from one dispersion curve

Near \(k_0=10\) m⁻¹ a guide has \(\omega(k)=120+8(k-k_0)+0.50(k-k_0)^2\) in consistent SI units. Find phase velocity, group velocity and the estimated spread of group velocity across a full wave-number band of 2 m⁻¹. Predict the carrier and envelope travel times across 24 m.

Use the value, slope and curvature

At the centre \(v_p=120/10=12\) m/s, while differentiating gives \(v_g=8\) m/s and \(\omega''=1.0\) m²/s. The group velocities at \(k_0\pm1\) are 7 and 9 m/s, spanning 2 m/s. The phase travel time is 2.0 s and the envelope-centre estimate is 3.0 s. Over that time velocities at the band edges build up a separation of about 6 m; an initially very narrow packet would be noticeably distorted.

Place an object and a screen

A 150 mm thin converging lens must project a 10 mm object as an inverted 30 mm image on a screen. Find both lens distances, then determine how far the lens itself can move to a second sharp position if the object and screen stay fixed.

Solve the conjugate distances

The magnification magnitude gives \(s'=3s\). Substitution in \(1/f=1/s+1/(3s)\) gives \(s=4f/3=200\) mm and \(s'=600\) mm. Object and screen are 800 mm apart. Interchanging the two distances is a second solution because the lens equation is symmetric, giving a lens motion of 400 mm. The new image magnification is \(-1/3\). A check of both sharpness and image size distinguishes which solution was reached.

Estimate a colour focus shift

A symmetric biconvex lens has \(R_1=+0.12\) m and \(R_2=-0.12\) m. Its red and blue refractive indices are 1.50 and 1.52. Locate both foci for a distant object and the axial shift, then explain why a ray calculation of two foci does not by itself give the size of a white-light spot.

Use dispersion in the lensmaker equation

For the symmetric lens \(f=R/[2(n-1)]\). Red focuses at 0.120 m and blue at 0.1154 m, about 4.62 mm closer to the lens. The refractive index is larger for blue, increasing optical power. To predict a spot on one fixed screen one also needs the illuminated pupil, spectral weighting, aberrations and diffraction. A focal shift is a longitudinal distance; the observed blur is transverse.

Distinguish transmission amplitude, flux and penetration

At normal incidence send light from index 1.60 into air. Find the electric amplitude and transmitted power fraction. At \(50.0^\circ\) incidence and vacuum wavelength 640 nm, find how far one must move into air for the squared field to fall to a quarter of its surface value.

Apply the two boundary regimes

At normal incidence \(t=2(1.60)/(1.60+1)=1.231\) and \(T=(1/1.60)t^2=0.947\). Reflection supplies the remaining 0.0533. At the oblique angle \(\kappa=(2\pi/640\,{\rm nm})\sqrt{1.60^2\sin^250^\circ-1}=0.00696\) nm⁻¹. The squared-field ratio is \(e^{-2\kappa z}\), hence \(z=\ln2/\kappa\simeq99.6\) nm. A Fresnel formula for real transmitted ray angles is inapplicable in this regime.

Chapter 5 · Polarisation and phase retardation

Predict the output of a polariser–retarder arrangement and distinguish changes of polarisation from loss of beam power.

From first principles

Project a polarised field onto an analyser

A linear polariser transmits the electric-field component along its transmission axis. Assume an ideal lossless transmitted axis, complete rejection of the perpendicular component and a monochromatic linearly polarised incident wave. For incident field amplitude \(E_0\) at angle \(\theta\) to the analyser axis \(\mathbf a\), the emerging field is the projection

\[\mathbf E_{\rm out}=(\mathbf E_{\rm in}\cdot\mathbf a)\mathbf a=E_0\cos\theta\,\mathbf a.\]

A detector averages energy flux over many optical cycles. For equal propagation impedances, intensity is proportional to squared field amplitude. This gives Malus's law and predicts extinction at crossed linear axes.

\[I_{\rm out}=I_{\rm in}\cos^2\theta .\]

A retarder takes a different action on its two orthogonal components. Both propagate through the crystal thickness \(d\) with different phase wavenumbers \(2\pi n_f/\lambda_0\) and \(2\pi n_s/\lambda_0\). Their relative phase on emerging is therefore

\[\delta=\frac{2\pi d}{\lambda_0}(n_s-n_f).\]

The analyser can turn this phase difference into a measurable intensity because it projects both coherent components onto one axis before the detector squares their sum.

Derivation and applications

Infer a plate's retardance from a rotation scan

A plate between crossed polarisers looks dark whenever an optical axis coincides with the first polariser. Away from those angles, both its orientation and its retardance control the transmitted light. Assume ideal polarisers, a uniform lossless linear retarder, normal incidence and monochromatic illumination. Let the first polariser transmit \(x\), the analyser transmit \(y\) and the fast axis of the plate make angle \(\theta\) with \(x\). Write the input field magnitude immediately after the first polariser as \(E_0\). Projection into the fast and slow axes gives

\[E_f=E_0\cos\theta,\]
\[E_s=-E_0\sin\theta .\]

The slow component emerges with relative phase \(\delta\) in the chosen complex convention. Its projection onto \(y\) has coefficient \(\cos\theta\), while the fast component projects with \(\sin\theta\). The two contributions need to be added as complex fields before computing detector intensity.

\[E_y=E_0\sin\theta\cos\theta(1-e^{i\delta}).\]

Use \(|1-e^{i\delta}|^2=4\sin^2(\delta/2)\) and normalise to the intensity \(I_0\) after the first polariser. Expressing the angular factor through a quadruple angle identifies an observable modulation at four times the rotation frequency.

\[\frac{I_\perp}{I_0}=\sin^2(2\theta)\sin^2(\delta/2)=\frac{1-\cos4\theta}{2}\sin^2(\delta/2).\]

The scan maximum provides \(\sin^2(\delta/2)\) independently of where the instrument labels zero angle, provided the full rotation is sampled. Lossless plates that differ in retardance by a full wave give identical readings, and this intensity measurement alone also loses the sign of the phase delay.

Worked example

Determine a birefringent film thickness

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

At 600 nm the measured maximum is 0.250 of \(I_0\). Independent fabrication information places \(\delta\) between zero and \(\pi\), so

\[\delta=2\arcsin(0.500)=\pi/3.\]

With index difference 0.0100 and \(\delta=2\pi\Delta n\,d/\lambda\), the thickness is \(d=10.0\) µm. At a 15.0° axis angle the predicted intensity is \(0.250\sin^2(30.0^\circ)=0.0625\) of \(I_0\).

Second application

Diagnose angular misalignment at a nominal extinction

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A calibrated half-wave plate gives 0.0100 of \(I_0\) at a setting labelled parallel to the input polariser. If angular misalignment dominates, its magnitude near the expected minimum solves \(\sin^2(2\theta)=0.0100\). Thus

\[|\theta|=\arcsin(0.100)/2=2.87^\circ.\]

A full rotation tests that explanation: the same angular offset should shift all four minima, and each corrected minimum should reach the independently measured background.

Resolve the electric field into two components

A narrow-band plane wave propagating along \(z\) in a transparent medium has a transverse electric field. Write its physical value as the real part of \((A_x\hat{\mathbf x}+A_y\hat{\mathbf y})e^{i(kz-\omega t)}\). A common phase attached to both complex amplitudes shifts the time origin of the field oscillation. Their relative phase and amplitude ratio determine how the tip of the field vector moves within a cycle.

For amplitudes in phase the field traces a line. Equal magnitudes with relative phase \(\pi/2\) produce a circle, while intermediate combinations usually give an ellipse. A two-component Jones vector describes one fully polarised coherent beam. For randomly changing amplitudes, predictions require averaging intensities or field correlations over the exposure. A single Jones vector cannot represent every partially polarised state.

Project onto a measuring axis

An ideal linear polariser with transmission direction \(\hat{\mathbf a}=(\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha)\) passes the component \(\hat{\mathbf a}\cdot\mathbf A\) and emits a field along \(\hat{\mathbf a}\). Its matrix is the outer product \(\hat{\mathbf a}\hat{\mathbf a}^{\mathsf T}\). A linearly polarised input at angle \(\beta\) therefore transmits a fraction \(\cos^2(\beta-\alpha)\) of the intensity. This is Malus’s law. It applies to the projection of the field before the detector takes a squared magnitude.

Two ideal polarisers at right angles transmit zero for an input aligned with the first. Inserting a third between them at 45° can increase transmitted power. After the middle polariser the direction of the transmitted wave has changed. The first projection passes half the original power and the second passes half of that half, so one quarter reaches the detector. The inserted component changes the state incident on the last analyser.

Background · Can a rotating linear analyser determine handedness?

Take two normalised Jones vectors \((1,i)/\sqrt2\) and \((1,-i)/\sqrt2\). Their electric vectors rotate in opposite senses with time. An ideal linear analyser responds to a real projection direction \((\cos\alpha,\sin\alpha)\). The two squared projections are identical for every angle.

\[\frac{I_\pm(\alpha)}{I_0}=\frac12|\cos\alpha\pm i\sin\alpha|^2=\frac12.\]

A quarter-wave plate that delays the second component by \(\pi/2\) first maps the two inputs to \((1,-1)/\sqrt2\) and \((1,1)/\sqrt2\) in the chosen phasor convention. A linear analyser at 45° can now distinguish them. An unpolarised beam also gives a flat angular scan through a linear analyser, so constant throughput on its own cannot establish circular polarisation.

Transform a phase delay into an intensity reading

A lossless birefringent plate has two orthogonal principal axes with refractive indices \(n_f\) and \(n_s\). Through thickness \(d\), their phase difference is \(\delta=2\pi(n_s-n_f)d/\lambda_0\), where \(\lambda_0\) is vacuum wavelength. In the plate-axis basis its Jones matrix, apart from a common phase, is \(\operatorname{diag}(1,e^{i\delta})\). The matrix preserves the sum of squared amplitudes. Only the relative phase changes.

Let the incident linear polarisation make an angle \(\beta\) to the fast axis. An analyser crossed with the original input has unit vector \((-\sin\beta,\cos\beta)\) in that basis. Projecting the output onto it gives amplitude proportional to \(\sin\beta\cos\beta(e^{i\delta}-1)\). Squaring yields

\[I_\perp/I_{\rm in}=\sin^2(2\beta)\sin^2(\delta/2).\]

Thus a dark crossed-analyser setting can indicate an axis-aligned input or an integer number of full-wave delays. One dark reading alone does not identify thickness or birefringence.

Distinguish a quarter-wave plate from a half-wave plate

Take \(\lambda_0=600\) nm and \(\Delta n=0.010\). The thinnest plate with \(\delta=\pi/2\) has \(d=\lambda_0/(4\Delta n)=15.0\) µm. At \(\beta=45°\), equal input amplitudes acquire a relative quarter-cycle delay, producing circular polarisation. A rotating ideal analyser then transmits half the intensity at every angle. With 4.0 mW arriving at the plate, the analyser passes 2.0 mW throughout the rotation.

Double the thickness. The delay becomes \(\pi\), so the two components have opposite signs and the output is linearly polarised along the original crossed analyser. It transmits the full 4.0 mW in that setting and zero in its orthogonal setting. Flat response under rotation does not by itself distinguish circularly polarised from unpolarised light. Adding a known quarter-wave plate before the analyser converts circular light to a linear state but leaves ideal unpolarised light unpolarised, giving an independent measurement.

Rotating the input polarization relative to a plate also checks the orientation of its principal axes. If the input is aligned with either axis, only one component is present and retardation has no effect on the polarization ellipse. The phase remains physically detectable only through interference with an independent reference. For comparable input components both axes contribute and an analyser becomes sensitive to their relative phase. This is why a small alignment error can suppress a retardance measurement even for a perfectly fabricated plate.

Recover polarization from six intensity readings

A rotating linear analyser measures orientation and some of the shape of a polarization ellipse, but opposite handedness gives the same result at every linear angle. Let the complex field components be \(E_x,E_y\) and average products over the detector integration time. Choose intensity units that absorb the common medium factor. Define \(S_0=\langle |E_x|^2+|E_y|^2\rangle\), \(S_1=\langle |E_x|^2-|E_y|^2\rangle\),

\[S_2=2\mathrm{Re}\langle E_x^*E_y\rangle,\]
\[S_3=2\mathrm{Im}\langle E_x^*E_y\rangle.\]

The sign convention for the last component specifies the handedness label used throughout the measurement.

An ideal linear analyser at angle \(\theta\) projects onto \((\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\). Squaring the projected amplitude and using double-angle identities gives

\[I(\theta)=\tfrac12[S_0+S_1\cos(2\theta)+S_2\sin(2\theta)] .\]

Hence the difference of horizontal and vertical readings gives \(S_1\), while the difference between \(+45^\circ\) and \(-45^\circ\) gives \(S_2\). Finally pass the input through a calibrated quarter-wave analyser combination selecting the two circular basis vectors \((1,\pm i)/\sqrt2\). The difference of their outputs in this order gives \(S_3\). Their sum, like the sum of either linear pair, gives \(S_0\). Multiple estimates of total intensity check for source drift and detector gain imbalance.

Distinguish a partially polarized state from a pure ellipse

Suppose the two principal linear readings are 7.00 and 3.00 mW, the diagonal readings are 6.50 and 3.50 mW, and the circular readings are 8.00 and 2.00 mW. Each pair sums to 10.0 mW. The inferred Stokes parameters are \((10.0,4.00,3.00,6.00)\) mW. The degree of polarization is

\[\sqrt{S_1^2+S_2^2+S_3^2}/S_0=\sqrt{61}/10=0.781.\]

A single fixed Jones vector has degree one, so a pure elliptical state cannot reproduce these repeated intensity readings.

The polarized fraction has major-axis azimuth \(\psi=\tfrac12\operatorname{atan2}(S_2,S_1)=18.4^\circ\). Its signed ellipticity angle satisfies \(\sin(2\chi)=S_3/\sqrt{S_1^2+S_2^2+S_3^2}\), giving \(\chi=25.1^\circ\) and minor-to-major axis magnitude \(\tan|\chi|=0.468\). At zero polarized fraction both angles cease to describe a measurable ellipse. A fitted degree above one signals an inconsistent set of readings, since the correlation matrix must have nonnegative eigenvalues. Sequential readings should therefore include a reference power monitor when source brightness can change during the six settings.

One can predict readings at analyser settings never used in reconstruction. For a linear analyser at \(30^\circ\) relative to horizontal, the stated parameters give

\[[10+4\cos60^\circ+3\sin60^\circ]/2=7.30\,\mathrm{mW}.\]

A separate fourth linear orientation therefore validates the fitted linear components but has no sensitivity to the circular component. To test handedness, interchange the two calibrated circular analyser states. The difference should reverse while their sum remains constant. If different linearly polarized calibration standards all generate a large circular signal, an analyser retardance or alignment error is a more immediate explanation than an exotic input state.

The total intensity is itself useful for another check. An ideal lossless wave plate changes the three polarized components while preserving their length and the total intensity. Measuring before and after a known plate therefore distinguishes a true change of polarization fraction from a simple rotation of its polarization coordinates.

Read retardance between crossed polarisers

A linear retarder sits between crossed ideal polarisers. At plate angle \(\theta\) its two principal waves acquire relative phase \(\delta\); projection onto the final analyser gives

\[I/I_0=\sin^2(2\theta)\sin^2(\delta/2).\]

Here \(I_0\) is the intensity after the first polariser.
