Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

A retarder rotates the state of a coherent beam, while unresolved mixtures can reduce its measured degree of polarisation. Chapter 6 · Coherence and interferometry

Use a variable path delay to connect fringe visibility to a source spectrum and estimate how far two copies of a wave can remain correlated.

From first principles

Derive fringe visibility from the field correlation

Split stationary light into two arms and recombine them in the same polarisation and spatial mode. Let \(E_1,E_2\) denote complex optical fields with detector averaging over many periods, and scale units so intensity equals \(\langle |E|^2\rangle\). A controllable relative phase \(\phi\) changes the interference term while the individual intensities \(I_1,I_2\) stay fixed. Expanding the squared sum gives

\[I(\phi)=I_1+I_2+2\operatorname{Re}[e^{i\phi}\langle E_1^*E_2\rangle].\]

The largest and smallest readings occur when the phased correlation points along the positive or negative real axis. Normalise the difference of those readings by their sum to define visibility \(\mathcal V\). Normalising the correlation by the geometric mean of the arm intensities defines the degree of coherence \(\gamma\).

\[\gamma=\frac{\langle E_1^*E_2\rangle}{\sqrt{I_1I_2}},\]
\[\mathcal V=\frac{I_{\max}-I_{\min}}{I_{\max}+I_{\min}}=\frac{2\sqrt{I_1I_2}}{I_1+I_2}|\gamma|.\]

Equalising the arms makes visibility directly measure \(|\gamma|\). A relative delay then reveals how long a field retains correlation with a delayed copy. Particular spectra lead to different delay dependences.

Background · Fringe visibility
\[\mathcal V=(I_{\max}-I_{\min})/(I_{\max}+I_{\min})\]

Use maxima and minima from the same scan. Unequal intensities can reduce contrast in a fully coherent field.

Derivation and applications

Find the interferogram of a rectangular spectral band

An optical filter with a flat passband and sharp edges predicts a distinctive pattern of coherence zeros. Consider stationary light whose spectral power per unit frequency is constant between \(\nu_0-\Delta\nu/2\) and \(\nu_0+\Delta\nu/2\). Combine two equal-intensity copies with adjustable delay \(\tau\) and ignore polarisation mismatch. Different frequencies add in mean intensity, while each frequency acquires its own relative phase. Normalising by total spectral power gives the complex degree of temporal coherence

\[\gamma(\tau)=\frac1{\Delta\nu}\int_{\nu_0-\Delta\nu/2}^{\nu_0+\Delta\nu/2}e^{2\pi i\nu\tau}\,d\nu .\]

Shift the integration variable to \(s=\nu-\nu_0\). Frequencies equally spaced above and below the centre contribute opposite imaginary parts to the shifted integral. Integrating the real cosine contribution yields an envelope multiplying the carrier phase.

\[\gamma(\tau)=e^{2\pi i\nu_0\tau}\frac{2}{\Delta\nu}\int_0^{\Delta\nu/2}\cos(2\pi s\tau)\,ds=e^{2\pi i\nu_0\tau}\frac{\sin(\pi\Delta\nu\tau)}{\pi\Delta\nu\tau}.\]

A fine local phase scan measures the fringe visibility \(|\gamma|\). The signed envelope changes sign after a zero, shifting the bright optical fringes by half a cycle relative to a continuation of the carrier. In a vacuum Michelson interferometer an arm-mirror displacement \(x\) changes the optical path by \(2x\), hence

\[\tau=\frac{2x}{c},\]
\[x_{\rm first\ zero}=\frac{c}{2\Delta\nu}.\]

Reducing the spectral width stretches the whole envelope and moves its zeros apart. A spectrum flat per unit wavelength transforms with a Jacobian when expressed per unit frequency, so a broad wavelength passband would require its measured frequency weights in the integral.

Worked example

Predict a fringe revival beyond the first zero

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

For \(\Delta\nu=100\) GHz and \(c=3.00\times10^8\) m/s, the first zero occurs at 1.50 mm mirror travel. At 2.25 mm, \(\Delta\nu\tau=1.50\). The signed envelope is \(-1/(1.50\pi)=-0.212\), predicting 21.2 percent visibility and a phase reversal. Loss of visible fringes at the first zero therefore need not persist as the mirror continues moving.

Second application

Infer a filter width from neighbouring nulls

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Another scan shows consecutive envelope zeros separated by 0.750 mm of mirror travel. The inferred bandwidth is

\[c/(2\Delta x)=200\,\mathrm{GHz}.\]

For a narrow fractional band centred on 750 nm the wavelength width is approximately \(\lambda_0^2\Delta\nu/c=0.375\) nm. This conversion uses the local slope of \(\nu=c/\lambda\), and the null-spacing inference uses the known double transit of the moving arm.

Try it · Rectangular spectrum

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Start from this chapter. Add two complex amplitudes of equal magnitude with a fixed relative phase. Predict where they cancel. The model drives coherent sound sources, so it isolates the path-difference contribution to fringe contrast.

OneSimulation laboratory · Find the dark fringes of a double slit

Compare a wave with its delayed copy

A Michelson interferometer divides one incoming beam into two arms, reflects the beams and recombines them. Moving one end mirror by \(d\) changes the round-trip optical path by \(2d\) in air. With equal arm intensities, identical polarisations and a monochromatic source, the recorded intensity at one output varies from dark to bright and back. One complete fringe passes a detector whenever the mirror moves by half a wavelength.

A real source contains several frequencies or a continuous band. Different frequencies then accumulate different phases for the same delay. A detector averages over many optical cycles and, under the usual stationary-source assumption, adds the intensity contributions of mutually uncorrelated spectral components. Bright fringes of one component can coincide with dark fringes of another. The visibility of the combined pattern can decay even when the beam paths remain perfectly aligned.

Write the measurement in terms of a correlation

Write \(E(t)\) for the complex field amplitude in one arm and \(E(t-\tau)\) for its delayed copy, with optical delay time \(\tau\). Expanding the detected intensity gives two arm intensities plus a cross term proportional to \(\operatorname{Re}\langle E^*(t)E(t-\tau)\rangle\). Normalise the correlation by its zero-delay value to define \(g^{(1)}(\tau)\). For equal arm intensities the contrast of fringes around a chosen delay is \(|g^{(1)}|\), provided the envelope varies slowly across one fringe.

The magnitude expresses temporal coherence over that delay. A fixed imbalance of arm intensities also reduces visibility. If one arm has four times the power of the other, perfect coherence gives contrast 0.8 even at zero delay. To interpret a decrease with delay as spectral information, measure both arm powers individually and check that they remain stable during the scan. Spatial misalignment and unequal polarisation can also reduce the cross term without broadening the source spectrum.

Compute the signature of two spectral lines

Let two narrow lines of equal power have frequencies \(\nu_0-\Delta\nu/2\) and \(\nu_0+\Delta\nu/2\). For each line, the interference term varies with \(\cos(2\pi\nu\tau)\). Their sum is proportional to \(\cos(2\pi\nu_0\tau)\cos(\pi\Delta\nu\,\tau)\). The fast oscillation gives ordinary optical fringes and the envelope gives visibility \(|\cos(\pi\Delta\nu\,\tau)|\). Its first zero occurs at \(\tau=1/(2\Delta\nu)\). Beyond that zero the fringes return with a phase reversal.

For a continuous spectral density \(S(\nu)\), the same sum becomes an integral. The normalised correlation is the Fourier transform of \(S\) divided by its integrated power, up to the chosen phase convention. A uniform spectrum across a band of width \(B\) produces a sinc envelope \(\sin(\pi B\tau)/(\pi B\tau)\). Its first zero occurs at \(1/B\). A coherence time therefore depends on the chosen definition of width and on the line shape. A single universal numerical factor would be misleading.

Read an interferogram in the laboratory

For a pair of lines separated by 100 GHz, the first visibility minimum is at 5.0 ps. The optical path difference in air is \(c\tau=1.50\) mm; the required mirror displacement from equal paths is 0.750 mm. The first visibility revival occurs after 1.50 mm of mirror travel. A mirror scan that ends before the first dip will not resolve the two lines reliably from their contrast modulation.

Suppose the average wavelength near the centre is 600 nm. During the first 0.750 mm mirror displacement roughly \(2(0.750\,\mathrm{mm})/(600\,\mathrm{nm})=2500\) fine fringes pass. Counting fine fringes calibrates the path scale, while locating the envelope minimum estimates spectral separation. The two observables use different parts of the same measurement record. This is an example of extracting information about a source through interference without physically separating its colours on a detector.

A drifting mirror can reduce the measured visibility by averaging different optical phases within one exposure. Shorter exposures or a separate stable reference fringe allow this instrumental loss to be distinguished from the coherence time of the source.

From a Lorentzian line to a coherence time

Two sharp lines produce a periodic visibility envelope. A single broadened line instead produces a decaying correlation. Let the power spectral density around carrier frequency \(\nu_0\) be a normalized Lorentzian with full width at half maximum \(\Delta\nu\). Write frequency offset \(u=\nu-\nu_0\) and \(\Gamma=\Delta\nu/2\). The normalized offset density is

\[S(u)=\Gamma/[\pi(u^2+\Gamma^2)].\]

Extending the narrow line to all real offsets is an excellent approximation when \(\Delta\nu\ll\nu_0\).

For a stationary field, the complex first-order coherence at delay \(\tau\) is the Fourier transform of the normalized spectral density. The carrier supplies a rapidly rotating phase factor. The remaining integral is \(\int S(u)e^{2\pi iu\tau}du\). For positive delay close the integration contour in the upper half-plane where the exponential decays. Only the pole at \(i\Gamma\) contributes; its residue gives \(e^{-2\pi\Gamma\tau}\). Negative delays give the complex conjugate. Hence

\[g^{(1)}(\tau)= e^{2\pi i\nu_0\tau}e^{-\pi\Delta\nu|\tau|},\]
\[\tau_{1/e}=\frac1{\pi\Delta\nu}.\]

An equal-arm-intensity interferometer measures \(|g^{(1)}|\) as fringe visibility. Several definitions of coherence time are in use, so an experimental statement should name the chosen width or threshold. A Gaussian spectral line gives a Gaussian delay envelope, allowing visibility scans to distinguish line shapes with the same half-maximum frequency width.

Infer linewidth without resolving the optical carrier

A Michelson interferometer in air gives calibrated zero-delay visibility 0.900. Moving one mirror by 30.0 mm reduces visibility to 0.450, after correcting for arm power. The optical path difference is twice the displacement, hence delay is

\[0.0600/c=200\,\mathrm{ps}.\]

Division by the zero-delay visibility gives a normalized envelope of \(0.500\). For a Lorentzian this implies \(\Delta\nu=\ln2/(\pi\tau)=1.10\) GHz. The \(1/e\) coherence time is \(289\) ps and the corresponding path difference is

\[c\tau_{1/e}=86.6\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Doubling the mirror displacement to 60.0 mm doubles the delay. The normalized visibility should then fall to one quarter, giving measured visibility \(0.225\). A Gaussian envelope adjusted to the original half-visibility point would instead fall to \(0.5^4=0.0625\) of its initial value. Measuring a second delay therefore tests the assumed line shape rather than merely refining a fitted linewidth. As linewidth tends to zero, the predicted envelope remains near one over every fixed path difference, although imperfect spatial overlap or phase jitter can still reduce the optical fringe itself.

A linewidth inferred from delay data is fundamentally a measurement of temporal correlation, so the relevant observation interval matters. Slow source-frequency drift during a long scan can yield a different envelope from that seen during short repeated measurements. One practical separation is to record forward and backward delay scans and monitor a fixed, nonzero delay between them. A drifting carrier changes fringe phase there even when the single-scan visibility remains high; a broad instantaneous spectrum reduces the visibility within each scan. The two effects require different physical interpretations despite potentially resembling one another in a long-time spectrum.

Study the full derivation

Photon counting, bunching and the time resolution of a coincidence test · Optical fields, instruments and observations

An intensity trace can be steady while individual detector clicks show correlations. Derive the observable of a two-detector Hanbury Brown–Twiss measurement, quantify how background and bin width change it, and decide whether a dip near zero delay belongs to the source or to a detector.

Problem sheet 3 · Polarisation and coherence

Use Lectures 5 and 6 to diagnose a dim interference signal with separate polarisation, intensity and bandwidth measurements.

Measure a birefringent sample

A linearly polarised 2.0 mW beam of wavelength 500 nm enters a lossless plate at 45° to its fast axis. The crossed analyser transmits 0.50 mW. Find the smallest positive retardance and the corresponding thickness if \(\Delta n=0.020\). Explain why one reading cannot determine an arbitrary thickness.

Invert the analyser transmission

The measured fraction is 0.25, giving \(\sin^2(\delta/2)=1/4\). The first positive solution is

\[\delta=\pi/3.\]

Thus

\[d=\delta\lambda_0/(2\pi\Delta n)=4.17\,\mu\mathrm m.\]

Larger retardances with the same sine squared give the same reading. A thickness bound or a second wavelength can distinguish some of these branches. If the incident beam fails to make 45° to the plate, the inferred retardance is also biased.

Separate loss of coherence from unequal powers

Two interferometer arms separately deliver 0.90 and 0.10 mW at a detector. Together their fringes have visibility 0.30. Find the maximum visibility for perfect mutual coherence, estimate \(|g^{(1)}|\) and predict the new visibility after attenuating the brighter beam to match the weaker one.

Normalise by the unequal-arm ceiling

The perfect-coherence ceiling is

\[2\sqrt{I_1I_2}/(I_1+I_2)=0.60.\]

Thus the measured modulus of the normalised mutual coherence is 0.50, assuming matching polarisation and spatial overlap. With balanced arms the ceiling rises to one, so visibility becomes 0.50 if attenuation is uniform and the coherence is preserved. The mean power falls to 0.20 mW. Better contrast and poorer photon statistics can occur together.

Identify a line separation from a mirror scan

A source with two equal, narrow spectral lines has its first contrast minimum 0.30 mm from equal arm lengths on a Michelson mirror stage. Find the separation in hertz, the next stage displacement giving full contrast, and the effect of orthogonal polarisations in the two arms.

Convert motion into delay first

The optical path difference at the first dip is 0.60 mm, or a delay of 2.00 ps. For the doublet \(\Delta\nu=1/(2\tau)=250\) GHz. The first revival occurs at twice the delay and hence 0.60 mm of stage motion from equal paths. With mutually orthogonal arm polarisations their vector fields have zero cross term at a polarisation-insensitive detector. A common output analyser with nonzero projections onto both can recover fringes, provided the original temporal coherence remains.

Explore these relations

  • For the birefringent plate, vary retardance and orientation and inspect analyser transmission. Open the live calculation.
  • For coherence, vary spectral width and compare the coherence envelope with delay. This continuous rectangular spectrum is a contrast case for the two-line measurement. Open the live calculation.

Chapter 7 · Diffraction as a Fourier transform

Derive the far-field pattern from a finite aperture and identify which features control its scale and its sidelobes.

From first principles

Sum the field across a uniformly illuminated slit

A distant screen receives contributions from across the whole aperture. Consider monochromatic scalar illumination normal to a long slit of width \(a\). In the far field, paths from two points separated by distance \(dx\) differ by approximately \(dx\sin\theta\) in direction \(\theta\). Distance-dependent amplitudes are nearly constant across the slit, leaving a linear phase variation. Put \(k=2\pi/\lambda\) and choose the origin at the slit centre.

\[U(\theta)=C\int_{-a/2}^{a/2}e^{-ikx\sin\theta}\,dx .\]

Integrating the exponentials from the two edges and normalising to forward amplitude yields a sinc function. The central limit is one by continuity even though the displayed ratio takes the form zero divided by zero there.

\[\frac{U(\theta)}{U(0)}=\frac{\sin z}{z},\]
\[z=\frac{\pi a\sin\theta}{\lambda}.\]

Intensity is the squared magnitude of the coherent field sum. The first noncentral zero therefore occurs at \(a|\sin\theta|=\lambda\). Widening the slit narrows its central angular peak. For a general transmission profile the same weighted integral is its Fourier transform sampled at transverse wavenumber \(k\sin\theta\).

Explore the related parameter study beside the computed figure.

Derivation and applications

Locate missing interference orders in a double slit

A pair of finite slits can erase an interference maximum even when the detector is perfectly aligned. Consider two identical uniformly illuminated slits of width \(a\), centre separation \(d>a\), and long dimension perpendicular to the observation plane. Assume coherent monochromatic light of wavelength \(\lambda\), scalar diffraction and far-field detection. At observation angle \(\theta\) define transverse spatial frequency \(q=2\pi\sin\theta/\lambda\). Each point at coordinate \(x\) contributes phase \(e^{-iqx}\). A centred slit therefore contributes

\[U_1(q)=C\int_{-a/2}^{a/2}e^{-iqx}\,dx=Ca\,\frac{\sin(qa/2)}{qa/2}.\]

Translating a slit multiplies its transform by a phase factor. The two translated copies have centres at \(\pm d/2\). Add their fields, square the magnitude, and divide by the central intensity. The aperture width and slit separation now enter different factors.

\[\frac{I(\theta)}{I(0)}=\left[\frac{\sin(qa/2)}{qa/2}\right]^2\cos^2(qd/2).\]

The centres of the ideal two-point interference orders satisfy

\[d\sin\theta=m\lambda.\]

Evaluate the single-slit envelope at those positions. The order disappears exactly when its location coincides with a noncentral zero of the envelope.

\[a\sin\theta=p\lambda,\]
\[m=p\frac da,\]
\[p=\pm1,\pm2,\ldots .\]

If \(d/a\) is a rational number, only those choices of \(p\) producing integer \(m\) erase interference orders. Finite width can also shift the actual maxima of the product slightly away from the nominal interference centres. The coincidence of a zero with an order centre remains exact in this ideal model.

Worked example

Explain an absent green fringe

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Two slits 50.0 µm wide have centre spacing 200 µm. At \(\lambda=500\) nm the fourth order lies at \(\sin\theta=4\lambda/d=0.0100\). This is the first zero of the single-slit amplitude. A lens of focal length 200 mm would place that missing fringe about 2.00 mm from the centre. The first order samples an envelope factor \([\sin(\pi/4)/(\pi/4)]^2=0.811\).

Second application

Reconstruct slit dimensions from a pattern

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A different apparatus uses 600 nm light and a 300 mm focal length. Near the axis, the measured nominal interference spacing is 0.900 mm, giving \(d=f\lambda/\Delta x=200\) µm. The first envelope zero lies 2.25 mm from the centre, giving \(a=f\lambda/x_1=80.0\) µm. Their ratio is 2.50. The first envelope zero lies halfway between two nominal orders, while the second erases order five at 4.50 mm.

Start from this chapter. Use slit separation and wavelength to predict the first minimum before reading the finite-distance pressure field. Repeat at the same frequency with a different receiver radius to test the far-field assumption.

OneSimulation laboratory · Find the dark fringes of a double slit

Where a geometrical shadow breaks down

Illuminate a thin screen at normal incidence with a monochromatic plane wave of vacuum wavelength \(\lambda\) in air. Light transmitted through different parts of an opening reaches a distant observation point along slightly different paths. Their phases matter even where geometrical rays would draw a hard shadow. Use scalar diffraction, suited to features much larger than the wavelength and modest angles. Represent the complex field just beyond the screen by an aperture function \(A(x,y)\), incorporating amplitude and phase.

For a far-away observation direction \(\mathbf n\), the propagation distance from aperture point \(\mathbf r'\) is \(r-\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf r'\) to first order. Replacing the inverse distance in the wave amplitude with \(1/r\) while retaining this linear phase yields

\[U(\theta_x,\theta_y)\propto\iint A(x,y)e^{-ik(x\sin\theta_x+y\sin\theta_y)}\,dx\,dy .\]

The angular field samples a spatial Fourier transform. The neglected quadratic path term produces a phase of order \(\pi a^2/(\lambda r)\) for aperture half-width \(a\). The free-space far-field approximation requires this quantity to be small. A lens supplies a quadratic phase that creates the Fourier pattern in its back focal plane even at a practical laboratory distance.

Add the contributions of a uniform slit

For a very tall slit of width \(w\), study the horizontal cross-section. Uniform amplitude across the width gives an integral of the exponential between \(-w/2\) and \(w/2\). The result is \(w\sin u/u\) with \(u=\pi w\sin\theta/\lambda\). Taking squared magnitude and normalizing at the origin yields

\[I(\theta)/I(0)=\left(\frac{\sin u}{u}\right)^2 .\]

The first zeros satisfy

\[\sin\theta=\pm\lambda/w.\]

A broader opening makes the central spot narrower because a smaller angular change suffices to accumulate a full cycle across it. The oscillating tails arise from the abrupt edges. A gradual transmission taper reduces those sidelobes at the cost of a wider central maximum. Peak brightness additionally depends on the transmitted amplitude and collection geometry.

Measure width in a focal plane

Illuminate a \(120\) μm slit with 600 nm light and use a focal-length 200 mm lens. In the paraxial focal plane \(X\simeq f\sin\theta\). The first minima occur at \(X=\pm1.00\) mm, giving a central width of 2.00 mm. At half that distance \(u=\pi/2\), so the intensity is \(4/\pi^2=0.405\) of its central value. These two measurements separately test angular scale and profile shape.

If the slit is moved sideways, the Fourier shift theorem supplies only a phase factor in the far field, and the intensity pattern remains centred in this ideal arrangement. If it is illuminated at a small angle instead, the incident field carries a linear phase across the slit and the whole pattern moves. This distinction becomes useful when diagnosing whether a displaced focal-plane spot comes from a decentered opening or a tilted incoming beam.

A narrow receiving slit or detector pixel also integrates across part of the pattern. If it straddles a predicted zero, it collects light from either side and reports a positive count even for perfect diffraction. Reducing detector width tests this averaging interpretation.

Observe an aperture before its far field forms

A finite opening can produce bright and dark features even on its axis as the detector moves away. Consider a uniformly illuminated circular aperture of radius \(a\) in an opaque thin screen. Use a monochromatic scalar plane wave and a paraxial on-axis detector at distance \(z\) large compared with \(a\), yet not necessarily large compared with \(a^2/\lambda\). At aperture radius \(r\) the extra path is \(\sqrt{z^2+r^2}-z\simeq r^2/(2z)\). The Fraunhofer approximation drops this quadratic phase; Fresnel propagation keeps it.

Relative to the unobstructed advancing plane wave, the on-axis field is proportional to \(\int_0^a (2\pi r/i\lambda z)e^{i\pi r^2/(\lambda z)}\,dr\). The factor \(r\,dr\) makes the substitution \(u=\pi r^2/(\lambda z)\) immediate. Using the free propagation normalization gives

\[\frac{U(0,z)}{U_{\rm free}(0,z)}=1-e^{i\pi N_F},\]
\[\frac{I(0,z)}{I_{\rm free}}=4\sin^2\left(\frac{\pi N_F}{2}\right),\]
\[N_F=\frac{a^2}{\lambda z}.\]

Successive annuli adding half a wavelength of path alternately reinforce and cancel on axis. An even integer Fresnel number gives perfect cancellation in the ideal model. An odd integer makes the on-axis intensity four times the unobstructed value. There is no excess total power: interference redistributes light in the transverse plane. The result concerns local on-axis intensity at a specified distance rather than integrated transmission.

Predict successive axial extrema

Choose \(a=0.500\) mm and \(\lambda=500\) nm. Then

\[a^2/\lambda=0.500\,\mathrm{m}.\]

A detector at \(z=0.250\) m sees \(N_F=2.00\), predicting an axial null. Moving it to \(z=0.500\) m gives \(N_F=1.00\) and a fourfold maximum. The aperture-to-distance ratio is at most 0.002 in these two measurements, supporting the small-angle path expansion even though the far-field criterion is not satisfied.

At \(z=5.00\) m the Fresnel number is 0.100 and the exact Fresnel expression gives

\[I/I_{\rm free}=0.0979.\]

Its far-field expansion is \((\pi N_F)^2=0.0987\), now within about one percent. This limit recovers the inverse-square on-axis intensity at fixed opening and incident irradiance. A real photodiode averages several transverse points, which can fill an axial null, and a broadband source averages slightly different Fresnel numbers. Repeating the axial scan with a smaller active area or a narrower spectral band tests which averaging effect dominates the observed minimum.

A complementary screen can help verify that the observed oscillations arise from aperture interference. Within the same scalar boundary approximation, replacing the circular opening by an opaque disk makes the two complex transmitted fields add to the unobstructed field. On axis the disk's normalized field is therefore \(e^{i\pi N_F}\) in this model, with unit intensity and a distance-dependent phase. A bright point behind an opaque disk may seem surprising from geometric rays, but neighbouring angles still redistribute the blocked power. Finite coherence, source size and a detector averaging over the narrow central feature can strongly reduce its measured contrast.

From Fresnel zones to a diffractive lens

The axial null from a clear circular opening results from destructive addition of neighbouring half-period zones. A zone plate reverses that outcome by deliberately blocking every other annulus. At desired focal distance \(f\), a ray from the edge of zone number \(m\) has path excess \(m\lambda/2\) relative to the centre. Solving \(\sqrt{f^2+r_m^2}-f=m\lambda/2\) gives

\[r_m^2=m\lambda f+\frac{m^2\lambda^2}{4}\simeq m\lambda f .\]

The approximation requires the outer zone radius to remain small compared with focal distance. Consecutive radii therefore grow approximately as the square root of zone number, and outermost zones are narrowest. Leaving odd zones transparent and even zones opaque removes contributions with the opposite phase at the design focus. Power is also distributed into other diffraction orders and an undiffracted background, so this simple plate does not concentrate all incident light into one focus.

Set \(\lambda=500\) nm and design for \(f=0.200\) m with twenty zones. The radius of the first zone is \(\sqrt{\lambda f}=0.316\) mm, and the twentieth radius is \(1.414\) mm. The width of that outer zone is \(\sqrt{\lambda f}(\sqrt{20}-\sqrt{19})=35.8\) μm, setting a fabrication scale substantially finer than the overall aperture. If the plate is used at 600 nm without changing its radii, its first-order focal length becomes

\[r_1^2/\lambda=0.167\,\mathrm{m}.\]

This shortening with increasing wavelength follows from the required relative phase and is an observable distinction from simply changing the clear aperture of a refracting lens. Scanning on-axis intensity at two colours tests both the zone layout and its predicted chromatic shift.

Which interference orders disappear under diffraction?

Two uniformly illuminated slits have width \(a\) and centre spacing \(3a\). The Fraunhofer field is the product of the single-aperture transform and a two-source phase sum. Normalise intensity at zero angle and use \(u=a\sin\theta/\lambda\).

Chapter 8 · Images through a finite pupil

Separate single-point diffraction, two-point resolution and spatial-frequency contrast for a circular imaging aperture.

From first principles

Relate the pupil field to a point image

Treat an on-axis distant point as a uniform plane wave over a lens pupil. Assume scalar, monochromatic, paraxial propagation and an ideal thin lens of focal length \(f\). The lens cancels the quadratic propagation phase at focus. Moving a small distance \(x\) across the focal plane introduces a nearly linear phase across pupil position \(\xi\). For a one-dimensional pupil function \(P(\xi)\) the field sum is

\[U(x)\propto\int P(\xi)\exp\!\left[-i\frac{2\pi x\xi}{\lambda f}\right]d\xi .\]

The recorded point-spread function \(h\) is proportional to \(|U|^2\) and can be normalised to unit integral. For a uniform slit pupil of width \(D\), the first zero follows directly from the phase difference across its edges.

\[h(x)\propto\left[\frac{\sin(\pi D x/\lambda f)}{\pi D x/\lambda f}\right]^2,\]
\[|x_{\rm first}|=\lambda f/D .\]

An extended incoherent object consists of mutually uncorrelated point emitters, so their shifted intensity patterns add. Where the point response is independent of field position this gives \(I_{\rm image}=I_{\rm object}*h\) in common image coordinates. A wider pupil narrows the blur and passes finer object structure.

Background · Numerical aperture
\[\mathrm{NA}=n\sin\theta\]

The largest accepted angle controls which transverse spatial frequencies reach the pupil. Resolution also depends on the optical imaging and coherence assumptions.

Derivation and applications

Relate a defocus error to the on-axis point intensity

A displaced detector spreads the intensity of an unresolved point even if the lens is otherwise perfect. Consider a uniformly illuminated circular pupil of radius \(a\) and a monochromatic point on the optical axis. Use scalar paraxial diffraction in air and a clear pupil. Let \(\phi\) be the phase error at the rim relative to the centre. Defocus contributes a quadratic phase \(\phi r^2/a^2\). At the on-axis image point the in-focus contributions are in phase, so the defocused complex amplitude relative to its in-focus value is the pupil-area average

\[H(0)=\frac{1}{\pi a^2}\int_0^a e^{i\phi r^2/a^2}2\pi r\,dr=\int_0^1 e^{i\phi u}\,du,\]
\[u=r^2/a^2.\]

Equal increments of \(u\) label equal pupil areas. Integrating these phasors gives a phase factor times a real amplitude. Its squared magnitude is the axial intensity at the intended point relative to the ideal peak.

\[H(0)=e^{i\phi/2}\frac{\sin(\phi/2)}{\phi/2},\]
\[S=|H(0)|^2=\left[\frac{\sin(\phi/2)}{\phi/2}\right]^2.\]

For a detector displaced axially by magnitude \(|\Delta z|\) from a focus of focal length \(f\), the change in quadratic propagation phase has magnitude \(k|\Delta z|r^2/(2f^2)\) to first order in \(|\Delta z|/f\). With paraxial numerical aperture \(\mathrm{NA}=a/f\) this gives

\[|\phi|\simeq\frac{\pi\,\mathrm{NA}^2|\Delta z|}{\lambda},\qquad S\simeq1-\frac{\phi^2}{12}\quad (|\phi|\ll1).\]

Total transmitted power stays constant. A loss of axial intensity represents redistribution across the image plane and the same axial value can occur for displacements on either side of best focus.

Worked example

Set a permissible detector displacement

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A microscope working in air has \(\mathrm{NA}=0.100\) and \(\lambda=500\) nm. An axial displacement of 25.0 µm corresponds to \(|\phi|=\pi/2\). The exact pupil average gives

\[S=[\sin(\pi/4)/(\pi/4)]^2=0.811.\]

A detector that measures only the central intensity would therefore show a 19 percent loss even with ideal transmission.

Second application

Infer numerical aperture from a through-focus scan

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A separate lens at 640 nm shows its first axial zero 80.0 µm from best focus. The first nonzero root occurs at \(|\phi|=2\pi\), so

\[\mathrm{NA}=\sqrt{2\lambda/|\Delta z|}=0.126.\]

If the effective focal length is 50.0 mm, the inferred pupil radius is 6.32 mm. The scan thus measures the effective illuminated aperture; partial or graded illumination would change the pupil weighting in the first integral.

Try it · Defocus strehl

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

A point becomes an extended image

A thin lens focuses a distant point into its back focal plane. An ideal geometrical ray diagram predicts a mathematical point. The finite entrance pupil limits the transverse field that can contribute. Define the point-spread function as the image intensity from a single on-axis point. Restrict attention to an aberration-free, circular, uniformly illuminated pupil, a narrow spectral band and an image region sufficiently small that shifting the source translates the same point-spread function.

The preceding aperture transform now runs over a disk of radius \(a\). For an observation angle \(\theta\) the transverse wavenumber is \(q=k\sin\theta\). Performing the angular integral of the plane-wave phase gives \(2\pi J_0(qr)\). The radial integral then uses \(d[zJ_1(z)]/dz=zJ_0(z)\), so the field normalized at the centre is \(2J_1(qa)/(qa)\). Squaring gives the Airy intensity pattern. Its first zero is at \(qa=3.8317\), hence

\[\sin\theta_1=1.22\lambda/D,\]
\[D=2a .\]

Combine two point sources

For statistically independent emitters the fluctuating relative phase averages away. Their point-spread intensities add. The Rayleigh convention places the centre of one Airy spot on the first zero of its neighbour. It provides a useful separation scale for two comparable emitters, though achievable accuracy also depends on photon counts, background and whether the measurement estimates a known model. Two coherent emitters instead add complex fields before squaring. Their relative phase can brighten or extinguish the midpoint.

For an extended incoherent object each point contributes a shifted intensity pattern. Superposition gives convolution of object intensity with the intensity point-spread function. Taking a Fourier transform converts convolution to multiplication. The resulting optical transfer function is the autocorrelation of the pupil in spatial-frequency coordinates. Its support reaches twice the radius of the coherent field passband because displaced pupil copies overlap until their centres are separated by one diameter. In object space the incoherent cutoff in cycles per metre is \(2\,\mathrm{NA}/\lambda_0\), where \(\mathrm{NA}=n\sin\alpha\) and \(\lambda_0\) is vacuum wavelength.

Choose the object-side sampling

Use an air objective of numerical aperture 0.40 at 520 nm. The Rayleigh scale is \(0.61\lambda_0/\mathrm{NA}=0.793\) μm. The incoherent cutoff is \(1.54\) cycles/μm, corresponding to a limiting sinusoidal period of \(0.650\) μm with contrast vanishing at the cutoff. These describe different target geometries. A grating near the cutoff can have weak contrast even though its nominal period exceeds the reciprocal cutoff.

To sample the full image band without aliasing, the object-side pixel pitch must be at most \(1/(2f_{\rm cut})=0.325\) μm. A detector with 6.5 μm pixels therefore needs at least \(20\times\) lateral magnification. A still higher magnification gives more samples of the same optical image. Recovering finer physical structure would require a larger numerical aperture, a shorter wavelength, or additional measurement information.

A separate point-source image is a practical calibration target. Its recorded radial profile can be convolved with a proposed object model to predict the measured image, including overlapping spots, before attempting an inversion of noisy data.

Derive the contrast transfer from two overlapping pupils

A cutoff frequency specifies where contrast disappears, but it does not tell how much contrast survives below cutoff. For incoherent imaging with a clear circular pupil, the normalized optical transfer function is the overlap area of two pupil disks displaced in reciprocal space, divided by one disk area. Consider one spatial-frequency magnitude \(\nu\) and normalize it by the incoherent cutoff, \(q=\nu/\nu_c\). At \(q=0\) the disks coincide; at \(q=1\) their centres are one full diameter apart. For pupil radius \(a\) the centre separation is therefore \(2aq\).

The overlap lens consists of two circular segments. In either disk the half-angle between radii to the intersection points is \(\arccos q\). The sector area minus the area of its isosceles triangle is \(a^2[\arccos q-q\sqrt{1-q^2}]\). Double this result and divide by \(\pi a^2\). For ideal focus the transfer is real and nonnegative,

\[H(q)=\frac2\pi[\arccos q-q\sqrt{1-q^2}],\qquad 0\le q\le1 .\]

At zero spatial frequency \(H=1\), expressing conservation of a uniform background after suitable calibration. At the cutoff only tangential contact remains and \(H=0\). If the object intensity is \(I_0[1+m\cos(2\pi\nu x)]\), linear shift-invariant incoherent imaging leaves the average unchanged while multiplying its modulation depth by \(H(q)\). This establishes a direct experimental meaning for a frequency-dependent contrast loss.

Separate objective and detector contrast losses

An air microscope at 500 nm has NA 0.50, giving \(\nu_c=2\mathrm{NA}/\lambda=2.00\) cycles/μm in object space. A target with period 1.00 μm has \(q=0.500\). The circular overlap formula gives

\[H=(2/\pi)(\pi/3-0.5\sqrt{0.75})=0.391.\]

An object modulation \(m=0.600\) therefore appears optically with modulation \(0.235\), even under ideal focus.

Suppose each detector pixel integrates over object-side width \(p=0.250\) μm, with contiguous active pixels. Averaging the sinusoid over a width \(p\) gives another multiplier \(\sin(\pi\nu p)/(\pi\nu p)=0.900\) at this target frequency. The recorded modulation is consequently about \(0.211\). This decrease occurs before sampling values at the pixel centres and differs from aliasing. The detector Nyquist frequency is \(1/(2p)=2.00\) cycles/μm, just enough to cover the optical band. Reducing pixel pitch can recover its own averaging loss but leaves the objective's \(0.391\) transfer unchanged. Calibrating with several grating periods distinguishes an optical contrast curve from a simple constant gain error.

This test also explains why visibility estimates near the optical cutoff are noisy. Recovering an object modulation from an image would require dividing by the optical and pixel transfer factors. At the example period their product is about 0.352, already amplifying a small modulation measurement error by nearly three. Close to cutoff the corresponding inverse becomes much larger. More exposure time reduces photon-counting uncertainty but cannot recover a component at which the transfer is exactly zero. A known calibration target is preferable when testing the transfer curve, since otherwise a weak observed fringe can arise from either the specimen or the imaging system.

Study the full derivation

What an objective must collect to resolve a specimen · Optical fields, instruments and observations

A microscope can transmit plenty of light and still erase fine detail. Treat the specimen as a weak grating, follow its spatial harmonics through the objective, and determine why changing illumination coherence changes the transfer of contrast.

Problem sheet 4 · Fourier pupils and image sampling

Reconstruct a slit, predict a shifted diffraction image and specify a sampled image for an incoherent microscope.

Recover an aperture width

A 250 mm lens gives first minima separated by 2.40 mm under 480 nm light. Find the single-slit width. Predict the intensity fraction 0.60 mm from centre and the new separation if a 600 nm source is substituted.

Show the transform and its scale

The first minimum lies at \(X_1=f\lambda/w=1.20\) mm, so \(w=100\) μm. At \(X_1/2\), the sinc argument is \(\pi/2\) and intensity is \(4/\pi^2=0.405\) of peak. Keeping the same lens and aperture, angular width scales with wavelength. At 600 nm the first-zero separation becomes

\[2.40(600/480)=3.00\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Shift or tilt?

The same slit moves sideways by 50 μm. Derive the resulting far-field phase factor and identify the intensity change. Then restore the slit position and tilt the incoming plane wave by 2.0 mrad. Predict the position of the central spot and compare both manipulations.

Show the shift theorem

Writing the translated aperture as \(A(x-x_0)\) and putting \(u=x-x_0\) in the transform gives

\[U(q)=e^{-iqx_0}U_0(q).\]

Its squared magnitude is unchanged. Tilt instead multiplies the aperture field by \(e^{ik\alpha x}\), replacing the pattern by \(U_0(q-k\alpha)\). The peak moves to \(X=f\alpha=0.500\) mm for the chosen sign. Either statement presumes the illuminating beam covers the translated slit uniformly and the lens pupil does not clip the pattern.

Pixel pitch or optical resolution?

An air objective with NA 0.50 images independent emitters at 600 nm onto 5.0 μm pixels with magnification 10. Calculate the Rayleigh scale, optical cutoff and detector Nyquist frequency referred to object space. Find the minimum magnification sampling the full passband.

Show the independent limits

The Rayleigh scale is \(0.61(0.600)/0.50=0.732\) μm. The incoherent cutoff is \(2(0.50)/0.600=1.67\) cycles/μm. Each pixel spans \(0.500\) μm in the object plane, so Nyquist frequency is \(1/(2\times0.500)=1.00\) cycle/μm. To capture all optical frequencies without aliasing set object pitch at most \(0.300\) μm, requiring magnification \(M\ge5.0/0.300=16.7\). Better sampling preserves more optical contrast but cannot transmit spatial frequencies outside the optical passband.

Explore these relations

  • For the aperture transform, vary slit width and inspect the diffraction envelope; the two-slit interference factor is an additional feature absent from a single slit. Open the live calculation.
  • For pupil-limited imaging, vary aperture and wavelength and compare the diffraction response under defocus. Open the live calculation.

Infer slit spacing from a sampled angular minimum

The first minimum is measured at 9.75° on the 0.90 m receiver arc, with a 0.25° angular sample spacing. Assuming a far-field two-slit model, infer the dimensionless spacing \(d/\lambda\). Propagate half a sampling interval as an angle uncertainty. Does this source of uncertainty alone cover the designed spacing of 3?

Work through the solution

The first destructive condition is \(d\sin\theta=\lambda/2\), so

\[d/\lambda=1/[2\sin(9.75^\circ)]=2.9525.\]

The fractional change is \(|\cot\theta\,d\theta|\). With \(0.125^\circ=0.002182\) radians this is about 1.27%, or 0.0375 in \(d/\lambda\). The upper estimate is about 2.990, below 3. The sampling interval alone falls short. Finite observation distance and the model of aperture illumination can also move the observed minimum.

Use the OneSimulation laboratory to check your answer

Set separation/wavelength to 3, radius to 0.90 m and width to 0.050 m. Compare the sampled finite-radius minimum with the far-field curve before attributing the discrepancy to angular sampling alone. Open the corresponding live laboratory reference.

Chapter 9 · Multiple-beam interference in a cavity

Sum the returning fields in a plane-parallel optical cavity and turn measured line spacing and width into a mirror diagnostic.

From first principles

Sum the repeated transmissions of a cavity

Consider an ideal plane cavity with two identical lossless mirrors of power reflectance \(R<1\), normal incidence and coherent monochromatic light. The first transmitted field has magnitude \((1-R)\) times the incident field. Each later transmitted copy makes one extra round trip, acquiring an amplitude multiplier \(R\) and a phase \(\delta\) that includes any mirror reflection phases. In steady state the optical fields add as a convergent geometric series.

\[\frac{E_t}{E_i}=(1-R)e^{i\psi}\sum_{j=0}^\infty (Re^{i\delta})^j=\frac{(1-R)e^{i\psi}}{1-Re^{i\delta}}.\]

The phase \(\psi\) acquired on the first traversal cancels in intensity. Multiply the denominator by its complex conjugate and use \(1-\cos\delta=2\sin^2(\delta/2)\) to obtain

\[\mathcal T=\frac{(1-R)^2}{(1-R)^2+4R\sin^2(\delta/2)}.\]

At \(\delta=2\pi m\) all transmitted copies arrive in phase and the ideal transmission reaches unity. Increasing reflectance allows more copies to matter, so a smaller change in phase spoils their alignment and narrows the transmission peak. Loss, mirror imbalance or imperfect mode overlap can reduce its height.

Derivation and applications

Measure mirror imbalance from resonant transmission

High finesse alone does not determine the brightness of a resonant cavity. Consider two partially transmitting lossless mirrors at normal incidence, separated by a transparent gap. Their power reflectances are \(R_1\) and \(R_2\), and \(T_j=1-R_j\). Assume the incident spatial mode is matched, a single polarisation is present and reflected and transmitted fields are normalised to equal external impedances. Absorb mirror reflection phases into the round-trip phase \(\delta\). The first transmitted field has magnitude \(\sqrt{T_1T_2}\) relative to the incident field. Each extra circulation multiplies it by \(\sqrt{R_1R_2}e^{i\delta}\).

\[\frac{E_t}{E_i}=e^{i\psi}\sqrt{T_1T_2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\big(\sqrt{R_1R_2}e^{i\delta}\big)^n=\frac{e^{i\psi}\sqrt{T_1T_2}}{1-\sqrt{R_1R_2}e^{i\delta}}.\]

The common single-pass phase \(\psi\) disappears on taking the power ratio. At a resonance the phase gained per round trip is an integer multiple of \(2\pi\) and every contribution adds in phase. The resulting peak transmission is

\[\mathcal T_{\rm res}=\frac{(1-R_1)(1-R_2)}{(1-\sqrt{R_1R_2})^2}.\]

There is no internal loss, so the untransmitted fraction exits through the input port. Subtracting the numerator from the denominator and factoring the difference gives the reflected power on resonance.

\[\mathcal R_{\rm res}=1-\mathcal T_{\rm res}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{R_1}-\sqrt{R_2}}{1-\sqrt{R_1R_2}}\right)^2 .\]

Identical mirrors cancel the net reflected field on resonance even when each isolated mirror transmits only a small fraction. The result is symmetric under exchange of the two mirrors. A power scan by itself cannot determine which mirror is the more reflective one.

Worked example

Predict the output of unequal mirrors

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Let the input and output mirror field-reflection magnitudes be 0.900 and 0.800. Their power transmissions are 0.190 and 0.360. The peak transmitted fraction is

\[(0.190)(0.360)/(1-0.720)^2=0.872.\]

For 5.00 mW incident in the matched mode this gives 4.36 mW transmitted and 0.638 mW reflected. The sum equals the incident power to rounding.

Second application

Identify a replacement output mirror

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A second cavity has a known input field-reflection magnitude 0.800 and an output mirror independently known to be less reflective. Its measured resonant reflected power is \(1/16\) of incident power. Taking the appropriate square root gives

\[(0.800-r_2)/(1-0.800r_2)=0.250.\]

Solving yields \(r_2=0.6875\) and \(R_2=0.473\). The predicted peak transmitted fraction is \(15/16=0.9375\), providing a separate measurement to test the lossless and mode-matching assumptions.

Try it · Asymmetric cavity

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

Many transmitted copies

A transparent gap of length \(L\) and refractive index \(n\) sits between two identical plane mirrors. Illuminate it at normal incidence with a coherent, monochromatic plane wave. Assume the mirrors are lossless, their intensity reflectivity \(R\) is constant over the band, and the outgoing beams overlap in space. Each trip between mirrors adds phase. Each completed round trip also reflects the field twice. Because one mirror reflection has amplitude magnitude \(\sqrt R\), the field magnitude after both is multiplied by \(R\).

Choose a phase convention absorbing constant reflection phases into the resonance condition. A single transmitted pass has amplitude magnitude \((1-R)\) times the incident amplitude. The pass emerging after one additional round trip acquires \(R e^{i\delta}\) with \(\delta=4\pi nL/\lambda_0\). Summing the geometric series and squaring the magnitude gives

\[\mathcal T=\frac{(1-R)^2}{|1-Re^{i\delta}|^2}=\frac{1}{1+F\sin^2(\delta/2)},\]
\[F=\frac{4R}{(1-R)^2}.\]

Resolving the peaks

Transmission reaches unity at \(\delta=2\pi m\) in this symmetric lossless model. Coherent addition of multiply reflected fields simultaneously cancels the returning field at the input. With unequal mirrors or intracavity loss, peak transmission is generally lower. The resonance frequencies are \(\nu_m=mc/(2nL)\) when dispersion is negligible, giving a free spectral range \(\mathrm{FSR}=c/(2nL)\). If \(n\) varies appreciably, differentiation of round-trip phase replaces it with the group index.

The half-power points satisfy \(|\sin(\delta/2)|=1/\sqrt F\) near resonance. Their full separation in phase is \(4\arcsin(1/\sqrt F)\) when \(F\ge1\). Dividing the \(2\pi\) phase interval by that width yields finesse,

\[\mathcal F=\pi/[2\arcsin(1/\sqrt F)].\]

For large \(F\) this reduces to \(\pi\sqrt R/(1-R)\). Finesse counts how many linewidths fit between adjacent orders. It is distinct from resolving power \(\nu/\Delta\nu\), which also depends on the order.

Try it · Fabry perot

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Open this live calculation

Inspect a scanned cavity

An air gap of 15.0 mm with \(R=0.900\) has a free spectral range of 9.99 GHz. The exact finesse is about 29.8, so adjacent half-power frequencies across one peak differ by 335 MHz. At a 600 nm carrier that width corresponds to about \(0.00040\) nm. Two lines one free spectral range apart nevertheless reach resonance together. Very narrow peaks and unambiguous identification across a broad input spectrum are different instrument requirements.

Mirror tilt makes different parts of a beam see different round-trip phases. Finite source divergence has a similar effect, since oblique rays accumulate a different axial phase. If raising the nominal mirror reflectivity no longer narrows a measured peak as predicted, compare the line shape at two illuminated spot sizes or angular apertures. Such a test separates instrumental phase averaging from a change in the source spectrum.

Measuring a frequency scan in both directions supplies a useful consistency test. Thermal expansion of the spacer or actuator hysteresis can change the calibration between scan position and optical frequency. An independently known reference line or sideband establishes that calibration without relying on the actuator displacement alone.

Derive cavity linewidth from the decay after switch-off

A cavity that transmits a narrow frequency range also releases stored energy slowly. This connection can be demonstrated without fitting another steady interference pattern. Let \(t_{\rm rt}=2nL/c\) be the round-trip time in a nondispersive empty-gap model and let \(\rho\) be the intensity survival fraction per completed round trip, including both mirrors and all distributed losses. After turning off a resonant input, the energy remaining after \(j\) round trips is \(U_j=U_0\rho^j\). For decay slow compared with one trip, replace the discrete sequence by its continuous envelope,

\[U(t)=U_0e^{-t/\tau_U},\]
\[\tau_U=-t_{\rm rt}/\ln\rho .\]

The output power leaking through a fixed mirror follows this energy envelope. A field amplitude instead decays as \(e^{-t/(2\tau_U)}\). Fourier transforming that causal amplitude gives a response proportional to \([1/(2\tau_U)-i2\pi(\nu-\nu_m)]^{-1}\) close to a resonance. Squaring the magnitude gives a Lorentzian with full width at half maximum \(\Delta\nu\simeq1/(2\pi\tau_U)\). This single-line approximation requires many round trips during a lifetime, or well-separated narrow resonances. It links a time-domain loss measurement to the spectral width obtained by scanning the cavity.

For two identical mirrors of intensity reflectivity \(R\) and negligible internal absorption, \(\rho=R^2\). In the high-reflectivity limit \(\ln\rho\simeq-2(1-R)\), reproducing the familiar finesse dependence of linewidth. Other loss channels can shorten the lifetime even if the two measured peak frequencies keep the same separation.

Determine an additional internal loss

An air cavity has length 0.300 m, giving \(t_{\rm rt}=2.00\) ns and free spectral range 500 MHz. Both mirrors have independently calibrated \(R=0.9950\). With no other losses the expected survival is \(0.990025\) and energy lifetime is

\[-2.00\,{\rm ns}/\ln(0.990025)=200\,\mathrm{ns}.\]

A fast detector instead records an intensity \(1/e\) time of 150 ns. Effective survival is then

\[\exp(-2.00/150)=0.98676.\]

Dividing by \(R^2\) gives an internal survival factor \(0.9967\) per round trip, an extra loss of about \(0.33\) percent.

The inferred full linewidth is \(1/(2\pi\times150\,{\rm ns})=1.06\) MHz, well below the free spectral range. A frequency sweep should agree if the driving laser is narrower than this line and the scan is sufficiently slow to reach steady response. If a measured line is broader while the ring-down lifetime remains unchanged, source jitter or mechanical length fluctuations can broaden the scan without absorbing additional circulating energy. At very low reflectivity the separate-resonance model fails, and individual delayed output pulses provide a more direct interpretation.

The extra loss can be turned into a material attenuation estimate if its path length is known. If all the inferred internal loss came from a sample traversed over 20.0 mm of material per round trip, its intensity attenuation coefficient would be \(-\ln(0.9967)/0.0200=0.165\) m⁻¹. This attribution assumes that inserting the sample does not add appreciable surface reflection or scattering. Measuring otherwise identical samples of two thicknesses separates a loss growing with path length from a nearly constant contribution at the surfaces, using the same ring-down protocol.

Study the full derivation

Measure rotation with two waves following the same path · Optical fields, instruments and observations

A stationary optical loop gives equal return times for opposite propagation directions in a reciprocal medium. When the loop rotates the beams meet their starting beamsplitter at different times. Derive the measurable differential phase and compare a passive interferometer with a ring resonator.

Chapter 10 · How a grating separates wavelengths

Derive the angular dispersion and resolving power of a finite grating and diagnose an order overlap.

From first principles

Derive a grating peak from equal phase steps

Take \(N\) equally spaced, equally illuminated grooves of pitch \(d\) under normal monochromatic illumination. Observe in the far field at angle \(\theta\) to the grating normal. Neighbouring positions produce a path difference \(d\sin\theta\). Ignoring the common single-groove envelope for the moment, each groove contributes an equal field with phase increment \(\delta\). Their sum is

\[\delta=\frac{2\pi d}{\lambda}\sin\theta,\]
\[U=U_1\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}e^{ij\delta}=U_1 e^{i(N-1)\delta/2}\frac{\sin(N\delta/2)}{\sin(\delta/2)} .\]

The fields line up at \(\delta=2\pi m\), giving \(d\sin\theta=m\lambda\). The nearest zero requires an additional phase step of \(2\pi/N\). Convert that change to a small angle and compare with the angular separation of two neighbouring wavelengths at fixed order.

\[\Delta\theta_{\rm zero}\simeq\frac{\lambda}{Nd\cos\theta},\]
\[\Delta\theta_{\lambda}\simeq\frac{|m|\Delta\lambda}{d\cos\theta}.\]

Equating the separations gives the ideal Rayleigh resolving power \(\lambda/\Delta\lambda=|m|N\) for nonzero order. It is the finite illuminated groove count that fixes the intrinsic width; optical aberrations, slit width and pixel sampling enter a measured line profile separately.

Derivation and applications

Convert an entrance-slit width into spectral broadening

An ideal grating can distinguish wavelengths separated by about \(\lambda/(mN)\) in order \(m\) with \(N\) illuminated grooves. A finite entrance slit adds another width because light from different points on the slit arrives at different incidence angles. Consider a slit of full width \(w\) in the focal plane of a collimator of focal length \(f_c\). Use small angular deviations around incidence \(\alpha\) and diffraction angle \(\beta\), both defined in the convention \(m\lambda=d(\sin\alpha+\sin\beta)\) for groove spacing \(d\). Differentiating at fixed wavelength gives

\[0=d(\cos\alpha\,d\alpha+\cos\beta\,d\beta),\]
\[|d\beta|=\frac{\cos\alpha}{\cos\beta}|d\alpha| .\]

The angular interval incident on the grating is \(w/f_c\). A camera lens of focal length \(f_{\rm cam}\) converts the outgoing angular spread into a full image width \(W\) at its focal plane. Measure local displacements perpendicular to the central diffracted ray.

\[W=f_{\rm cam}\frac{\cos\alpha}{\cos\beta}\frac{w}{f_c},\]
\[\left|\frac{dx}{d\lambda}\right|=\frac{f_{\rm cam}m}{d\cos\beta}.\]

The second relation follows by varying wavelength at fixed incidence. Dividing the slit image width by this local dispersion gives the wavelength interval corresponding to the full geometric slit image.

\[\Delta\lambda_{\rm slit}=\frac{d\cos\alpha}{m}\frac{w}{f_c}.\]

The camera focal length cancels because it magnifies both the slit image and the spectrum equally. Actual recorded lines are convolutions of the projected slit, diffraction response and detector response. Their distinct width definitions must be retained when fitting line profiles.

Worked example

Identify the dominant width in a green spectrometer

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

For \(d=1.00\) µm, \(\alpha=0\), \(m=1\), \(f_c=100\) mm and \(w=50.0\) µm, the geometric width is 0.500 nm. At 500 nm, \(N=10{,}000\) illuminated grooves give a diffraction resolution scale of 0.0500 nm. The slit width corresponds to ten such intervals. Doubling the camera focal length would double the image width in pixels while leaving the geometric 0.500 nm wavelength interval unchanged.

Second application

Size a slit for an angled input

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A separate instrument uses \(d=2.00\) µm, \(\alpha=60.0^\circ\), \(m=2\) and \(f_c=200\) mm. To obtain a full geometric spectral width of 0.100 nm, invert the result: \(w=m f_c\Delta\lambda/(d\cos\alpha)=40.0\) µm. At \(\lambda=600\) nm the grating equation gives \(\sin\beta=0.600-0.866=-0.266\), a physically accessible direction. This angle check matters when changing high orders or incidence settings.

Try it · Slit resolution

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Start from this chapter. Treat the two slits as the two-element limit of an array. Check how changing wavelength moves its first minimum. The numerical model has two apertures throughout, so many-groove resolving power remains a separate prediction.

OneSimulation laboratory · Find the dark fringes of a double slit

Interference across equally spaced openings

Illuminate \(N\) identical narrow slits separated by distance \(d\) with normally incident monochromatic light. In the far field every slit produces the same single-slit envelope. At observation angle \(\theta\) adjacent contributions differ in optical path by \(d\sin\theta\), so their phases differ by \(\phi=2\pi d\sin\theta/\lambda\). The array field equals the single-slit field multiplied by a sum of \(N\) unit phasors. Taking its geometric sum and squared magnitude gives the array intensity factor

\[G(\phi)=\left[\frac{\sin(N\phi/2)}{\sin(\phi/2)}\right]^2 .\]

At \(\phi=2\pi m\) the expression has a removable limit \(N^2\). Those directions satisfy the grating equation \(d\sin\theta_m=m\lambda\). Each peak collects contributions from all illuminated slits in phase. Some orders can nevertheless be faint or missing if the single-slit envelope is small or zero there. Transmission efficiency into a selected order thus depends on the groove or opening profile in addition to the grating period.

Dispersion and finite width

At fixed order different wavelengths peak at different angles. Differentiate the grating equation to obtain

\[d\theta_m/d\lambda=m/(d\cos\theta_m).\]

A large angular dispersion aids spatial separation at the detector. To assess resolution, also compute the width of each peak. The adjacent zeros of the numerator lie one phase increment \(2\pi/N\) either side of the maximum. Converting the increment back to angle gives a one-sided width \(\lambda/(Nd\cos\theta_m)\).

Choose the conventional criterion that one wavelength's peak falls on the neighbour's first zero. Equate the angular separation due to \(\Delta\lambda\) to this one-sided width and obtain \(\lambda/\Delta\lambda=|m|N\). Broad illumination of a grating therefore improves the ideal resolving power even with unchanged dispersion. The derivation assumes uniform coherent illumination, collimation, adequate detector sampling, and an entrance slit narrow enough to preserve this diffraction-limited width.

A small spectrometer

A 600-lines/mm grating has \(d=1.667\) μm. A collimated 10.0 mm beam illuminates 6000 lines. At \(\lambda=600\) nm first order occurs at \(\arcsin(0.360)=21.1^\circ\). The ideal smallest separation is

\[600/6000=0.100\,\mathrm{nm}.\]

The angular dispersion is \(6.43\times10^{-4}\) rad/nm. A camera of focal length 200 mm centred on that order maps a 0.100 nm change to about \(12.9\) μm near its axis. Detector pixels that are large compared with this separation reduce the measurable resolution.

At that same angle, 300 nm light in second order has the same value of \(m\lambda\). Increasing the beam width narrows both peaks without separating their centres. An order-selecting filter can restrict the allowed band before measurement. A second grating or prism dispersing perpendicular to the first can instead separate overlapping orders into different traces. This illustrates why calibration with only one visible line cannot certify the identity of every line in a wide-band spectrum.

Test the finite-array argument with the two-slit acoustic field. For two equally excited slits the array factor has its second positive-angle zero at \(d\sin\theta=3\lambda/2\). With \(d/\lambda=3\), predict \(30.0^\circ\) and compare with the sampled \(30.50^\circ\) at receiver radius 0.90 m. At \(d/\lambda=2\) predict \(48.59^\circ\). Compare the measured angle at both receiver radii to see how the finite observation distance affects the far-field limit.

Map overlapping orders in a high-order spectrometer

Using high diffraction order increases the ideal resolving power for a fixed number of illuminated grooves. It also reduces how much spectrum belongs unambiguously to a single order. Consider a reflection grating in a geometry where incidence and diffraction are on the same side of the normal in the usual grating-angle convention. The path difference between neighbouring grooves is \(d(\sin\alpha+\sin\beta)\), so peaks obey \(m\lambda=d(\sin\alpha+\sin\beta)\). In a Littrow setting the selected diffracted beam retraces the incident direction and \(\alpha=\beta=\theta\).

At fixed angle two wavelengths overlap exactly if \(m\lambda_m=(m+1)\lambda_{m+1}\). Solving for their separation gives \(\lambda_m-\lambda_{m+1}=\lambda_m/(m+1)\), approximately \(\lambda_m/m\) at high order. This is the scale of a free spectral range in wavelength. Combined with resolving power \(mN\) for \(N\) uniformly illuminated grooves, the number of independently resolved spectral elements per order is approximately \(N\). Higher order makes each element narrower while proportionately reducing the unambiguous range at a fixed spatial coordinate.

Real echelle gratings use inclined groove facets to redirect much of the reflected power into the chosen high orders. The groove spacing sets peak positions and order overlap; the groove profile sets an efficiency envelope. A second dispersive element ordinarily separates the superposed orders along another detector coordinate. Its angular dispersion can be lower than the primary grating's because it only needs to separate different orders, not resolve the closest spectral lines within one.

Design the spectral window at one detector position

Take groove spacing \(d=31.6\) μm, order \(m=100\) and central wavelength 550 nm. The Littrow relation requires \(\sin\theta=m\lambda/(2d)=55.0/63.2=0.870\), hence \(\theta=60.5^\circ\). Illuminating 1000 grooves gives ideal wavelength resolution \(550/(100\times1000)=0.00550\) nm. The wavelength in order 101 landing at the same position is

\[550(100/101)=544.55\,\mathrm{nm}.\]

Thus the separation between these coincident-order wavelengths is 5.45 nm, about 990 resolution intervals.

A narrow-band filter passing 548 to 552 nm would reject that particular neighbouring order at the central position while retaining many resolvable lines near 550 nm. An instrument meant to record hundreds of nanometres at once needs cross-dispersion or several separate exposures. As incidence approaches grazing, simple angular-dispersion estimates can become very large, but finite beam footprint, usable groove count, diffraction efficiency and detector size limit the physical gain. Accounting for order separation, illuminated footprint and sampling is necessary before describing two nearby spectral features as resolvable in the laboratory.

A rough detector-format estimate follows directly from this example. If each independent spectral resolution interval requires at least two samples, one full free spectral range near the chosen order needs about two thousand useful pixels along the high-dispersion direction. That count ignores additional margins, optical distortion and the need to sample the entrance-slit profile adequately. The perpendicular axis must also have room for the traces of neighbouring orders and for a background estimate between them. Resolution, spectral coverage and detector format therefore impose independent constraints on what a single exposure can realistically measure.

Separate groove count from grating period

Keep the grating period and incident wavelength fixed. Adding \(N\) equal amplitudes as a geometric series gives a central peak whose first zeros occur at phase mismatch \(\pm 1/N\) in turns. Each plotted peak is normalised to its own height.

Problem sheet 5 · Orders, linewidths and spectral identity

Use cavity and grating data to separate linewidth, resolving power and overlap of different spectral orders.

Infer mirror reflectivity

A symmetric, lossless air cavity 30.0 mm long has a transmission peak full width of 100 MHz. Determine the free spectral range and finesse. Estimate \(R\) using the high-finesse formula and predict how doubling cavity length changes width.

Show the cavity inference

The free spectral range is \(c/(2L)=5.00\) GHz and finesse is approximately 50. Writing \(x=\sqrt R\), the approximation \(\mathcal F=\pi x/(1-x^2)\) gives

\[x=[-\pi+\sqrt{\pi^2+4\mathcal F^2}]/(2\mathcal F)=0.969.\]

Thus \(R\simeq0.939\). For unchanged mirrors and negligible dispersion the finesse stays constant. Doubling length halves both free spectral range and linewidth, to 2.50 GHz and 50 MHz.

Select enough grooves

A first-order grating must separate 589.0 and 589.6 nm by the Rayleigh criterion. Determine the minimum illuminated number of grooves and the width at 1200 grooves/mm. Calculate the first-order diffraction angle near the doublet centre.

Show the finite-array calculation

Using 589.3 nm as representative wavelength gives \(N\ge589.3/0.6=982.2\), so illuminate at least 983 grooves. Their span is approximately \(983/1200=0.819\) mm. The period is 833.3 nm. The grating equation gives \(\sin\theta=589.3/833.3=0.7072\), hence \(\theta\simeq45.0^\circ\). Detector sampling, entrance-slit size and unequal line intensities may require a larger illuminated width in practice.

Identify two peaks in one setting

A cavity with 8.0 GHz free spectral range transmits two optical frequencies separated by exactly one order at around 600 nm. Determine their wavelength separation to first order. Find the first-order grating resolving power needed to distinguish them and explain what separate measurements determine.

Show the order ambiguity

From \(\nu=c/\lambda\), \(|\Delta\lambda|\simeq\lambda^2\Delta\nu/c=0.0096\) nm. Their ratio \(\lambda/|\Delta\lambda|=62\,500\) requires about 62,500 uniformly illuminated grooves in first order under the ideal criterion. A narrow cavity scan can measure lines within one order very accurately but maps frequencies apart by the free spectral range to the same phase. A sufficiently resolving independently calibrated grating can distinguish the coincident orders. A second cavity of different spacing can also change their relative alignment.

Explore these relations

  • For the mirror inference, vary reflectivity and compare the cavity linewidth and free spectral range. Open the live calculation.
  • For groove counts and overlapping orders, vary groove number and compare the interference peaks. Open the live calculation.

Chapter 11 · Gaussian beams and focusing

Relate beam waist, wavefront curvature and divergence, and estimate the focus of a collimated laser beam.

From first principles

Obtain a Gaussian divergence from its waist profile

Take a monochromatic fundamental beam in a uniform medium at a waist plane with flat phase front. Let the transverse field be \(U(x,0)=U_0e^{-x^2/w_0^2}\) on one diameter. Scalar diffraction represents this field as a superposition of plane waves with transverse wavenumber \(q\). The Fourier transform of a Gaussian is again a Gaussian, with reciprocal width.

\[\widetilde U(q)\propto\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-x^2/w_0^2}e^{-iqx}dx\propto e^{-q^2w_0^2/4}.\]

For a paraxial plane wave \(q=k\sin\theta\simeq k\theta\), with \(k=2\pi/\lambda\). In the far field each angle addresses its corresponding plane-wave amplitude. The \(1/e\) field half-angle, also the \(1/e^2\) intensity half-angle, therefore satisfies

\[\frac{k^2\theta_0^2w_0^2}{4}=1,\]
\[\theta_0=\frac{\lambda}{\pi w_0}.\]

The characteristic propagation length is

\[z_R=w_0/\theta_0=\pi w_0^2/\lambda.\]

The paraxial Gaussian solution gives \(w(z)=w_0\sqrt{1+(z/z_R)^2}\), approaching \(\theta_0|z|\) far from the waist. A tighter waist thus brings both larger angular spread and shorter depth of focus.

Background · Gaussian Rayleigh range
\[z_R=\pi w_0^2/\lambda\]

Here the waist is the one-over-e field radius and the wavelength is in the medium. At one Rayleigh range its squared beam radius doubles.

Derivation and applications

Measure a Gaussian beam with a translating knife edge

A camera measures an intensity profile directly. A power meter behind a straight edge instead integrates the light that passes the edge. For a centred, circular fundamental Gaussian at one measurement plane, write \(I(x,y)=I_p\exp[-2(x^2+y^2)/w^2]\), where \(w\) is the \(1/e^2\) intensity radius. Assume that the power meter collects all transmitted light, the edge is opaque and straight, and diffraction at the edge does not send appreciable power outside the detector. With the opening extending from \(x=b\) to infinity, integration over \(y\) first gives

\[P(b)=I_p w\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\int_b^\infty e^{-2x^2/w^2}dx,\]
\[P_{\rm tot}=\frac{\pi w^2 I_p}{2}.\]

Change variables to \(u=\sqrt{2}x/w\) and normalise to the unobstructed reading. This step removes the absolute intensity calibration and gives a cumulative Gaussian curve in the measured edge position.

\[\frac{P(b)}{P_{\rm tot}}=\frac12\operatorname{erfc}\!\left(\frac{\sqrt2\,b}{w}\right).\]

At the half-power point the edge passes through the beam centre. At the ten and ninety percent positions the arguments of the complementary error function have equal magnitudes and opposite signs. Their separation is consequently independent of the arbitrary origin of the translation stage.

\[b_{10}-b_{90}=\sqrt2\,w\,\operatorname{erf}^{-1}(0.8)=1.28155\,w .\]

The derivative of the measured power curve is proportional to the intensity integrated along the blade, which gives another way to inspect asymmetry. For an elliptical Gaussian aligned with the axes, translation in \(x\) measures the \(x\) radius, and rotating the blade allows the other radius to be tested.

Worked example

Infer a beam radius without a calibrated camera

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

An edge produces 90.0 percent transmission at 1.24 mm and 10.0 percent at 1.88 mm. Their separation gives

\[w=0.640/1.28155=0.499\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The centre lies at their midpoint, 1.56 mm. An unobstructed reading of 2.00 mW then gives \(I_p=2P_{\rm tot}/(\pi w^2)=5.11\) kW/m² for a circular beam.

Second application

Locate a useful aperture radius

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

A different circular beam has \(w=0.250\) mm. A straight edge at \(b=w\) would transmit \(\operatorname{erfc}(\sqrt2)/2=0.0228\) of its power. A centred circular aperture of that radius transmits \(1-\exp(-2a^2/w^2)=0.865\) after radial integration. To pass 99.0 percent, its radius must instead be \(a=w\sqrt{\ln100/2}=0.379\) mm. The different answers follow from the areas collected by a half-plane and a disk.

Measure the phase that a waist adds to a Gaussian beam

Two axial planes on opposite sides of a waist can have identical intensity and different phase. An interferometer comparing the field with a calibrated reference can distinguish them. Predict the sign and size of the residual phase before drawing the quadrature traces.

Factor out ordinary plane-wave propagation

For a fundamental paraxial Gaussian beam with waist field proportional to \(\exp(-r^2/w_0^2)\), the transverse angular spectrum is a Gaussian in transverse wave number. Each component propagates with an additional paraxial phase \(\exp[-izk_\perp^2/(2k)]\) after removing the common \(e^{ikz}\) carrier. On the axis one integrates over both transverse wave-vector components. Each Gaussian integral contributes an inverse square root of a complex width; their product gives

\[\frac{U(0,z)}{U(0,0)e^{ikz}}=\frac{1}{1+iz/z_R},\]
\[z_R=\pi w_0^2/\lambda .\]

The residual phase with this travelling-wave convention is \(-\arctan(z/z_R)\). Its difference between positions very far upstream and downstream approaches \(-\pi\). Its difference across a finite focus is generally smaller. At \(z=\pm z_R\) the residual phases are \(\mp\pi/4\); relative to a plane wave the focused beam loses a total of \(\pi/2\) across those two points. The intensity, by contrast, is \(1/[1+(z/z_R)^2]\) in units of the waist intensity, which has identical values at the two positions.
