Open this live calculation

Download the corresponding editable notebook. The browser recalculates the reduced equations directly; the notebook exposes the full calculation and its checks.

For \(w_0=40\) micrometres and \(\lambda=0.633\) micrometre in the medium, \(z_R=7.94\) mm. The axial phase gradient at the waist is smaller than the carrier wave number by \(1/z_R\), about \(126\,\mathrm{rad/m}\). An ordinary path-length comparison over millimetres must remove many carrier cycles to see this residual accurately. Measuring visibility alone will not do so. With a reference field \(E_r\), the interference term in intensity is \(2\operatorname{Re}(E_sE_r^*)\). Shifting the reference phase by a known quarter turn gives the complementary quadrature. After subtracting backgrounds and calibrating gains one reconstructs phase by the signed two-argument arctangent and unwraps it through adjacent positions.

The notebook applies an adjustable constant phase offset to both synthetic channels. It disappears from a calibrated phase difference. Doubling the waist multiplies the Rayleigh length by four. The total residual phase across the complete focus is still \(-\pi\), but a scan over a fixed physical distance sees a smaller fraction of it. Wavefront curvature also contributes a phase away from the axis. Keep the detector on axis, or model its transverse position and aperture before attributing a measured phase entirely to the Gouy shift.

The residual Gouy phase changes by \(-\pi/3\) between symmetric planes \(z=-5\) mm and \(z=+5\) mm. Infer the Rayleigh length and the waist radius at 0.633 micrometre wavelength.

Work through the solution

Symmetry gives \(2\arctan(5\ \mathrm{mm}/z_R)=\pi/3\), hence

\[z_R=5/\tan(\pi/6)=8.66\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

The waist radius is \(\sqrt{\lambda z_R/\pi}\), about \(41.8\) micrometres. This inference uses the one-over-e field radius of a fundamental circular Gaussian beam and a phase referenced to the same plane-wave propagation.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Diffraction during propagation

A narrow parallel-looking laser beam eventually spreads. Use a scalar monochromatic field \(U=u(x,y,z)e^{ikz}\), with \(u\) changing slowly on the scale of a wavelength. Substitution into the homogeneous Helmholtz equation cancels the largest longitudinal terms. Dropping \(\partial_z^2u\) relative to \(2ik\partial_zu\) gives the paraxial equation

\[2ik\,\partial_z u+\nabla_\perp^2u=0 .\]

This approximation requires a small angular spectrum about the optical axis. The transverse Fourier component with wavevector \(\mathbf q\) acquires phase \(\exp[-iq^2z/(2k)]\). A Gaussian waist field \(u(r,0)=u_0e^{-r^2/w_0^2}\) has a Gaussian angular spectrum proportional to \(\exp(-w_0^2q^2/4)\). On propagation, multiplication by the quadratic phase keeps that spectrum Gaussian with a complex width.

Invert the Gaussian transform

Collect its exponent as \(-q^2(w_0^2/4+iz/2k)\) and invert the transverse Fourier transform. The reciprocal complex width determines both the real amplitude decay in \(r\) and the phase curvature. Separating the real and imaginary parts gives

\[w(z)=w_0\sqrt{1+(z/z_R)^2},\]
\[R(z)=z[1+(z_R/z)^2],\]
\[z_R=\pi w_0^2/\lambda .\]

The convention here defines \(w\) at \(1/e\) of field amplitude and \(1/e^2\) of on-axis intensity. The on-axis intensity falls as \(w_0^2/w^2\) so the transverse integral of intensity stays constant. At a Rayleigh range from the waist the area doubles and the axial intensity halves. At large distance the half-angle divergence approaches \(\theta=\lambda/(\pi w_0)\). Waist size and angular spread therefore cannot be chosen independently for this fundamental mode.

OneSimulation · Two identical intensity photographs, two different waists

Give a 633 nm Gaussian beam a 0.40 mm field radius and take an intensity photograph at \(z=0\). Reversing the sign of its quadratic phase leaves every pixel of that photograph unchanged. The signed wavefront radius \(R\), however, sets the local tilt of the wave normal. In the paraxial limit the predicted radius is

\[w^2(z)=w_0^2\!\left[\left(1+\frac zR\right)^2+\left(\frac{z}{z_R}\right)^2\right],\]
\[z_R=\frac{\pi w_0^2}{\lambda}.\]

Here \(w_0\) names the radius in the photograph. The true waist can lie elsewhere. Propagate both complex fields with OneSimulation's angular spectrum. Each sampled transverse spatial frequency acquires its own longitudinal phase. Measure the radius as twice the square root of the intensity-weighted second moment and compare with the paraxial prediction.

"""Propagate two Gaussian fields with the same measured input intensity."""
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np
from onesim.physics.optics.propagation.numerics.angular_spectrum import (
    AngularSpectrumPropagation, longitudinal_wavenumber,
)

wavelength, width = 633e-9, 0.40e-3  # m; width is the 1/e field radius
k = 2 * np.pi / wavelength
rayleigh = np.pi * width**2 / wavelength
x = np.linspace(-6e-3, 6e-3, 2048, endpoint=False)
kx = 2 * np.pi * np.fft.fftfreq(len(x), d=x[1] - x[0])
propagate = AngularSpectrumPropagation(longitudinal=longitudinal_wavenumber(kx[:, None], k),
    forward=np.fft.fft, inverse=np.fft.ifft, reference_wavenumber=k)
z = np.linspace(0, 0.90, 91)
fig, ax = plt.subplots(figsize=(6.6, 3.5), layout="constrained")
for curvature, label in [(-0.60, "Converging"), (0.60, "Diverging")]:
    field = np.exp(-(x / width)**2 + 1j * k * x**2 / (2 * curvature))
    radii = []
    for distance in z:
        intensity = np.abs(propagate(field, distance))**2
        radii.append(2 * np.sqrt(np.average(x**2, weights=intensity)))
    reference = width * np.sqrt((1 + z / curvature)**2 + (z / rayleigh)**2)
    assert np.allclose(radii, reference, rtol=2e-5, atol=0)
    print(f"{label}: narrowest sampled radius {1000*min(radii):.3f} mm at {z[np.argmin(radii)]:.2f} m")
    ax.plot(z, 1000 * np.asarray(radii), label=label)
ax.set(xlabel="Propagation distance (m)", ylabel="Beam radius (mm)")
ax.grid(alpha=0.2)
ax.legend()
fig.savefig("waves-optics.png", dpi=180)

When does a beam propagate without diffracting?

The Gaussian beam spreads freely because its angular components develop different longitudinal phases. A dielectric guide can instead admit a transverse field that reproduces itself times a single propagation phase. Take a symmetric slab of core index \(n_1\), cladding index \(n_2<n_1\), and core extending from \(x=-a\) to \(a\). For electric polarization parallel to the interfaces write \(E_y=X(x)e^{i(\beta z-\omega t)}\). The scalar wave equation gives

\[X''+(n^2k_0^2-\beta^2)X=0.\]

For a guided mode require oscillation in the core and decay in the cladding, so \(n_2k_0<\beta<n_1k_0\).

The even solution is proportional to \(\cos(hx)\) in the core and \(e^{-\gamma(x-a)}\) in the right cladding. Continuity of \(X\) and its derivative for equal magnetic permeabilities requires

\[h\tan(ha)=\gamma.\]

Define \(u=ha\), \(w=\gamma a\) and \(V=k_0a\sqrt{n_1^2-n_2^2}\). Then \(u^2+w^2=V^2\) and \(u\tan u=w\). The first even solution exists for any positive \(V\) and becomes weakly confined as \(V\) tends to zero. Odd solutions instead obey \(-u\cot u=w\), with the first appearing above \(V=\pi/2\).

For \(\lambda_0=1.00\) μm, \(n_1=1.50\) and \(n_2=1.40\), choose \(a=0.328\) μm. This gives \(V\simeq1.11\), for which \(u=w\simeq\pi/4\) satisfies both equations. The effective index is \(\beta/k_0=\sqrt{n_2^2+(w/ak_0)^2}=1.451\), between the two material indices. The cladding amplitude decays over \(a/w=0.418\) μm. This mode can have a fixed width along a uniform guide. An arbitrarily shaped input can excite radiation or several modes and need not retain its initial profile.

Practise on sheet 6 · Choose a slab width for one transverse TE mode. Further reading · Massachusetts Institute of Technology · Haus and Melcher, Electromagnetic Fields and Energy · §13.5 Dielectric Waveguides: Optical Fibers, Figures 13.5.1–13.5.4.

A focused spot with finite depth

A collimated ideal Gaussian at 633 nm has \(1/e^2\) intensity radius \(w_{\rm in}=1.00\) mm at a lens of focal length 100 mm. Assume negligible clipping and a nearly flat incident wavefront. The lens converts input radius into convergence angle \(w_{\rm in}/f=0.0100\) rad. Equating this angle to the far-field Gaussian divergence gives \(w_0\simeq\lambda f/(\pi w_{\rm in})=20.1\) μm. The focused Rayleigh range is \(2.01\) mm. At axial displacement 4.02 mm the spot radius grows to about \(45.0\) μm and the axial intensity drops to a fifth.

Increasing the input radius by a factor of two halves the waist while reducing Rayleigh range by four. This trade matters when illuminating a sample extending several millimetres along the beam. A hard aperture that clips substantial power reshapes the angular spectrum, so this simple product no longer predicts the entire spot. A measured waist and divergence with product exceeding \(\lambda/\pi\) can also signal imperfect mode quality. Radius measurements must use the same intensity convention before making that comparison.

Two radius measurements equidistant from a proposed waist should give the same result for an ideal beam. If they do not, first refine the estimate of waist position. Multiple axial samples can be fitted to \(w^2(z)=w_0^2[1+(z-z_0)^2/z_R^2]\) to infer the waist plane and divergence simultaneously. Power falling through an aperture, however, also depends on the beam centre. A beam walking sideways past a fixed detector can imitate a growing radius unless beam position is measured independently. Recording transverse profiles instead of only total collected power makes this distinction observable.

Propagate a Gaussian that already has curved wavefronts

An input to a lens is often converging or diverging before reaching the lens. Its radius alone then cannot determine the new waist. Combine wavefront curvature and intensity radius in a complex beam parameter \(q\) defined by \(1/q=1/R-i\lambda/(\pi w^2)\). In homogeneous space the solution to the paraxial wave equation has \(q=z-z_0+i z_R\), where \(z_0\) is the waist plane and \(z_R>0\) the Rayleigh range. Straight propagation through distance \(d\) therefore adds \(d\) to \(q\).

A thin positive lens multiplies the field by a quadratic phase that changes curvature according to \(1/R_{\rm out}=1/R_{\rm in}-1/f\) and leaves transverse intensity unchanged at the lens. Combining its two effects in the definition gives

\[\frac1{q_{\rm out}}=\frac1{q_{\rm in}}-\frac1f .\]

More generally free propagation and lenses act through the same real ray matrices used for first-order geometrical optics. Composing their actions on \(q\) gives

\[q_{\rm new}=(Aq+B)/(Cq+D).\]

To locate a subsequent waist in free space, propagate until the real part of \(q\) is zero. Its positive imaginary part then supplies \(z_R\), hence the new waist radius. This construction retains finite diffraction and avoids predicting a singular geometric focus.

Reconstruct a focus downstream of a displaced lens

An ideal beam of wavelength 633 nm has an initial Rayleigh range 100 mm. Its input waist is 50.0 mm upstream of a lens of focal length 100 mm. At the lens \(q_{\rm in}=50.0+i100\) mm, so \(1/q_{\rm in}=0.00400-i0.00800\) mm⁻¹. Subtracting \(1/f=0.0100\) mm⁻¹ gives \(1/q_{\rm out}=-0.00600-i0.00800\) mm⁻¹. On inversion \(q_{\rm out}=-60.0+i80.0\) mm. The waist is 60.0 mm after the lens and has Rayleigh range 80.0 mm.

At this new waist \(w_0=\sqrt{\lambda z_R/\pi}=0.127\) mm. At the lens the outgoing parameter predicts \(w=w_0\sqrt{1+(60/80)^2}=0.159\) mm, the same incident radius obtained from the original 100 mm Rayleigh range and 50 mm propagation. This consistency check tests both the signs and the continuity of intensity. Treating the incident beam as fully collimated would place the waist close to the focal plane and miss its measured position by tens of millimetres. In the limit \(f\to\infty\) the reciprocal correction vanishes and the original waist and curvature are recovered. A measured beam profile at several axial planes can distinguish this finite-curvature effect from a simple error in the stated lens focal length.

The complex parameter can be recovered experimentally from intensity alone if enough axial profiles are recorded. Fit squared radius to a quadratic in position, locate its minimum to obtain the waist plane, and infer Rayleigh range from how rapidly that squared radius increases on either side. A separately measured wavelength then predicts the relation between fitted waist radius and divergence for a fundamental Gaussian. If the fitted values violate that relation beyond uncertainty, the more likely problems include a higher-order mode contribution, clipping or an inconsistent radius definition. Simply adjusting the lens focal length cannot repair all three discrepancies at once.

A temporal imaging system can stretch the time separation of two optical features without requiring them to arrive more slowly at the source. Chapter 12 · Measuring a star with interference

Infer angular diameter or binary separation from the spatial coherence of light at two separated collectors.

From first principles

Connect a projected baseline to source coherence

Take two collectors observing a distant thermal source in a narrow wavelength band. Let \(\theta\) be the small angular offset of one source element along the projected baseline of length \(B\). Its geometric path difference between collectors, after removing the delay to the field centre, is approximately \(B\theta\). At wavelength \(\lambda\) this produces the phase difference

\[\phi(\theta)=2\pi B\theta/\lambda .\]

Different source elements fluctuate independently, so their correlations add with weights proportional to brightness. If \(S(\theta)\) is brightness integrated in the perpendicular angular direction, dividing by total brightness gives normalised complex coherence.

\[\gamma(B)=\frac{\int S(\theta)e^{i2\pi B\theta/\lambda}\,d\theta}{\int S(\theta)\,d\theta}.\]

For two equally bright point sources at projected angles \(\pm s/2\), this sum has just two phasors. Their opposite phases make the normalised visibility real.

\[\gamma(B)=\tfrac12(e^{i\pi Bs/\lambda}+e^{-i\pi Bs/\lambda})=\cos(\pi Bs/\lambda).\]

The first contrast zero occurs at \(B=\lambda/(2s)\) for equal arm intensities. Rotating the projected baseline changes the separation component being measured. A source extended perpendicular to one baseline can therefore look unresolved there and extended on another.

Derivation and applications

Infer the orientation-dependent size of a Gaussian source

A star surrounded by elongated emission can have a visibility that decays without crossing zero. Model its sky brightness as an elliptical Gaussian with full widths at half maximum \(\Theta_x,\Theta_y\) along known perpendicular sky axes. Assume spatially incoherent emission, a narrow spectral channel of wavelength \(\lambda\), small angles in radians and far-field collectors. At projected baseline components \((B_x,B_y)\), the normalised visibility is the two-dimensional Fourier transform of the brightness. The Gaussian factors separate along the two axes.

\[S(x,y)=S_0\exp\!\left[-4\ln2\left(\frac{x^2}{\Theta_x^2}+\frac{y^2}{\Theta_y^2}\right)\right],\]
\[u=\frac{B_x}{\lambda},\]
\[v=\frac{B_y}{\lambda}.\]

For each axis complete the square in the Fourier integral. Dividing by the integral at zero baseline cancels the normalisation of the Gaussian. Taking the product of the two factors then gives a positive real visibility for a source centred at the coordinate origin.

\[\frac{\int e^{-ax^2}e^{2\pi iux}\,dx}{\int e^{-ax^2}\,dx}=e^{-\pi^2u^2/a},\]
\[|\gamma|=\exp\!\left[-\frac{\pi^2(\Theta_x^2B_x^2+\Theta_y^2B_y^2)}{4\ln2\,\lambda^2}\right].\]

For a baseline of length \(B\) at angle \(\psi\) to the \(x\) axis, define the projected squared width by weighting the two squared widths by the directional cosines squared. Solving for the baseline that halves the visibility gives

\[\Theta_{\rm proj}^2=\Theta_x^2\cos^2\psi+\Theta_y^2\sin^2\psi,\]
\[B_{1/2}=\frac{2\ln2}{\pi}\frac{\lambda}{\Theta_{\rm proj}}.\]

A larger projected source loses fringe contrast at a shorter baseline. The half point remains available as an observable even though an ideal Gaussian has no exact visibility zero at finite baseline.

Worked example

Read two angular widths from a rotating baseline

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

At 1.60 µm, calibrated visibility reaches 0.500 at 40.0 m along one sky axis and at 80.0 m along the perpendicular axis. The widths are \(0.4413(1.60\times10^{-6})/40.0=1.77\times10^{-8}\) rad and half that value. Multiplication by \(2.063\times10^8\) milliarcseconds per radian gives 3.64 and 1.82 milliarcseconds. The longer half-visibility baseline corresponds to the narrower source axis.

Second application

Predict a diagonal baseline reading

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

For another source, independently measured half-visibility baselines are 30.0 m and 60.0 m on the principal axes in a single spectral channel. At 45.0° the reciprocal squared half-baseline is \((1/30^2+1/60^2)/2\), giving 37.9 m. For a 60.0 m diagonal baseline the predicted visibility is

\[2^{-(60.0/37.9)^2}=0.177.\]

This diagonal reading tests the elliptical model against information beyond the two fitted widths.

Try it · Stellar ellipse

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

When two copies cease to interfere

A narrow-band point source sends essentially the same waveform to two distant collectors after the proper path delay. Two extended sources can light both collectors equally while producing different fringe patterns. The measurable quantity is the complex degree of coherence \(\gamma\), normalized by the geometric mean of the two intensities. If the two collected beams have equal intensities, controlled recombination with variable phase produces fringe visibility \(|\gamma|\). Unequal intensities reduce visibility by an additional known balance factor.

Consider collectors separated by projected baseline \(B\) transverse to their line of sight. A source element at a small angular coordinate \(\theta\) along the baseline has geometric delay \(B\theta/c\) and phase \(2\pi B\theta/\lambda\). Different surface elements of a thermal source radiate independently on average, so cross terms between distinct elements disappear. Each element still interferes with itself through the two collectors. Adding their complex interference contributions, weighted by angular intensity \(S\), gives

\[\gamma(B)=\frac{\int S(\theta)e^{2\pi iB\theta/\lambda}\,d\theta}{\int S(\theta)\,d\theta}.\]

Here \(S\) is angular brightness integrated perpendicular to the baseline. Successive baselines sample different Fourier components of the projected brightness profile. The narrow-band, far-field conditions let one use a single wavelength and a planar angular phase across the entire source.

Two stars and a disk

For equally bright unresolved points at angles \(\pm s/2\), the normalized sum is \(\gamma=\cos(\pi Bs/\lambda)\). Its first zero occurs at \(B=\lambda/(2s)\). Rotating the baseline changes the projected separation and can restore visibility. If one point is brighter, the unequal phasor lengths cannot cancel completely. For a uniform circular disk of angular diameter \(\Theta\) the two-dimensional brightness integral instead gives \(2J_1(x)/x\) with \(x=\pi B\Theta/\lambda\). The first zero occurs at \(B=1.22\lambda/\Theta\). The same Bessel function appeared for a filled circular aperture, now describing coherence as a function of collector separation.

Interpret a visibility measurement

At 550 nm a uniform disk first loses its fringes at projected baseline 50.0 m. The inferred diameter is \(1.22(550\times10^{-9})/50.0=1.34\times10^{-8}\) rad or 2.77 milliarcseconds. At distance 10.0 parsecs its linear diameter would be \(0.0277\) astronomical units, about \(4.14\times10^9\) m. If independent spectroscopy suggested a binary made of two unresolved equals instead, the same null would correspond to projected separation 1.13 milliarcseconds. One zero by itself does not determine which brightness distribution produced it.

Measurements at several baseline lengths distinguish the cosine from the disk transform, and observations after rotation examine orientation. A broad optical passband averages different \(\lambda\) values and can fill a sharp null. In addition, uncontrolled fluctuations of the optical path during an exposure reduce contrast even for a point source. Observing a nearby unresolved calibrator at the same instrumental setting separates that loss from the target’s physical angular extent.

The measured baseline is a projection perpendicular to the line of sight. Earth’s rotation changes both its effective length and orientation for a fixed pair of collectors, providing additional Fourier samples over a night.

Remove telescope-dependent phase errors with three collectors

Two telescopes measure a complex visibility: its magnitude describes contrast while its argument contains the position of brightness features. Atmospheric delay at each telescope can overwhelm that phase. Use three telescopes and orient the measured baselines cyclically from 1 to 2, 2 to 3 and 3 to 1. If the instantaneous phase delay at telescope \(j\) is \(\phi_j\), measured visibility on baseline \(ij\) is \(V_{ij}^{\rm obs}=V_{ij}e^{i(\phi_i-\phi_j)}\). Multiply the three measured visibilities. Every telescope phase then appears once with each sign and cancels. The argument of the product,

\[\Phi_{\rm cl}=\arg[V_{12}V_{23}V_{31}],\]

is called the closure phase. The three oriented baseline vectors sum to zero. Translating the entire source on the sky introduces a linear phase proportional to baseline, which cancels in the same product. Closure phase thus probes relative brightness structure while losing sensitivity to an absolute position offset. A brightness distribution symmetric about a point has real visibilities after such a translation; its closure phase is zero or \(\pi\) away from zeros of visibility.

Cancellation requires phases measured over the same atmospheric realization or combined in a suitable short-time correlation. A separate instrumental phase error specific to one baseline is not expressible as a difference of telescope phases and will remain in the product. Observing a point-source calibrator can detect such a residual.

Detect asymmetry in an unequal binary

Place a star of unit brightness at the origin and a companion of relative brightness one half at angular separation \(s\) along a line of projected baselines. Adopt the Fourier sign convention yielding \(V(\phi)=(1+0.5e^{i\phi})/1.5\) with \(\phi=2\pi Bs/\lambda\). Choose collectors so that the first two oriented baselines each give phase \(\pi/2\). The closing baseline then gives \(-\pi\). Their complex visibilities are \((1+0.5i)/1.5\), \((1+0.5i)/1.5\) and \((1-0.5)/1.5=1/3\).

Only the first two terms contribute nonzero phase. Their summed argument is \(2\arctan(0.5)=53.1^\circ\). Reflect the binary about the origin and all visibility phases reverse, giving \(-53.1^\circ\) while every two-telescope visibility magnitude is unchanged. At 600 nm and projected separation 3.00 milliarcseconds, the first two projected baseline lengths are

\[B=\lambda/(4s)=10.3\,\mathrm{m}.\]

For a vanishingly faint companion each visibility tends to one and closure phase tends to zero. Combining several independent triangles with magnitude observations can therefore distinguish asymmetric brightness distributions that reproduce an identical contrast on a single baseline.

The chosen one-dimensional triangle can be realized with three equally spaced collectors. Its closing baseline then has twice the magnitude of either short baseline and points in the opposite direction under cyclic orientation. In a general two-dimensional array the vectors still form a closed triangle, even though their lengths no longer have this simple ratio. Reversing every baseline convention conjugates the triple product and reverses the reported phase. Astrometric orientation and instrument phase conventions must therefore be calibrated before interpreting a phase sign as the direction of a companion on the sky.

Infer an angular diameter from a visibility null

For a monochromatic, uniformly bright circular disk the normalised mutual coherence is \(2J_1(s)/s\),

\[s=\pi B\theta/\lambda.\]

A two-aperture instrument measuring contrast sees its magnitude. Seek a minimum across a range of baselines.

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Problem sheet 6 · Beam transport and spatial coherence

Estimate a focus, diagnose beam quality and determine what a partially resolved binary can tell an interferometer.

Set the depth of focus

An ideal 532 nm Gaussian should maintain at least half its peak axial intensity throughout a 4.00 mm sample centred on the waist. Find the smallest allowed waist radius and the required collimated input radius for a 75.0 mm lens forming that waist.

Show the Gaussian design

The half-intensity planes lie at \(\pm z_R\), so require \(z_R\ge2.00\) mm. The smallest waist is \(\sqrt{\lambda z_R/\pi}=18.4\) μm. For a flat incoming wavefront, \(w_{\rm in}\simeq\lambda f/(\pi w_0)=0.690\) mm. A smaller focus would raise central intensity at fixed power while decreasing the end-to-centre ratio below the required half.

Compare a beam with the fundamental mode

At 633 nm a beam has waist radius 40.0 μm and far-field half-angle 7.50 mrad. Find the product relative to the ideal Gaussian value. Under the convention \(M^2=\pi w_0\theta/\lambda\), estimate the minimum collimated input radius to make a 25.0 μm waist with a 100 mm lens.

Show the quality and focusing estimates

The measured product is \(3.00\times10^{-7}\) m rad and the ideal value is \(\lambda/\pi=2.01\times10^{-7}\) m rad. Their ratio is \(M^2=1.49\). In the paraxial, approximately Gaussian beam-quality model \(\theta=M^2\lambda/(\pi w_0)\), giving \(w_{\rm in}\simeq f\theta=1.20\) mm for 25.0 μm. This comparison needs consistent radius and divergence definitions, and a non-clipping lens.

A binary with unequal brightness

Two unresolved stars have flux ratio \(3:1\) and projected angular separation \(4.00\) milliarcseconds. Derive the minimum visibility and find its first baseline at 600 nm. What happens if the baseline rotates perpendicular to their separation?

Show the phasor addition

Normalize one flux to 3 and the other to 1. Their complex degree of coherence has magnitude \(|3+e^{i\phi}|/4\), with \(\phi=2\pi Bs/\lambda\). Its first minimum occurs at \(\phi=\pi\) and equals

\[(3-1)/4=0.50.\]

The separation is \(4.00\times10^{-3}/206265=1.94\times10^{-8}\) rad, giving \(B=\lambda/(2s)=15.5\) m. A perpendicular baseline gives zero projected separation, equal phases and unit intrinsic visibility if individual stellar disks remain unresolved.

Can a camera record what its objective resolves?

A 550 nm incoherent image is formed in air by an objective of numerical aperture 0.25 and lateral magnification 20. The detector pitch is 6.5 µm. Compare the Rayleigh separation, the object-side incoherent cutoff and the detector Nyquist limit. Predict the apparent object frequency if a 0.85 cycle/µm sinusoidal target is sampled without prefiltering.

Worked solution

The Rayleigh distance for two point emitters is 0.61λ/NA = 1.34 µm. For a sinusoidal intensity pattern the relevant quantity is the incoherent cutoff 2NA/λ = 0.909 cycle/µm. These refer to different targets. Taking the reciprocal of the Rayleigh distance would give the wrong cutoff.

At the object plane each detector pitch corresponds to 6.5/20 = 0.325 µm. Its Nyquist frequency is 1/(2 × 0.325) = 1.54 cycles/µm, above the optical cutoff. The 0.85 cycle/µm target remains at 0.85 cycle/µm. The pupil transfers it weakly because it is close to cutoff. A finite pixel further reduces its contrast by averaging.

Now reduce the magnification to 10. The effective pitch becomes 0.65 µm, with sampling frequency 1.538 cycles/µm. Since 0.85 exceeds half this frequency, the sampled pattern aliases to |0.85 − 1.538| = 0.688 cycle/µm. The optics are unchanged. Choosing M ≥ 2 × 6.5 × 0.909 ≈ 11.8 places even the optical cutoff within the detector passband.

Choose a slab width for one transverse TE mode

At 1.55 μm use \(n_1=1.50\) and \(n_2=1.45\). Find the full slab thickness at the first odd-mode cutoff. Classify full thicknesses 1.60 and 2.20 μm for TE polarization. What changes just above cutoff?

Use the half-thickness convention consistently

With full thickness \(d=2a\),

\[V=\pi d\sqrt{n_1^2-n_2^2}/\lambda_0.\]

The odd threshold \(V=\pi/2\) gives \(d_c=\lambda_0/[2\sqrt{1.50^2-1.45^2}]=2.02\) μm. The 1.60 μm slab supports only the fundamental TE mode, while 2.20 μm also supports the first odd TE mode. Just above threshold its cladding decay constant is small and its field extends far outside the core. The count here excludes TM polarization.

Chapter 13 · Reflection, polarisation and the field beyond a reflecting surface

A ray in glass can reflect completely at an air boundary. A second piece of glass brought close to that boundary can nevertheless change the reflection. The field outside the glass follows from the same equations that determine the reflected intensity.

Match the waves where the materials meet

Put the interface at \(z=0\), with transparent nonmagnetic media of refractive indices \(n_1,n_2\). Write the incoming wave as \(e^{i(k_xx+k_zz-\omega t)}\). To satisfy Maxwell's boundary conditions at every point on the surface, the incident, reflected and transmitted waves need the same \(k_x\) and \(\omega\). With \(k_0=\omega/c\) this gives

\[n_1\sin\theta=n_2\sin\theta_t,\]

where angles are measured from the normal. The normal component may differ on either side.

For s polarisation the electric field points along the interface perpendicular to the incidence plane. Continuity of tangential electric and magnetic fields gives

\[1+r_s=t_s,\]
\[n_1\cos\theta(1-r_s)=n_2\cos\theta_t\,t_s.\]

The minus sign on the reflected magnetic term follows from reversing the normal wavevector. Elimination of \(t_s\) gives the first expression below. For p polarisation it is the full magnetic field that points perpendicular to the incidence plane. Resolving the electric field into tangential components supplies the interchanged index weights in the second expression. A reversal of the reflected p basis reverses the sign of \(r_p\) without changing the measurable reflectance.

\[r_s=\frac{n_1\cos\theta-n_2\cos\theta_t}{n_1\cos\theta+n_2\cos\theta_t},\]
\[r_p=\frac{n_2\cos\theta-n_1\cos\theta_t}{n_2\cos\theta+n_1\cos\theta_t},\]
\[R_{s,p}=|r_{s,p}|^2.\]

At normal incidence glass of index 1.50 against air reflects \([(1.50-1)/(1.50+1)]^2=0.040\) for either polarisation. Setting the p numerator to zero and using Snell's law gives \(\tan\theta_B=n_2/n_1\), here \(33.69^\circ\). Only the p reflection vanishes there. At higher angles both reflected intensities approach one.

Continue the transmitted wave through the critical angle

The transmitted normal wavenumber satisfies

\[k_{z,2}^2=k_0^2(n_2^2-n_1^2\sin^2\theta).\]

Beyond \(\theta_c=\arcsin(n_2/n_1)=41.81^\circ\) it becomes imaginary. Choose \(k_{z,2}=+i\kappa\), the root giving a field that decays into the upper half space. The tangential phase still propagates. In the s Fresnel amplitude the denominator and numerator are now complex conjugates. Its modulus is exactly one, but its phase depends on angle. The p wave also has unit reflectance with its own phase.

\[\frac{1}{\kappa}=\frac{\lambda_0}{2\pi\sqrt{n_1^2\sin^2\theta-n_2^2}}.\]

For free-space wavelength \(500\,\mathrm{nm}\) at \(50^\circ\), the electric-field amplitude decays by \(e^{-1}\) over approximately 141 nm. Its squared magnitude falls by the same factor in just 70 nm. A semi-infinite, lossless second medium carries no time-averaged normal flux in this regime. A nearby second boundary changes the solution by introducing another evanescent component, enabling optical tunnelling.

Locate the two special angles

Compare s and p reflectances as the glass index changes. Read the predicted critical and Brewster angles before moving the angle slider. Near the critical angle an ideal plane wave has a very long penetration length. A finite beam has an angular spread, so its measured response mixes nearby incidence angles.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a liquid cladding

Replace air with a liquid of index 1.33. For glass of index 1.50 determine whether the same \(50^\circ\) beam still undergoes total internal reflection. At what angle does the p reflection vanish?

Work through the solution

The critical angle increases to \(\arcsin(1.33/1.50)=62.46^\circ\). At \(50^\circ\) a propagating wave now enters the liquid. Its p-polarised reflected amplitude is zero at \(\arctan(1.33/1.50)=41.56^\circ\). The sensitivity of the critical angle to the surrounding medium underlies a refractometer.

At \(50^\circ\) and free-space wavelength 500 nm, a dilute, weakly responding layer samples intensity 70 nm above the glass–air interface. Estimate the local intensity relative to its value immediately outside the glass.

Work through the solution

The field penetration length is \(140.6\) nm. Squaring the decay factor gives

\[\exp[-2(70)/140.6]=0.369.\]

This ratio concerns local squared field; it does not give the net normal power crossing a lossless interface. A strongly perturbing layer would require recomputing the field.

Estimate the critical-angle shift for a small change of ambient refractive index near air. Use fixed glass index 1.50 and an increase \(\delta n_2=0.01\).

Work through the solution

Differentiating \(\arcsin(n_2/n_1)\) gives \(d\theta_c/dn_2=1/\sqrt{n_1^2-n_2^2}\) in radians. Near air the predicted shift is \(0.00894\) rad or \(0.512^\circ\). This local linear estimate becomes less accurate as the indices approach matching.

Chapter 14 · How a dielectric slab chooses its guided modes

A plane interface supports a decaying field outside glass under total reflection. Put two interfaces around a thin core and a new question appears. Which transverse patterns reproduce themselves as the wave propagates along the guide?

Separate longitudinal and transverse variation

Take a uniform slab of half-width \(a\), core index \(n_c\), and equal claddings of lower index \(n_s\). A transverse-electric mode can be written \(E_y=F(x)e^{i(\beta z-\omega t)}\). Substitution into the scalar Helmholtz equation in each homogeneous region leaves \(F''+(n^2k_0^2-\beta^2)F=0\). A guided wave must oscillate in the core and decay outside. Those requirements give the interval \(n_sk_0<\beta<n_ck_0\). Equivalently the effective phase index \(\beta/k_0\) lies between the material indices.

Introduce \(u=a\sqrt{n_c^2 k_0^2-\beta^2}\) and \(w=a\sqrt{\beta^2-n_s^2 k_0^2}\). Adding their squares eliminates the unknown \(\beta\). The possible pairs lie on a circle of radius \(V=a k_0\sqrt{n_c^2-n_s^2}\). Here \(a\) always denotes the half-width. Using the full thickness in \(V\) would move every numerical cutoff by a factor of two.

Enforce continuity at the core boundary

Choose the even fundamental mode with \(F(0)=1\). The core solution is \(\cos(ux/a)\). In the upper cladding write \(F=\cos u\,e^{-w(x-a)/a}\), which has the same value at \(x=a\) and is bounded at infinity. Nonmagnetic media require both \(E_y\) and the tangential magnetic field, proportional to \(dF/dx\), to be continuous. Equating the two derivatives gives the mode equation

\[u\tan u=w=\sqrt{V^2-u^2},\qquad 0<u<\min(V,\pi/2).\]

On this interval the left side increases from zero, and the right side decreases. At the far end either the tangent diverges or the square root reaches zero. There is exactly one root, however thin the symmetric core. For the first odd mode, the core uses \(\sin(ux/a)\), and the derivative condition instead gives

\[-u\cot u=w.\]

Its first branch starts at \(u=\pi/2\). Consequently \(V>\pi/2\) is needed for that mode to become bound.

Resolve the tail with a root solver

At \(V=1\), bisection gives \(u=0.7391\), \(w=0.6736\). Substitution yields the same number on the two sides of the mode equation. The amplitude tail beyond each boundary extends over \(a/w=1.48a\) before dropping by a factor \(e\). At \(V=2\), the fundamental has \(u=1.0299,w=1.7145\), so the decay length falls to \(0.583a\). The first odd mode can now also exist. A guided mode may therefore spread substantially beyond the nominal guide material.

Vary \(V\) while the graph keeps the same half-width units. The display solves the transcendental equation afresh, using its monotonic branch to bracket the root. Compare the apparent width at half the field amplitude with the decay length reported in the readout. The former includes part of the oscillatory core, the latter describes only the tail. A detector measuring local intensity responds to \(|F|^2\), which has twice the logarithmic decay slope.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a fabrication tolerance

At a free-space wavelength of \(1.55\,\mu\mathrm m\), take \(n_c=1.50\) and \(n_s=1.45\). What is the full thickness at the first odd TE cutoff? How would decreasing the wavelength affect the number of modes?

Work through the solution

At cutoff set \(ak_0\sqrt{n_c^2-n_s^2}=\pi/2\). The full thickness is \(2a=\lambda_0/[2\sqrt{n_c^2-n_s^2}]\simeq2.02\,\mu\mathrm m\). At fixed geometry and approximately fixed indices, decreasing the wavelength increases \(V\). Additional oscillatory branches can then intersect the available circle and become guided.

Express the squared effective index in terms of \(w/V\). Then estimate it at \(V=1\), \(n_c=1.50,n_s=1.45\).

Work through the solution

From the definitions \(w^2/V^2=(\beta^2/k_0^2-n_s^2)/(n_c^2-n_s^2)\). With \(w=0.6736\), the normalised squared-index fraction is 0.454. Substitution gives

\[n_{\rm eff}=\sqrt{1.45^2+0.454(1.50^2-1.45^2)}=1.473.\]

This is a phase index obtained with the mode solution.

How does the fundamental cladding tail scale in a very thin symmetric guide?

Work through the solution

At small \(V\), \(u\) is small and \(u\tan u\simeq u^2\), hence \(w\simeq u^2\). The constraint \(u^2+w^2=V^2\) then gives

\[u\simeq V,\]
\[w\simeq V^2.\]

The field decay length in units of the core half-width grows as \(V^{-2}\), so the mode exists mathematically while occupying mostly cladding.

Further reading · MIT slab dielectric waveguides, TE modes and confinement.

Chapter 15 · Why opening a circular aperture can darken its centre

The on-axis intensity behind a round opening may decrease when the opening grows. Following the phase contributed by each annulus gives a quantitative prediction for this near-field diffraction effect.

Add the complex contributions from the pupil

Illuminate a circular hole of radius \(a\) in a thin opaque screen with a uniform monochromatic plane wave. Observe at distance \(z\) on the symmetry axis. An annulus at radius \(r\) has area \(2\pi r\,dr\). Its path to the detector is \(\sqrt{z^2+r^2}=z+r^2/(2z)-r^4/(8z^3)+\cdots\). When \(a/z\) is small, slowly varying amplitude and inclination factors can be taken at the centre. Retain the quadratic phase but demand that the discarded quartic phase \(k a^4/(8z^3)\) be small. The quadratic phase itself can span several cycles.

With the Fresnel propagator \(e^{ikz}/(i\lambda z)\), the field relative to an unobstructed plane wave at the same point is therefore an elementary integral. Use \(v=r^2\), so \(2r\,dr=dv\). The increasing annular area exactly compensates the decreasing radial width of successive equal-phase intervals.

\[\frac{U}{U_{\rm free}}=\frac{2\pi}{i\lambda z}\int_0^a r\,e^{i\pi r^2/(\lambda z)}\,dr=1-e^{i\pi N},\]
\[N=\frac{a^2}{\lambda z}.\]

Taking the squared magnitude gives

\[I/I_{\rm free}=2-2\cos(\pi N)=4\sin^2(\pi N/2).\]

The phase convention can reverse the exponent while leaving the intensity unchanged. Odd integer \(N\) gives intensity four times the unobstructed intensity on axis. Even integer \(N\) gives an ideal zero. A local enhancement is compatible with energy conservation because interference redistributes intensity across the observation plane.

Divide the opening into half-period zones

Boundaries \(r_j=\sqrt{j\lambda z}\) separate annuli whose outer and inner points differ by phase \(\pi\). All complete zones have area \(\pi\lambda z\) and alternating complex contributions under this approximation. Opening one complete zone gives a bright axial spot. Opening its neighbour provides the opposite amplitude. If a small detector instead averages over off-axis positions, the phases no longer have perfect azimuthal symmetry and the measured minimum fills in.

For \(\lambda=500\,\mathrm{nm}\) and \(z=0.50\,\mathrm m\), the first boundary has radius \(0.500\,\mathrm{mm}\). The next has radius \(0.707\,\mathrm{mm}\). At fixed opening \(a=0.500\,\mathrm{mm}\) decreasing the screen distance to \(0.25\,\mathrm m\) also changes \(N\) from one to two. Thus an axial scan with a fixed pupil tests the same interference argument as changing the aperture. The small ratio \(a/z\) at these distances makes the approximations internally consistent.

Compare amplitude addition with area scaling

At small \(N\), expand the exponential to find \(U/U_{\rm free}\simeq-i\pi N\), hence intensity grows as \(N^2\) or \(a^4\). This follows because all small-pupil contributions arrive nearly in phase. The small-opening expansion fails long before the paraxial integral does. Vary the radius and distance below. Compare the position of the first minimum with the radius at the first maximum. The display evaluates intensity as a function of physical radius so that the square-root spacing becomes visible. Real propagation includes the pupil transmission profile, spectral bandwidth and detector size.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a partially blocked zone

Keep only the annulus between the first and second half-period boundaries. What intensity would it produce on axis by itself? What if that annulus and the central disc both transmit, but the annulus transmits only half the electric-field amplitude?

Work through the solution

At \(N=1\) the central disc contributes \(2U_{\rm free}\). At \(N=2\) the total contribution vanishes. The annulus therefore supplies \(-2U_{\rm free}\) and by itself has normalised intensity four. With amplitude transmission one half its contribution is \(-U_{\rm free}\). Adding the disc gives \(U_{\rm free}\), or normalised intensity one.

Behind a \(0.50\) mm opening at 500 nm, find the first three on-axis maxima as the detector approaches from large positive \(z\).

Work through the solution

Maxima occur at odd \(N=1,3,5\). Since \(a^2/\lambda=0.50\,\mathrm m\), the distances are 0.50, 0.1667 and 0.100 m. The spacings shrink toward the aperture because the controlled phase is proportional to \(1/z\). Continuing the sequence requires checking the neglected quartic phase.

At the \(N=2\) null, increase the aperture radius by 1 percent with everything else fixed. Estimate the residual relative intensity from the leading nonzero term.

Work through the solution

The new Fresnel number is

\[2(1.01)^2=2.0402.\]

Near an even integer, \(4\sin^2(\pi\delta N/2)\simeq\pi^2(\delta N)^2\), giving 0.0160. The first derivative of intensity vanishes at the ideal null but a small geometric error still creates a measurable finite signal.

Further reading · Delft Interactive Optics, Fresnel propagation and paraxial approximation.

Chapter 16 · When do two curved mirrors support a confined beam?

A ray launched slightly away from the axis may stay near it for many round trips or walk off a mirror. A single round-trip matrix predicts which behaviour occurs and selects the Gaussian beam that can reproduce its wavefront.

Multiply one complete round trip

Use a paraxial ray vector \((x,\theta)\), with \(\theta=dx/dz\). Free propagation through distance \(L\) changes \(x\) by \(L\theta\), leaving \(\theta\) fixed. In unfolded coordinates reflection from a concave spherical mirror of radius \(R>0\) changes \(\theta\) by \(-2x/R\). The factor two arises from the change in direction of the local surface normal. Represent propagation and reflection by

\[P=\begin{pmatrix}1&L\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad M=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\-2/R&1\end{pmatrix}.\]

For two identical mirrors a round trip is \(C=(MP)^2\) starting just after a reflection. Each constituent has determinant one. The half-trip has trace \(2-2L/R=2g\), so the identity \(\operatorname{tr}(A^2)=(\operatorname{tr}A)^2-2\det A\) gives

\[\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}C=2g^2-1.\]

Its eigenvalues are reciprocal. If their trace lies strictly between −2 and +2 they are complex conjugates of modulus one and repeated round trips remain bounded. If the absolute trace exceeds two, a component grows exponentially. This produces the usual symmetric resonator range \(0<L<2R\). The exact confocal point \(L=R\) has \(C=-I\) and bounded rays despite the repeated eigenvalue. Real alignment and apertures also influence losses.

Match the field curvature to the mirrors

For a symmetric cavity a fundamental Gaussian has its waist at the midpoint. At distance \(z\) from that waist its wavefront radius is \(R_w(z)=z+z_R^2/z\), where \(z_R=\pi w_0^2/\lambda\). At the mirror \(z=L/2\), a reproducing wave must have the spherical mirror curvature. Equating \(R_w(L/2)\) to \(R\) gives

\[z_R^2=\frac L2\left(R-\frac L2\right),\]
\[w_0=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda z_R}{\pi}},\]
\[w_m=w_0\sqrt{1+(L/2z_R)^2}.\]

The same interval emerges from requiring a positive squared Rayleigh length. The radius \(w\) is the distance at which intensity has fallen to \(e^{-2}\) of the on-axis value. For \(R=0.50\,\mathrm m\), \(L=0.50\,\mathrm m\) and \(\lambda=600\,\mathrm{nm}\), \(z_R=0.25\,\mathrm m\), \(w_0=0.219\,\mathrm{mm}\) and \(w_m=0.309\,\mathrm{mm}\). These scale as the square root of the wavelength at fixed geometry.

Move towards the concentric limit

Keep the same mirror radius and increase the separation to \(0.90\,\mathrm m\). The Rayleigh length becomes \(0.150\,\mathrm m\), the waist contracts to \(0.169\,\mathrm{mm}\), and the mirror spot grows to \(0.535\,\mathrm{mm}\). A smaller focus can therefore require a larger mirror aperture. The ideal spherical paraxial solution ceases to describe an actual beam if diffraction clipping or large ray angles become important.

Change \(L/R\) and inspect the beam radius across one pass. Compare settings equidistant from the confocal ratio, such as 0.4 and 1.6. Their waist radii agree because \(L(2R-L)\) is symmetric. Their mirror spots differ because the distances from the waist differ.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on an aperture measurement

A symmetric air cavity has \(L=0.40\,\mathrm m\) and measured \(w_m/w_0=\sqrt2\). Find \(R\) and, at \(\lambda=632.8\,\mathrm{nm}\), the waist radius.

Work through the solution

The width ratio gives \(L/(2z_R)=1\), hence \(z_R=0.20\,\mathrm m\). The curvature there is

\[R=0.20+0.20^2/0.20=0.40\,\mathrm m.\]

The cavity is confocal, and \(w_0=\sqrt{632.8\times10^{-9}\times0.20/\pi}=0.201\,\mathrm{mm}\).

At fixed curvature radius \(R\) and wavelength, which symmetric separation maximises the waist radius? Find that maximum and check it against the confocal example.

Work through the solution

Maximising \(w_0\) is equivalent to maximising \(z_R^2=L(2R-L)/4\). Differentiation sets \(L=R\), with \(z_R=R/2\). Thus \(w_{0,\max}=\sqrt{\lambda R/(2\pi)}=0.219\,\mathrm{mm}\) for the parameters in the chapter. Near either end of the ideal stability window the waist shrinks instead.

A ray has \(x=0.1\,\mathrm{mm}\), \(\theta=1\,\mathrm{mrad}\) just after a reflection in an exactly confocal cavity. Where is the ray in the same section after one and after two round trips?

Work through the solution

At \(L=R\) the round-trip matrix is \(-I\), so after one return the coordinates are \(-0.1\,\mathrm{mm},-1\,\mathrm{mrad}\). After two trips they return to their initial values. This exact symmetry of identical mirrors explains why a degenerate trace does not automatically imply secular walk-off.

Further reading · MIT Fundamentals of Photonics, Gaussian beams and resonators.

Chapter 17 · Threshold, gain saturation and the decay of stored light

After establishing a transverse cavity mode, consider its intensity over many round trips. The same mirror losses determine a threshold with pumping and a measurable decay time when gain is removed.

Record every factor in one round trip

Let \(L\) be the physical distance between mirrors. Their intensity reflectances are \(R_1,R_2\). Suppose the entire length contains a uniform medium with intensity gain coefficient \(g\) and distributed intensity loss coefficient \(\alpha\), both per metre. Traversing length \(dz\) multiplies intensity by \(1+(g-\alpha)dz\), which integrates to \(e^{(g-\alpha)L}\). During a round trip the light crosses the medium twice and reflects once at each mirror. The multiplier is \(\mathcal M=R_1R_2e^{2(g-\alpha)L}\). Small fluctuations in light intensity grow if \(\mathcal M>1\). Setting it to one and taking logarithms gives

\[g_{\rm th}=\alpha+\frac1{2L}\ln\frac1{R_1R_2}.\]

This is a balance for a resonant mode. Phase closure also selects the allowed longitudinal frequencies and only modes overlapping the gain band can meet threshold. If a shorter gain section occupies length \(\ell<L\), its exponent is \(2g\ell\) while background loss can still act over \(2L\). Using an amplitude reflectivity in a formula derived for intensity would introduce a second independent factor-of-two error.

Find the stationary intensity

As stimulated emission increases, the available population inversion typically falls. In a simple homogeneously saturated, single-mode, spatially averaged model use \(g(I)=g_0/(1+I/I_s)\) with small-signal gain \(g_0\) and saturation intensity \(I_s\). A stationary positive intensity requires \(g(I)=g_{\rm th}\), hence \(I/I_s=g_0/g_{\rm th}-1\). Below threshold the positive solution disappears and the deterministic zero-intensity state is stable. Above threshold a small increase in intensity lowers gain and pushes the system back. Spontaneous emission seeds a real laser and smooths the ideal transition. Relaxation oscillations would require an explicit time equation for the inversion.

For \(L=0.30\,\mathrm m\), \(\alpha=0.10\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}\), \(R_1=0.98\) and \(R_2=0.90\),

\[g_{\rm th}=0.3093\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}.\]

A pump giving \(g_0=0.50\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}\) therefore sustains \(I\simeq0.617I_s\). If the output mirror transmitted more light, it could extract a greater fraction of the circulating light per encounter. It would also raise threshold. At a fixed pump these effects compete.

Read the passive lifetime from the same losses

Switch the gain term to zero. After \(j\) round trips the intensity is \(I_j=I_0[R_1R_2e^{-2\alpha L}]^j\). The time per round trip in a uniform medium of group index \(n_g\) is \(t_{\rm rt}=2n_gL/c\). Match \(I_j/I_0=e^{-jt_{\rm rt}/\tau}\) to obtain

\[\tau=-t_{\rm rt}/\ln(R_1R_2e^{-2\alpha L}).\]

In air the preceding example gives \(\tau=10.8\,\mathrm{ns}\). This is the intensity lifetime. The electric-field amplitude would decay twice as slowly.

Adjust the output reflectance, then predict which way the threshold and passive lifetime will move. The graph gives the stationary circulating intensity against small-signal gain. Its change of slope is observable even without knowing \(I_s\) exactly. The readout evaluates the selected pump point and the passive lifetime independently.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on inferring a mirror reflectance

A passive air cavity of length \(0.30\,\mathrm m\) with negligible internal absorption has an intensity lifetime \(20.0\,\mathrm{ns}\). Mirror one reflects 99.0 percent. Infer mirror two's intensity reflectance and the uniform gain coefficient required for threshold.

Work through the solution

The round-trip time is \(2.00\,\mathrm{ns}\). The round-trip intensity retention is \(e^{-2.00/20.0}=0.9048\), so

\[R_2=0.9048/0.990=0.914.\]

The threshold is

\[-\ln(0.9048)/(0.60\,\mathrm m)=0.167\,\mathrm{m^{-1}}.\]

Scattering or absorption unaccounted for in the inference would be attributed to mirror two.

Compare the energy lost per round trip with the exponential time constant in the example. Why would treating the logarithm as its first-order expansion bias the lifetime?

Work through the solution

The retention is

\[0.98\times0.90e^{-0.06}=0.8306.\]

The fractional loss is 0.1694, but the exponential log decrement is \(-\ln0.8306=0.1856\). Using the fractional loss instead would give \(2.00\,\mathrm{ns}/0.1694=11.8\,\mathrm{ns}\), longer than the exact 10.8 ns. The difference vanishes only in the small-loss limit.

If an ideal gain crystal fills half the original cavity and all other losses remain, determine the gain coefficient required inside the crystal.

Work through the solution

The round-trip gain becomes \(2g\ell\) with \(\ell=L/2\). The target round-trip log gain is unchanged. Therefore the necessary crystal gain doubles from \(0.3093\) to \(0.6186\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\). This comparison assumes the gain crystal does not alter reflectance, group velocity or losses elsewhere.

Further reading · Delft Interactive Optics, gain, resonators and laser threshold.

Chapter 18 · Turning invisible phase into visible intensity in a Fourier plane

A transparent specimen can vary the phase of a beam while transmitting nearly constant intensity. A pair of lenses gives independent access to the undiffracted beam and the waves carrying those small phase variations. Recombining them with a controlled relative phase makes the specimen visible.

Locate the orders in a four-focal-length system

Use coherent, normally incident monochromatic illumination and two ideal thin lenses of equal focal length \(f\). Put the specimen one focal length before lens one; its back focal plane coincides with the front focal plane of lens two, and the final image lies one further focal length beyond lens two. A spatial harmonic \(e^{i2\pi\nu x}\) leaves the object with transverse wavevector \(2\pi\nu\). In the paraxial approximation its propagation angle is \(\lambda\nu\). The first lens therefore maps it to \(X=f\lambda\nu\) in the common focal plane. The second lens recombines transmitted orders into an inverted image, apart from a common phase and scale.

For grating period \(d=20\,\mu\mathrm m\), \(f=100\,\mathrm{mm}\) and \(\lambda=500\,\mathrm{nm}\), the first diffraction orders lie \(2.50\,\mathrm{mm}\) to either side of the centre. A mask small enough to cover just the undiffracted spot changes the reference wave independently of those orders if the illumination extent makes each spot sufficiently narrow.

Keep the field complex until the detector

Let a weak pure phase grating have transmission \(t(x)=e^{i\epsilon\cos(2\pi x/d)}\), \(|\epsilon|\ll1\). To first order it consists of a constant reference and two imaginary sidebands of amplitude \(i\epsilon/2\). The original intensity \(|t|^2\) is exactly one. Apply a phase shift \(\varphi\) to the central order alone. To first order the output field is \(e^{i\varphi}+i\epsilon\cos(2\pi x/d)\). The inverted coordinate gives the same cosine. Its intensity becomes

\[I(x)/I_0=1+2\epsilon\sin\varphi\cos(2\pi x/d)+O(\epsilon^2).\]

The cross term comes from multiplying the sidebands by the conjugate of the modified reference. At \(\varphi=+\pi/2\) the phase grating gains positive intensity contrast; the opposite shift reverses it. With \(\epsilon=0.10\), the leading extrema are 1.20 and 0.80. Blocking the reference entirely instead leaves a dark-field intensity \(\epsilon^2\cos^2(2\pi x/d)\), insensitive at leading order to the sign of the phase.

Retain the exact phase grating for a numerical check

Within an ideal infinite-aperture relay, only the central Fourier coefficient is changed. That coefficient is \(c_0=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_0^{2\pi}e^{i\epsilon\cos u}du\), real because imaginary contributions cancel by symmetry. Thus the exact transmitted image field is \(e^{i\epsilon\cos u}+(e^{i\varphi}-1)c_0\). It reduces exactly to the original grating at zero phase shift. Its period-averaged intensity remains one because a lossless mask changes phases only. When \(\epsilon\) is no longer small, the image contains distorted harmonics that the linear contrast formula misses.

Change the phase amplitude and central phase in the experiment. It uses periodic quadrature for \(c_0\), computes the exact intensity, and compares the linear prediction. Looking at both minima and maxima exposes weak-phase breakdown even when the overall image looks periodic. Aberrations, partial coherence and finite objective aperture can modify real measurements by affecting additional Fourier components.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on the geometry of a phase plate

Change to a \(10\,\mu\mathrm m\) grating at unchanged optics and then halve the focal length. Where do the first orders lie in the final configuration relative to their original spots?

Work through the solution

Halving \(d\) doubles \(\nu\), while halving \(f\) cancels that change. Both spots again sit 2.50 mm from centre. Although the spot positions agree, the two physical gratings have different spatial periods in their respective image planes.

A specimen gives normalised peak-to-trough intensity difference 0.32 with an ideal quarter-cycle central shift. Infer its phase amplitude in the weak model. How could you check for an amplitude grating contaminating the signal?

Work through the solution

The leading difference is \(4|\epsilon|\), giving \(|\epsilon|=0.080\) rad. Reverse the central phase shift and repeat. The pure-phase contribution changes sign. Also image with no central phase shift, for which a true pure-phase transmission produces constant intensity in an ideal relay.
