A microscope can transmit plenty of light and still erase fine detail. Treat the specimen as a weak grating, follow its spatial harmonics through the objective, and determine why changing illumination coherence changes the transfer of contrast.

Chapter 19 · Building a dielectric mirror from weakly reflecting interfaces

A single transparent dielectric interface often reflects only a few percent of the optical power. A periodic stack can reflect nearly all incident light over a finite wavelength band. Its response follows from matching both fields at every interface, including every internally reflected wave.

Propagate two continuous tangential fields

At normal incidence use the field vector \((E,HZ_0)\), with free-space impedance \(Z_0\). A forward travelling wave in a nonmagnetic lossless layer of index \(n\) has \(HZ_0=nE\); a backward wave has the opposite sign. With time factor \(e^{+i\omega t}\), forward propagation contributes \(e^{-ikz}\). Resolve the fields at the exit face into these two waves, propagate back by distance \(d\) and recombine. The input fields are the output fields multiplied by

\[M(n,\delta)=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\delta&i\sin\delta/n\\in\sin\delta&\cos\delta\end{pmatrix},\qquad\delta=2\pi nd/\lambda .\]

Its determinant is one. Use a product \(M_HM_L\) for a high-index layer encountered first, followed by a low-index layer. The tangential fields are continuous at every interface, so no separate interface matrices are required in this field basis. Changing the order can change the reflection phase and the way the stack matches different external media.

Compute a quarter-wave pair without numerical matrices

Choose layer thicknesses with \(n_Hd_H=n_Ld_L=\lambda_0/4\). At the design wavelength both phases are \(\pi/2\). Multiplying the two off-diagonal matrices gives the diagonal pair matrix \(\operatorname{diag}(-n_L/n_H,-n_H/n_L)\). A forward transmitted wave in substrate \(n_s\) has output admittance \(Y_s=HZ_0/E=n_s\). After \(N\) identical pairs the entrance admittance becomes

\[Y_{\rm in}=n_s(n_H/n_L)^{2N}.\]

Matching to the incident plus reflected waves in \(n_0\), \(Y_{\rm in}=n_0(1-r)/(1+r)\), therefore

\[r=\frac{n_0-Y_{\rm in}}{n_0+Y_{\rm in}},\]
\[R=|r|^2 .\]

With incident air, \(n_H=2.0\), \(n_L=n_s=1.50\), and four pairs, \(Y_{\rm in}=14.98\) and \(R=0.765\). Eight pairs give \(Y_{\rm in}\simeq149.7\) and \(R\simeq0.974\). Each extra pair multiplies the admittance at the design point, while the reflectance approaches a ceiling of one. The enhancement depends on fixed phase relations across the layers. Adding interface intensities would double-count some energy and lose the interference.

Use the same equations away from design

At a general wavelength the matrices contain both diagonal and off-diagonal terms. For total entries \(M_{ij}\),

\[Y_{\rm in}=(M_{21}+M_{22}n_s)/(M_{11}+M_{12}n_s).\]

One may instead propagate the substrate admittance backwards through each layer by this fractional linear transformation. This is the recursion used below. It avoids introducing any fictitious absorption. The number of periods mainly controls how close the stop-band reflection comes to unity, whereas the index contrast strongly influences the bandwidth. Material dispersion, roughness and optical absorption are excluded from the model.

Set the design wavelength to the fixed 600 nm reference in the experiment. Change the high index or number of pairs. Compare the on-design readout to the diagonal formula, then inspect the finite-stack ripples away from the central band. The display holds quarter-wave optical thicknesses at the design wavelength as indices change, so it represents redesigned layers.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on a coating specification

For the worked indices find the minimum pair count with on-design intensity reflectance above 0.99.

Work through the solution

Require

\[Y_{\rm in}/n_0>(1+\sqrt{0.99})/(1-\sqrt{0.99})=398.0.\]

Thus

\[N>\ln(398.0/1.5)/[2\ln(2/1.5)]=9.70.\]

Ten complete pairs suffice in this ideal lossless calculation.

What happens when the two layer indices become equal? Why can a central wavelength still be defined geometrically yet fail to produce a stop band?

Work through the solution

Every internal interface then has identical fields on both sides with zero reflection. The pair transfer at the design wavelength is \(-I\), which leaves the substrate admittance unchanged for any number of pairs. With a substrate of the same index only the external air-to-material mismatch remains. A chosen phase delay alone does not create contrast without a spatial modulation of index.

Further reading · MIT Fundamentals of Photonics, thin-film transfer matrices and Bragg mirrors.

Chapter 20 · Why a grating makes images of itself in empty space

Illuminate a periodic mask with a monochromatic plane wave. A screen placed at certain distances records the original pattern even though there is no imaging lens. Derive the recurrence from the longitudinal phase of each diffraction order, predict the half-period displacement, and use exact propagation to test the paraxial argument.

A near-field measurement that retains the relative phases

Imagine a plane transmission mask made by repeating one profile \(t(x)\) every distance \(d\). Coherent, normally incident illumination makes the complex transverse field immediately behind the mask proportional to this transmission. In a far-field experiment one might separate diffraction orders and measure their individual intensities. To reconstruct the mask in free space, however, the orders must illuminate the same region of the screen and preserve their relative phases. Assume an effectively infinite mask and a scalar field with wavelength \(\lambda\) in the propagation medium. A wide finite illuminated region can approximate this idealisation near its centre for a limited distance.

Because \(t(x+d)=t(x)\), its Fourier series requires only transverse wave numbers \(k_{x,m}=2\pi m/d\) with integer \(m\). Write the input as \(\sum c_m e^{2\pi i m x/d}\). The homogeneous Helmholtz equation for a single plane-wave component is \(k_x^2+k_z^2=k^2\) with \(k=2\pi/\lambda\). Choose \(k_z=\sqrt{k^2-k_x^2}\) with nonnegative propagation or decay, so a forward field propagates as \(e^{ik_z z}\) and an evanescent component decays. This branch choice is physical; the opposite choice could turn unresolved fine structure into unbounded growth with distance.

Subtract the common carrier \(e^{ikz}\). It has unit modulus in a lossless medium and cannot affect intensity. When \(|k_x|\ll k\), expand the square root:

\[k_z-k=-k_x^2/(2k)-k_x^4/(8k^3)+\cdots.\]

Keeping the quadratic part gives a phase proportional to the square of an integer. This arithmetic is the source of the rephasing; it does not require a caustic or refraction at an interface.

Derive the first complete field recurrence

With \(\xi=x/d\), the paraxial field envelope a distance \(z\) behind the mask is

\[U(\xi,z)=\sum_{m=-\infty}^{\infty} c_m e^{2\pi i m\xi}\exp\!\left(-i\pi\lambda z m^2/d^2\right).\]

Demand that the propagation factor be one for every integer \(m\), including \(m=1\). The first positive distance that meets this condition for a generic mask is \(z_T=2d^2/\lambda\). Each factor becomes

\[e^{-2\pi i m^2}=1.\]

The complex field is thus restored up to the common carrier, a stronger result than merely recovering an intensity pattern. Some masks have extra symmetries or contain only selected orders and can give earlier intensity revivals. Define the length used in a report explicitly, particularly when shifted self-images are included.

For a 12 micrometre period and wavelength 0.60 micrometre, the full paraxial recurrence is 480 micrometres, short enough that the separation of individual far-field orders would be a misleading picture. Doubling the period at fixed wavelength produces a fourfold distance. Doubling wavelength while holding the period fixed halves the predicted length and at the same time increases the angles of the orders. The latter change makes the paraxial expansion less accurate, so variation of \(\lambda\) tests two effects at once.

Half a recurrence shifts the image; a quarter combines two images

At \(z_T/2\) the factor is

\[e^{-i\pi m^2}=(-1)^m.\]

A spatial shift by half a period contributes exactly the same phase to Fourier order \(m\), since \(e^{\pi i m}=(-1)^m\). Thus the field becomes \(t(\xi+1/2)\). A photograph at this plane can look identical if one ignores the grating origin; registration against a marked edge discloses the shift. The derivation depends on integers modulo two, not on the detailed transmission of a transparent or absorbing structure.

At \(z_T/4\) an even order has factor one and an odd order factor \(-i\). Split the field into the components even and odd under a half-period translation. Their respective projectors acting on a pattern are \([t(\xi)+t(\xi+1/2)]/2\) and \([t(\xi)-t(\xi+1/2)]/2\). Recombining with the two phases gives

\[U(\xi,z_T/4)=\frac{1-i}{2}t(\xi)+\frac{1+i}{2}t(\xi+1/2).\]

These are complex amplitudes, so they must be summed before taking an absolute square. For a real mask, the coefficients differ in phase by a right angle and the two cross terms cancel in intensity. The quarter-plane intensity is then the average of the intensities in the original and shifted masks. A complex phase mask need not have that cancellation. Replace a real transmission by one with the same modulus and a sinusoidal phase and the input photograph can remain unchanged while the propagated pattern changes markedly.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Compare an exact angular spectrum with the paraxial reconstruction

The notebook uses a deliberately smooth, real mask with a nonzero constant term and two pairs of Fourier orders. Its peak transmission is below one. Starting with only finitely many orders separates propagation error from the truncation error that a numerical square-wave mask would introduce. The code computes the forward Fourier amplitudes, multiplies each by the exact propagator and transforms back. The numerical grid contains many more points than required to represent these five orders. Finer pixel sampling cannot correct a wrong propagation phase.

Try it · Talbot

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Open this live calculation

Download the corresponding editable notebook. The browser recalculates the reduced equations directly; the notebook exposes the full calculation and its checks.

For an order \(m\) the leading phase error of the paraxial approximation at one Talbot distance is \(-(\pi/2)m^4(\lambda/d)^2\). This follows by multiplying the neglected quartic term in \(k_z\) by \(2d^2/\lambda\). A small diffraction angle ensures that the wavenumber expansion is locally accurate, but the propagation distance can still accumulate an appreciable phase, especially for high orders. At \(\lambda/d=0.05\) and \(m=2\), the leading magnitude is \(0.0628\) rad. The visual distortion remains small for the present mask because this order also has only a modest amplitude. Frequency content and propagation distance both enter an accuracy statement.

Increase the wavelength-to-period ratio and compare the near-coincidence at full distance with the exact curves. Run the included assertions on the complex paraxial field, not only its intensity. Equality at half distance is checked against a discrete half-period shift. At the quarter distance it is checked against the analytic superposition. An optional OneSimulation cell applies its angular-spectrum propagation operator to the identical field and compares the complex output with the explicitly constructed exact spectrum. Agreement here validates the branch, units and normalisation. It does not make the paraxial approximation exact.

Which aspects of the ideal experiment survive a laboratory source?

A finite wavelength distribution produces a distribution of propagation phases. At a fixed screen location the camera records the intensity averaged over mutually incoherent wavelengths. Orders that agree in phase at the central wavelength accumulate a spread proportional to \(m^2 z\). Fine structure requiring higher orders therefore loses contrast first. Averaging wavelengths as fields instead of intensities would represent a phase-locked coherent source and a detector model with different temporal resolution. The distinction matters most when interpreting a blurred revival as evidence of a faulty grating.

A finite angular spread of incident plane waves has a different signature. Under a small tilt \(\theta\), the transverse wave number becomes \(k\theta+2\pi m/d\). Squaring produces a common phase, the usual term in \(m^2\), and a term linear in \(m\). A linear Fourier phase shifts the whole pattern laterally by approximately \(z\theta\); averaging over unknown incident angles thus convolves it with a distribution of translations. A finite lateral aperture additionally limits the distance over which all orders overlap. These mechanisms suggest separate controls: narrowing spectral width, improving collimation, and enlarging illuminated area alter different parts of the experiment.

For quantitative comparison register the predicted and observed patterns in a common coordinate, subtract camera dark level, and state whether normalisation removes total-power information. An intensity correlation near one may mean that two profiles have the same shape even when one carries much less power. A complex-field discrepancy asks an even stricter question that normally requires phase-sensitive measurements. The numerical field is available in the notebook, but the physical camera does not automatically measure that entire array.

Tutorial on recurrence, fractional images and coherence

A grating of period 20 micrometres makes a full paraxial image 1.60 mm downstream. Determine the wavelength and the location of the shifted image.

Work through the solution

Rearranging gives \(\lambda=2d^2/z_T=0.50\) micrometre. The half-period-shifted pattern appears at 0.80 mm, displaced by 10 micrometres. If the recorded plane were actually the shifted rather than full recurrence, the inferred wavelength would differ by a factor of two. A fixed lateral reference resolves that ambiguity.

Only the constant order and the pair \(m=\pm2\) are present. How soon does the exact paraxial complex envelope recur when distances are expressed using the nominal period \(d\)?

Work through the solution

The variable propagation factor is \(e^{-2\pi i\,4z/z_T}\). It reaches one first at \(z_T/4\). The actual mask constructed from these orders has fundamental period \(d/2\), whose ordinary Talbot length \(2(d/2)^2/\lambda\) gives exactly the same answer.

A screen is at 1.0 mm and incident directions vary over an rms 1 mrad. Estimate the translation blur for a grating of period 10 micrometres. Which harmonic is most sensitive?

Work through the solution

Small-angle translations have rms width about \(1.0\) micrometre, one tenth of a grating period. For a Gaussian angular distribution, averaging the intensity harmonic with lateral wave number \(2\pi n/d\) multiplies its amplitude by \(\exp[-(2\pi n z\sigma_\theta/d)^2/2]\). The fundamental retains about 82 per cent and the second harmonic about 45 per cent. Higher spatial frequencies are progressively more sensitive.

Further reading · M. S. Chapman and collaborators · Near-field imaging of atom diffraction gratings

Problem sheet 7 · Choosing an output mirror for a small optical resonator

A laboratory has two interchangeable output mirrors for the same symmetric air cavity. One transmits twice as much light as the other at each reflection. Determine which one can deliver light at the available pump, and use a beam-width measurement and a ring-down trace to test the assumptions.

Predict the geometry before inserting gain

Both mirrors have curvature radius \(R=0.50\,\mathrm m\), separation \(L=0.30\,\mathrm m\), and operating wavelength \(600\,\mathrm{nm}\). Find the Rayleigh length, the waist radius and the spot radius at the mirror. Calculate the fraction of Gaussian power beyond a circular mirror of clear radius \(0.60\,\mathrm{mm}\).

Work through the solution

Symmetry fixes \(z_R=\sqrt{(L/2)(R-L/2)}=0.2291\,\mathrm m\). Thus

\[w_0=\sqrt{\lambda z_R/\pi}=0.2092\,\mathrm{mm},\]
\[w_m=w_0\sqrt{1+(L/2z_R)^2}=0.2500\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

Integrating the radial Gaussian \(r\exp(-2r^2/w_m^2)\) from \(a\) to infinity and dividing by the total integral gives

\[e^{-2a^2/w_m^2}=9.9\times10^{-6}.\]

Neglecting clipping against percent-level mirror losses is plausible for this centred mode.

Set the separation-to-radius ratio to 0.60 and verify the mirror spot

Predict the operating point for each mirror

The input mirror has intensity reflectance \(R_1=0.98\), distributed intensity loss is \(\alpha=0.10\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\), and uniform small-signal gain is \(g_0=0.50\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\). Compare output mirrors with \(R_2=0.90\) and \(R_2=0.80\). Use \(g(I)=g_0/(1+I/I_s)\) and estimate transmitted intensity relative to \(I_s\) for both.

Work through the solution

The thresholds are \(\alpha-\ln(R_1R_2)/(2L)=0.3093\) and \(0.5056\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\). The first mirror supports circulating intensity \(I/I_s=0.50/0.3093-1=0.617\), with transmitted fraction \(0.10\times0.617=0.0617\) relative to \(I_s\). The second has no positive stationary lasing solution at this pump in the ideal model. Doubling transmission per encounter has raised the required gain above that available.

Run the two reflectance settings at the same pump

Check the losses using a passive decay

For the first mirror combination a passive ring-down fit gives an intensity lifetime of \(9.0\,\mathrm{ns}\). Under the uniform model infer the effective attenuation coefficient and compare it to the stated distributed loss. Could the previously estimated clipping of the centred beam explain the discrepancy?

Work through the solution

With an air round trip,

\[1/(c\tau)=g_{\rm th,eff}=0.3706\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}.\]

Subtract the mirror term \(-\ln(0.98\times0.90)/0.60=0.2093\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\) to infer \(\alpha_{\rm eff}=0.1613\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\). The excess is about \(0.0613\,\mathrm{m}^{-1}\), corresponding to \(1-e^{-2L(0.0613)}=3.61\%\) extra round-trip loss. Two centred aperture losses of order \(10^{-5}\) cannot account for this observation under the assumed mode. Alignment or additional scattering could be examined directly.

Transfer the measurement to a changed geometry

Increase the separation to \(0.90\,\mathrm m\) with unchanged curvature and wavelength. Estimate the new mirror clipping for \(a=0.60\,\mathrm{mm}\). Explain why using the original measured attenuation as a material coefficient could fail.

Work through the solution

Now \(z_R=0.150\,\mathrm m\), \(w_0=0.1693\,\mathrm{mm}\), and \(w_m=0.535\,\mathrm{mm}\). The estimated per-encounter clipped fraction rises to \(e^{-2(0.60/0.535)^2}\approx0.081\). This magnitude is substantial. The field shape may itself be altered by the aperture, so a new ring-down and beam-profile measurement should accompany the estimate. Geometry-dependent clipping can be folded into an inferred attenuation at one geometry without representing distributed absorption.

Problem sheet 8 · Measure a double source with a two-aperture interferometer

Two distant point emitters cannot be separated in a direct image. An observer combines the light collected by two small apertures a variable distance apart. Derive how the visibility of the combined fringes can reveal the angular separation and how a faint companion differs from imperfect temporal coherence.

Add the light from one emitter

A plane wave from a small sky angle \(\theta\) arrives at apertures separated by projected baseline \(B\). One arm has an adjustable phase delay \(\phi\). Starting with the complex field amplitude from each aperture, derive the intensity as the delay is scanned. State which phase one can remove by choosing the origin of the time coordinate.

Work through the solution

The path difference for the arriving wave is \(B\sin\theta\simeq B\theta\). Its contribution to the relative phase is \(2\pi B\theta/\lambda\). Equal aperture amplitudes add as \(a[1+e^{i(\phi+2\pi B\theta/\lambda)}]\), up to an overall phase. Taking the squared modulus gives \(2|a|^2[1+\cos(\phi+2\pi B\theta/\lambda)]\). An overall phase disappears from this expression, while a relative phase remains observable when the delay is scanned. The calculation assumes equal throughput and matching polarisation in the two arms.

Combine independent sources and recover their separation

Let two mutually incoherent emitters of equal brightness have angles \(\pm\delta/2\). Show why intensities add between emitters, even though fields added between apertures for each emitter. Find the first visibility minimum and use a first null at \(B=20\,\mathrm m\), \(\lambda=600\,\mathrm{nm}\) to determine \(\delta\).

Work through the solution

Each source is coherent with its own delayed copy for sufficiently narrow bandwidth. The relative phase between the two different emitters wanders during the exposure, removing their mutual cross term. Average the two cosine fringes. Their sum is proportional to \(1+\cos(\phi)\cos(\pi B\delta/\lambda)\). The measured visibility, defined by the extremal intensities, is the absolute value of the second cosine.

\[\mathcal V=\frac{I_{\max}-I_{\min}}{I_{\max}+I_{\min}}=|\cos(\pi B\delta/\lambda)|,\]
\[\delta=\frac{\lambda}{2B_{\rm first}}.\]

These data give \(1.5\times10^{-8}\,\mathrm{rad}\), or about 3.09 milliarcseconds. A single nonzero visibility may correspond to many angular separations because the cosine repeats. Scanning outward from small baselines distinguishes the first null from subsequent ones. A baseline perpendicular to the separation measures zero projected separation, irrespective of the distance on the sky.

Infer an intensity ratio from a filled minimum

The second emitter has intensity \(r\) times that of the first with \(0<r\le1\). Derive the new visibility. A well calibrated instrument obtains minimum 0.25. Infer \(r\). Decide whether unequal emitter brightness can move the first minimum in this ideal model.

Work through the solution

The normalised complex fringe amplitude, after dropping an overall phase, is \((1+r e^{i2\pi B\delta/\lambda})/(1+r)\). Its modulus is the visibility. At odd half-fringe offsets the phasors oppose and the minimum is \((1-r)/(1+r)\). Inversion gives

\[r=(1-0.25)/(1+0.25)=0.60.\]

The phase of opposition is unchanged. Interchanging source labels leaves the measured visibility invariant, so it identifies a ratio up to that choice.

Design an observation that separates the instrument from the source

Change angular separation and intensity ratio before reading any numerical values. Compare the location of the first trough with its depth. A calibrator at effectively zero angular size yields visibility 0.80. A binary has raw minimum 0.20 in the same setup. What can be inferred if the entire contrast loss is multiplicative? Which additional observations test this assumption?

Open this experiment online

Work through the solution

Dividing by the calibrator visibility gives an intrinsic trough of 0.25 and the same inferred intensity ratio 0.60. The model assumes the instrument suppresses contrast by a common factor at that baseline, wavelength and time. Observing the calibrator as a function of baseline checks one part of that assumption. Narrowing the spectral band checks whether the fringe phase changes appreciably across the passband. Turning the baseline tests whether the measured spacing follows the projected binary direction. Each intervention singles out a different step in the derivation.

The imaging and spatial coherence material in TU Delft’s Interactive Optics develops the continuous-source version of the same phasor sum.

Notebook practice · Why a grating makes images of itself in empty space

Optical fields, instruments and observations

Trace an optical field through a physical instrument. Calculate the signal a detector measures and test the approximations on which the interpretation rests.

  1. What an objective must collect to resolve a specimen

    A microscope can transmit plenty of light and still erase fine detail. Treat the specimen as a weak grating, follow its spatial harmonics through the objective, and determine why changing illumination coherence changes the transfer of contrast.

  2. From a slowly varying wave to a curved ray in a graded index

    A ray bends continuously in a glass whose index changes across its width. Derive where that ray equation comes from, predict the focusing length of a graded-index rod, and locate the points at which a smooth ray description ceases to determine an optical amplitude.

  3. Measure rotation with two waves following the same path

    A stationary optical loop gives equal return times for opposite propagation directions in a reciprocal medium. When the loop rotates the beams meet their starting beamsplitter at different times. Derive the measurable differential phase and compare a passive interferometer with a ring resonator.

  4. Dispersive pulse compression and a detector's view of a tilted pulse

    An ultrashort pulse can broaden because its spectral components acquire different group delays or because different parts of its beam arrive at different times. Derive the two mechanisms separately, predict when added glass can shorten an already chirped pulse, and decide which measurements distinguish them.

  5. Which polarisation measurements identify an optical element?

    A retarder rotates the state of a coherent beam, while unresolved mixtures can reduce its measured degree of polarisation. Derive the passage from Jones fields to a Mueller matrix, reconstruct a simple instrument from measured intensities, and identify claims the available input states cannot support.

  6. Photon counting, bunching and the time resolution of a coincidence test

    An intensity trace can be steady while individual detector clicks show correlations. Derive the observable of a two-detector Hanbury Brown–Twiss measurement, quantify how background and bin width change it, and decide whether a dip near zero delay belongs to the source or to a detector.

  7. Build a time lens from dispersion and a quadratic temporal phase

    A temporal imaging system can stretch the time separation of two optical features without requiring them to arrive more slowly at the source. Derive the imaging condition from delay and frequency, calculate the sign of time magnification, and estimate the finite temporal aperture that limits which features can be recovered.

  8. Recover an optical phase from one off-axis intensity frame

    A camera measures intensity and discards field phase. A known tilted reference wave can shift a cross term away from the intensity background so the missing complex field becomes accessible. Derive how much camera bandwidth that costs, and convert the recovered phase into an optical-path measurement with a stated ambiguity.

  9. Resolve two nearby wavelengths by watching fringes disappear

    A Michelson interferometer shows very fine bright and dark fringes as one mirror moves. Two narrow spectral lines produce a much slower variation of the fringe contrast. Use the mirror travel between contrast minima to determine the separation of the lines.

  10. Where a thick lens focuses and which aperture an instrument actually sees

    The distance from a lens mount to a sharply focused sensor is often shorter than the focal length printed on the barrel. Two principal planes, together with the image of the physical aperture stop, reconcile these measurements and determine which rays an instrument accepts.

  11. From measured wavefront error to coma, astigmatism and focus

    A point source can become a comet-shaped patch or two perpendicular line foci. Describing these observations by a single blur diameter loses the distinction between aberrations and suggests the wrong correction. The gradient of a pupil wavefront predicts the ray displacement at the detector.

  12. Why light can generate its second harmonic and then lose it again

    A nonlinear polarisation can oscillate twice during one period of the applied field. Whether the generated wave grows through a crystal depends on how successive source layers arrive in phase. This spatial phase relation makes crystal length and dispersion as important as local nonlinearity.

  13. How a material changes the speed, attenuation and direction of light

    A detector behind a sample loses light both when matter absorbs energy and when light is scattered outside its acceptance. A microscopic oscillator connects refraction to absorption. A radiating dipole then shows how a transparent particle can also reduce the measured direct beam.

Optical fields, instruments and observations · 1. What an objective must collect to resolve a specimen

A microscope can transmit plenty of light and still erase fine detail. Treat the specimen as a weak grating, follow its spatial harmonics through the objective, and determine why changing illumination coherence changes the transfer of contrast.

Diffract a sinusoidal object into orders

Use a thin specimen with amplitude transmission \(t(x)=1+\epsilon\cos(2\pi fx)\), \(|\epsilon|\ll1\), and uniform normally incident coherent light. Write the cosine as half the sum of two exponentials. Immediately after the sample the field has spatial frequencies zero and \(\pm f\). A lens maps transverse spatial frequency into angle, and its finite numerical aperture \({\rm NA}=n\sin\theta_{\max}\) accepts \(|f|\le{\rm NA}/\lambda_0\) for coherent normal illumination, where \(\lambda_0\) is the vacuum wavelength. Beyond that limit both first orders are blocked, leaving only a constant image field and no sinusoidal contrast.

If the zero order and both sidebands pass through a clear ideal pupil, inverse Fourier transformation reproduces \(1+\epsilon\cos(2\pi fx)\). Its intensity is approximately \(1+2\epsilon\cos(2\pi fx)\). If only one sideband and the zero order pass, their interference still produces a feature at \(f\), but the first-order intensity contrast is halved for this object and the image field acquires a spatially varying phase. If the zero order is suppressed as well, the intensity contrast follows a different mixing law. These possibilities show why the statement that one needs two interfering orders must specify which orders and which image quantity is measured.

Oblique coherent illumination shifts the object's spatial spectrum by the incident transverse frequency \(f_i\). A sideband initially outside the pupil can enter while one from the opposite direction exits. With illumination aperture \({\rm NA}_{\rm illum}\) and objective aperture \({\rm NA}_{\rm obj}\), the extreme accessible object frequency from angular coverage can reach \(({\rm NA}_{\rm illum}+{\rm NA}_{\rm obj})/\lambda_0\). That support limit alone does not guarantee uniform contrast or isotropic information. The illumination distribution and reconstruction determine how well any frequency inside the support can be measured.

Derive the incoherent transfer from a pupil autocorrelation

For spatially incoherent emitters, intensities from different object points add. If \(h\) is the coherent amplitude point-spread function, the incoherent intensity point-spread function is \(|h|^2\), and the intensity image is its convolution with object brightness. Fourier transforming the product \(|h|^2\) produces an autocorrelation of the pupil \(P\). A clear circular pupil gives a transfer value proportional to the overlap of two equal disks displaced by a spatial-frequency-dependent distance. At zero shift the disks overlap fully; at a diameter of shift their overlap vanishes. The incoherent cutoff is therefore \(f_c=2{\rm NA}/\lambda_0\), twice the coherent on-axis field cutoff.

For \(u=|f|/f_c\le1\), the normalised overlap area is

\[{\rm MTF}(u)=\frac{2}{\pi}\left[\cos^{-1}u-u\sqrt{1-u^2}\right].\]

To derive it divide the overlap lens into two symmetric disk segments. Each segment is a circular sector of angle \(2\arccos u\) with its isosceles triangle subtracted. Summing and dividing by the full area yields the expression above. It declines continuously and gives about \(0.391\) at half the incoherent cutoff. The coherent transfer of a clear pupil has unit field transmission inside its support; it operates on complex amplitude. These two curves describe different imaging experiments. Replacing a coherent field by its intensity before applying a coherent pupil would use the wrong linear system.

For \(\lambda_0=0.550\,\mu{\rm m}\) and \({\rm NA}=0.80\), the coherent cutoff is \(1.45\) cycles per micrometre and the incoherent cutoff \(2.91\) cycles per micrometre. An incoherent sinusoidal fluorescent brightness pattern at \(f=2.0\) cycles per micrometre has \(u=0.6875\) and ideal transmitted contrast about \(0.20\) of its original. A perfectly transmitting objective cannot recover the missing higher frequencies merely by magnifying the image farther. Magnification can, however, change camera sampling and therefore recover detail that the objective passed but undersampled camera pixels discarded.

Separate diffraction, sampling and noise in a resolution estimate

A detector with physical pixel pitch \(p\) at magnification \(M\) samples the specimen at \(p/M\). The Nyquist frequency is \(M/(2p)\). For \(p=6.5\,\mu{\rm m}\), \(M=40\) this gives \(3.08\) cycles per micrometre, just above the illustrative incoherent cutoff. Finite pixel area also averages over a square patch and multiplies the axial-frequency contrast by a sinc factor \(\sin(\pi f p/M)/(\pi f p/M)\). At \(2.0\) cycles per micrometre that factor is about \(0.835\). The optical and pixel factors multiply, leaving less measured contrast than an optical-only transfer curve predicts. A sinusoidal target in a diagonal orientation sees the product of the factors in the two camera axes.

A cutoff is a support statement. Discrimination in real noise requires a contrast or likelihood criterion, a measured background and a known photon count. Deconvolving a frequency where the transfer is \(0.1\) multiplies noise in that component by roughly ten when dividing by the transfer. At a true zero no such division can restore the missing datum without additional assumptions or measurements. A point-pair Rayleigh separation, the highest sinusoidal frequency transmitted, and precision in locating the centre of an isolated bright emitter answer three different quantitative questions.

Compare the pupil models directly

The live graph plots the normalised coherent field pupil and incoherent intensity modulation transfer on the same specimen-frequency axis, with a separately identified pixel-averaged incoherent curve. Change numerical aperture while holding camera sampling fixed, then change wavelength. Predict whether optical cutoff or camera Nyquist is limiting before reading the returned values. The rectangular coherent curve should be interpreted as amplitude transmission for normally incident coherent light, whereas the smoother curves act on brightness contrast of incoherent emitters.

Open this experiment online

Measure transfer with a sinusoidal calibration target

For an incoherent test pattern of mean brightness \(I_0\) and intrinsic modulation \(m_o\), measure the image modulation as \(m_i=(I_{\max}-I_{\min})/(I_{\max}+I_{\min})\) after correcting the additive detector background. In a linear shift-invariant imaging regime the ratio \(m_i/m_o\) estimates the magnitude of the total transfer at the target frequency. A constant stray background reduces the measured modulation even if the pupil is unchanged. It increases the denominator without increasing the numerator, so subtract its independently measured intensity before comparing with a diffraction formula.

For a target of modulation \(0.60\) producing corrected maximum and minimum signals \(550\) and \(450\) counts, the image modulation is \(0.10\), so the inferred transfer is \(0.167\). The earlier optical and pixel factors at two cycles per micrometre predict approximately this value. A target with sharp bars contains many harmonics rather than one pure frequency. Its peak-to-trough contrast cannot generally be inserted into the same single-frequency formula without accounting for those harmonics. A sinusoidal target or a measured Fourier coefficient gives a cleaner comparison with the pupil calculation.

Compare diffraction conventions across the pupil model

When checking a spatial transfer against a diffraction calculation, keep the vacuum wavelength, refractive index and numerical aperture together: index already enters through numerical aperture. Inserting the wavelength in the medium a second time in a formula written with vacuum wavelength would count that index twice. The distinction matters most for immersion objectives and for comparisons of cutoffs reported from different beam geometries.

The open TU Delft diffraction chapter develops the wave-optical treatment of apertures and optical systems.

Tutorial on resolution claims

A normal-incidence coherent image of a grating with period \(0.50\,\mu{\rm m}\) is uniform under the example objective. Can changing only illumination angle help?

Work through the solution

The spatial frequency is \(2.0\) cycles per micrometre, beyond the on-axis coherent limit \(1.45\). An illumination shift of order \(0.55\) cycles per micrometre can bring one first order inside while retaining the reference order, restoring some contrast. The shift corresponds to illumination NA about \(0.30\) at the stated wavelength. Other orientations can respond differently.

At \(20\times\) magnification with the same camera, which limit applies to an incoherent \(2.0\)-cycle-per-micrometre pattern?

Work through the solution

The specimen-plane sampling pitch is \(0.325\,\mu{\rm m}\), giving Nyquist \(1.54\) cycles per micrometre. Optics can transmit the pattern, but the camera aliases it. Increasing magnification can help until sampling ceases to be limiting; it cannot raise the optical transfer at a fixed specimen frequency.

In an ideal coherent microscope a weak pure-phase grating has \(t(x)\approx1+i\epsilon\cos(2\pi fx)\). Does passing all three orders guarantee first-order intensity contrast in focus?

Work through the solution

No first-order intensity term survives because the perturbation is in quadrature with the reference. The field modulation is present but the intensity changes only at higher order in this approximation. Introduce a controlled reference phase shift, defocus, or another phase-sensitive measurement to convert the phase modulation into brightness contrast. Distinguish absence of an order from absence of interference in the detected quadrature.

TU Delft's Advanced Optical Imaging course derives coherent image formation from the pupil.

Optical fields, instruments and observations · 2. From a slowly varying wave to a curved ray in a graded index

A ray bends continuously in a glass whose index changes across its width. Derive where that ray equation comes from, predict the focusing length of a graded-index rod, and locate the points at which a smooth ray description ceases to determine an optical amplitude.

Expand the wave equation in inverse optical frequency

In a transparent isotropic nonmagnetic medium, a scalar monochromatic field can satisfy \(\nabla^2 U+k_0^2n(\mathbf r)^2 U=0\), with \(k_0=2\pi/\lambda_0\). Assume \(n\) and a slowly varying envelope change over distances long compared with a local wavelength. Seek \(U=A(\mathbf r)e^{ik_0S(\mathbf r)}\). Substitution gives a leading term \(k_0^2A[n^2-|\nabla S|^2]\), a next term \(ik_0[2\nabla A\cdot\nabla S+A\nabla^2S]\), and \(\nabla^2A\). Vanishing of the leading term yields the eikonal equation \(|\nabla S|=n\). The gradient of optical path is the local normal to a phase front.

For a real leading amplitude the next term gives \(\nabla\cdot(A^2\nabla S)=0\): flux through a thin ray tube is conserved to this order. In a one-dimensional monotone geometry with rays along \(z\), \(S'=\pm n\) and the travelling-wave amplitude scales as \(n^{-1/2}\). Its field intensity parameter \(|A|^2\) falls as index rises while the transported flux proportional to \(n|A|^2\) stays constant. At an abrupt interface a reflected wave is generally needed to satisfy matching conditions, so the single-branch envelope law cannot determine the reflected power from a sudden index step.

The locally one-dimensional adiabatic criterion can be written \(|k'|/k^2\ll1\) with \(k=k_0 n\), accompanied by adequate smoothness of higher derivatives. Near a turning point the relevant longitudinal or transverse component of \(k\) can vanish even when \(n\) itself is finite. Then the WKB amplitude proportional to that component's inverse square root diverges. A finite-wave treatment gives a reflected and penetrating field. Thus merely comparing vacuum wavelength with the overall lens diameter cannot certify the ray approximation at every point.

Derive the graded-index ray equation and paraxial solution

A ray makes the optical path \(\int n\,ds\) stationary. With longitudinal axis \(z\) and transverse coordinate \(x(z)\), the integrand is \(n(x,z)\sqrt{1+x'^2}\). Its Euler–Lagrange equation is

\[d[nx'/\sqrt{1+x'^2}]/dz=n_x\sqrt{1+x'^2}.\]

For small slopes and a smooth index \(n(x)\simeq n_0[1-g^2x^2/2]\), retain the leading transverse restoring term. The equation becomes \(x''+g^2x=0\), with \(g\) in inverse metres. The axis has the highest index, and rays curve back toward it. Solving gives

\[x(z)=x_0\cos(gz)+\alpha_0\sin(gz)/g,\]

where \(\alpha_0=x'(0)\) is the internal paraxial slope.

A full spatial pitch is \(2\pi/g\), and a quarter-pitch length \(\pi/(2g)\) transforms entrance position into angle and entrance angle into exit position. Collimated input rays with \(\alpha_0=0\) cross the axis together at a quarter pitch. Rays launched from an axial point \(x_0=0\) instead emerge parallel there since \(x'=\alpha_0\cos(gz)\) vanishes. These are reciprocal ray maps. At a flat end face outside the rod, refraction changes the external slope according to the index ratio, so an internal angle-to-position scaling should not be directly applied to the angle of an external beam without that conversion.

For \(g=200\,{\rm m^{-1}}\) and \(n_0=1.50\), the quarter-pitch length is \(7.85\,{\rm mm}\). A ray launched at \(x_0=0.40\,{\rm mm}\) parallel to the axis reaches it with slope \(-g x_0=-0.080\), compatible with a paraxial starting estimate. The assumed index depression at that entrance radius is

\[n_0g^2x_0^2/2=0.0048.\]

If a measured rod reaches only \(0.002\) depression at the same radius, either \(g\) was overstated or the parabolic model is inaccurate. An independent transverse index map and a measured pitch provide complementary checks.

Connect ray turning points to confined wave modes

For a guide uniform in \(z\), seek \(U(x,z)=u(x)e^{i\beta z}\). The scalar Helmholtz equation gives

\[u''+[k_0^2n^2(x)-\beta^2]u=0.\]

The transverse local wave number \(k_x=\sqrt{k_0^2n^2-\beta^2}\) becomes imaginary outside the classical turning points. Away from them an oscillatory WKB mode has amplitude \(1/\sqrt{k_x}\) and phase \(\int k_x dx\); the evanescent tail decays as \(\exp[-\int |k_x|dx]\). Matching through two simple smooth turns gives the approximate condition \(\int_{x_-}^{x_+}k_xdx=(m+1/2)\pi\). The half-integer arises from the turning-point phase shifts and differs from the phase shifts at two rigid reflecting walls.

Under \(n^2(x)\simeq n_0^2(1-g^2x^2)\), the transverse equation takes harmonic-oscillator form with quadratic confinement. Direct polynomial-Gaussian normalisability yields

\[k_0^2n_0^2-\beta_m^2=(2m+1)k_0n_0g.\]

For small mode index relative to \(k_0n_0/g\),

\[\beta_m\simeq k_0n_0-(m+1/2)g.\]

Adjacent propagation constants then differ by nearly \(g\), so their relative phases refocus at \(2\pi/g\), exactly the ray pitch. A real finite core and a departure from parabolic index spoil exact equal spacing and can broaden a refocused image or pulse.

Estimate the wave scale of a smooth turning region

Near a transverse turning point \(x_t\) suppose \(k_x^2(x)\simeq-\gamma(x-x_t)\), where \(\gamma>0\) has units of inverse length cubed and the oscillatory side is \(x<x_t\). The transverse equation becomes \(u''-\gamma(x-x_t)u=0\). Rescale \(X=(x-x_t)\gamma^{1/3}\); the resulting dimensionless equation is \(u_{XX}-Xu=0\), whose decaying Airy solution matches a sinusoid with a definite quarter-phase shift on the oscillatory side. Thus the width of the turning region is \(l_t=\gamma^{-1/3}\). The wave does not reflect at an infinitely sharp geometric point, even for a perfectly smooth deterministic index profile.

If \(k_x^2\) changes by \(10^{10}\,{\rm m^{-2}}\) over \(10\,\mu{\rm m}\), the slope scale is \(10^{15}\,{\rm m^{-3}}\) and \(l_t\) is roughly \(10\,\mu{\rm m}\). A detector averaging over a larger transverse spot cannot establish the detailed penetration profile from a direct image. Increasing the index gradient makes the turning region narrower only as its inverse cube root. A ray sketch may still predict the trajectory well over millimetres while missing a micrometre-scale reflected-wave phase or modal shift. Compare the length scale of the particular observable with the turning region before replacing the local wave equation by a specular bounce.

A second reflection from a nearby turning point can overlap this Airy region. The usual two independent turning phases are then no longer justified. Solve the transverse wave equation with both sides at once or compare a matched model with the actual index profile and verify normalisable boundary conditions.

Check the paraxial assumptions against a measured ray map

Measure entrance and exit positions for several collimated beams launched at progressively larger distances from the axis. The ideal paraxial solution gives a straight line of exit versus entrance position with slope \(\cos(gL)\). Curvature in that plot can reveal aberration or departures from parabolic index. Comparing opposite launch offsets separates symmetric aberration from an off-centre guide or tilted coordinate origin.

Tutorial on a graded-index instrument

A guide with \(g=200\,{\rm m^{-1}}\) has length \(5.0\,{\rm mm}\). A ray enters at \(x_0=0.30\,{\rm mm}\) with zero internal slope. Predict its exit position and slope.

Work through the solution

The phase advance is \(gz=1.00\). Thus

\[x=0.30\cos1=0.162\,{\rm mm},\]
\[x'=-200(0.00030)\sin1=-0.0505.\]

The ray is still on its original side of the axis and moving inward. Treating this rod as an exact quarter-pitch element would predict a premature focus.

Using \(\lambda_0=0.60\,\mu{\rm m}\) and the example \(n_0,g\), estimate the approximate Gaussian ground-mode width scale \(w_0=(k_0n_0g)^{-1/2}\).

Work through the solution

Here \(k_0=1.047\times10^7\,{\rm m^{-1}}\), so \(w_0=17.8\,\mu{\rm m}\). The transverse field is proportional to \(\exp[-x^2/(2w_0^2)]\) in this convention. A quoted intensity \(1/e^2\) radius uses a different numerical factor. The computed width should be much smaller than the parabolic region for this ideal mode to be valid.

The ray-tube transport equation predicts unbounded amplitude at the common ray focus. Which measurement shows what the approximation omitted?

Work through the solution

Measure the finite transverse focal spot while changing wavelength and aperture. Wave diffraction limits resolution even when all geometric rays meet at one point. The leading transport approximation conserves flux as ray-tube area shrinks but cannot describe interference and phase near a caustic. A wave propagation or mode superposition through the same index map should produce a finite profile and permit a direct comparison with those measurements.

Optical fields, instruments and observations · 3. Measure rotation with two waves following the same path

A stationary optical loop gives equal return times for opposite propagation directions in a reciprocal medium. When the loop rotates the beams meet their starting beamsplitter at different times. Derive the measurable differential phase and compare a passive interferometer with a ring resonator.

Calculate the return times in a rotating circular loop

Take a vacuum circular loop of radius \(R\) rotating at constant angular speed \(\Omega\) in its plane, with a co-moving source, beamsplitter and receiver. In an inertial frame the receiver travels at \(v=\Omega R\). A beam propagating in the direction of rotation must catch up with the moving receiver, while the oppositely directed beam meets it early. For an ideal constrained circular optical path, \(ct_+=2\pi R+vt_+\) and \(ct_-=2\pi R-vt_-\). Thus \(t_\pm=2\pi R/(c\mp v)\), and their difference is \(4\pi Rv/(c^2-v^2)\). To first order in \(v/c\) this is \(4\Omega A/c^2\), with \(A=\pi R^2\).

The times just calculated are laboratory coordinate times. A clock on the rotating detector accumulates proper time with factor \(\sqrt{1-v^2/c^2}\); this changes the first-order result only at higher order. The local vacuum speed remains \(c\) for either beam. The effect comes from comparing their arrival at a moving part of a closed apparatus. There is no requirement to assign a different local fundamental light speed to the clockwise and anticlockwise beams.

A general co-moving loop at low rotational speed gives an extra time of flight \(d t_{\rm corr}=\mathbf v(\mathbf r)\cdot d\mathbf l/c^2\) along one orientation. Reversing orientation doubles its contribution to the difference. With \(\mathbf v=\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf r\),

\[\Delta t=\frac{2}{c^2}\oint(\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf r)\cdot d\mathbf l=\frac{4}{c^2}\boldsymbol\Omega\cdot\mathbf A,\]
\[\mathbf A=\frac12\oint\mathbf r\times d\mathbf l .\]

The oriented area handles tilted loops and folded paths. A figure-eight winding with two equal oppositely oriented lobes can enclose a large total unsigned area yet have zero net response to uniform rotation. Winding a fibre around the same area \(N\) times in the same orientation multiplies the directed area by \(N\).

Convert the time shift into optical phase

A continuous source of vacuum wavelength \(\lambda_0\) accumulates an optical phase difference \(\Delta\phi=\omega_0\Delta t=8\pi\boldsymbol\Omega\cdot\mathbf A/(\lambda_0c)\) at leading order. For a co-moving nondispersive dielectric loop the reciprocal static propagation time increases with index, but the standard first-order geometric Sagnac phase at a fixed external optical frequency retains this scale; applying relativistic velocity addition to propagation in the moving medium accounts for the cancellation. Flow of the medium relative to the loop, time-dependent index disturbances and nonreciprocal phase shifts can introduce additional effects.

For a hundred turns of radius \(R=0.10\,{\rm m}\), total directed area is \(3.142\,{\rm m^2}\). At \(\lambda_0=1.55\,\mu{\rm m}\) and \(\Omega=0.10\,{\rm rad/s}\) perpendicular to the loop, the time difference is only \(1.40\times10^{-17}\,{\rm s}\), yet the phase is \(0.0170\,{\rm rad}\). Direct electronic time-of-flight timing would be exceptionally demanding, while interference converts this small delay into an intensity change. Tilting the normal by \(60^\circ\) relative to rotation halves the signal and reversing the winding or rotation reverses its sign.

With equal returning intensities, a detector output can be written \(I=I_{\rm mean}[1+\mathcal V\cos(\Delta\phi+\phi_b)]\), where \(\mathcal V\) is visibility and \(\phi_b\) a controlled bias. Near \(\phi_b=0\) a small rotation changes intensity only quadratically and does not identify its sign. At quadrature, \(\phi_b=\pi/2\), the change is approximately \(-I_{\rm mean}\mathcal V\Delta\phi\). Alternating the applied bias or using both complementary outputs can reject a common power drift. A measured percentage intensity change only directly represents phase when the visibility and operating point are known.

Derive a ring resonator's beat instead of a passive phase

In an active vacuum ring laser or a tracked passive ring cavity, each propagation direction can have a separate resonant frequency satisfying an integer round-trip phase. In the simple nondispersive vacuum case let perimeter be \(P\), unperturbed time \(T=P/c\), and resonance frequency \(\nu=m/T\). A small directional time difference \(\Delta t\) gives a resonance frequency splitting of magnitude \(\Delta\nu\simeq\nu\Delta t/T=4A\Omega_\perp/(\lambda_0P)\). This readout measures a beat frequency. The passive interferometer measured a single-pass phase difference; the dimensions and perimeter dependence of the two formulas distinguish them.

A square vacuum ring of side \(1.0\,{\rm m}\) has \(A=1.0\,{\rm m^2}\), \(P=4.0\,{\rm m}\). At \(633\,{\rm nm}\) with \(\Omega_\perp=10^{-5}\,{\rm rad/s}\), the predicted directional splitting is \(15.8\,{\rm Hz}\). Double every side at fixed wavelength and angular speed: area quadruples but perimeter doubles, so the beat doubles. In a physical active ring, backscattering can couple the counterpropagating modes and produce a dead band near low rotation. A zero observed beat does not uniquely show a stationary loop unless this coupling and other bias effects have been characterised.

Estimate a rotation sensitivity from counted photons

At a balanced quadrature readout with visibility \({\cal V}\), a small phase changes the mean differential count divided by total count by roughly \({\cal V}\Delta\phi\). For independently detected photons and a shot-noise-limited balanced measurement the standard deviation of that normalised difference is approximately \(1/\sqrt N\), giving a phase uncertainty near \(1/({\cal V}\sqrt N)\). This estimate assumes the photons used in the signal actually reached the detectors and that gain mismatch, intensity noise and nonreciprocal drift do not dominate. It is a count budget, separate from the geometric scale relating phase to rotation.

For \({\cal V}=0.80\) and \(N=10^8\) detected photons the ideal phase uncertainty is \(1.25\times10^{-4}\,{\rm rad}\). The hundred-turn example then has projected-rate uncertainty about \(7.4\times10^{-4}\,{\rm rad/s}\) over that acquisition interval. Repeating statistically independent acquisitions improves the random part by the square root of their number, but cannot reduce a persistent reference bias in the same way. Rotating the coil normal through a known angle changes the physical signal as a cosine and can help identify additive biases. The calibration must use the actual normal orientation and total directed area, rather than assuming the apparatus remains level.

A longer fibre winding increases area at fixed turn radius while also increasing attenuation and susceptibility to distributed disturbances. The ideal phase scale grows with turn count; the number of detected photons and long-time noise can change at the same time. Compare those quantities independently when evaluating a different winding.

Tutorial on rejecting false rotations

What happens if a rigid loop translates at constant velocity without rotating in the first-order geometric calculation?

Work through the solution

Its velocity is constant around the path, and \(\oint\mathbf v\cdot d\mathbf l=\mathbf v\cdot\oint d\mathbf l=0\). There is no corresponding Sagnac area signal. A moving segment of a nonrigid loop can have a different line integral; the stated cancellation is for common translation of the entire closed path.

A passive interferometer with visibility \(0.80\) reports a quadrature change \(\Delta I/I_{\rm mean}=-0.0080\) for the hundred-turn geometry above. Estimate the projected rotation.

Work through the solution

At quadrature the phase change is \(0.0100\,{\rm rad}\). The example scale is \(0.170\,{\rm rad}\) of phase per radian per second, giving \(\Omega_\perp\simeq0.0589\,{\rm rad/s}\). Use the actual directed area and vacuum wavelength. A visibility correction or a bias angle error propagates directly into the rotation estimate.

Can a drifting temperature distribution mimic rotation in a geometrically reciprocal fibre loop?

Work through the solution

Two counterpropagating waves encounter a given fibre segment at different times. A static reciprocal phase shift cancels to leading order, but a changing local index can give different integrated phases on the two traversals. Compare runs with reversed physical rotation, symmetric thermal conditions and a calibrated artificial phase bias. Winding arrangement and timing of disturbances help distinguish a true oriented-area response from a transient nonreciprocal measurement error.

The Sagnac interferometer chapter hosted by Rutgers derives the phase shift of the two counterpropagating beams.

Compare a resonator's mode conditions with the directional frequency readout.

Optical fields, instruments and observations · 4. Dispersive pulse compression and a detector's view of a tilted pulse

An ultrashort pulse can broaden because its spectral components acquire different group delays or because different parts of its beam arrive at different times. Derive the two mechanisms separately, predict when added glass can shorten an already chirped pulse, and decide which measurements distinguish them.

Derive the propagation of a spectral envelope

Write the positive-frequency electric field as the real carrier with complex envelope \(A(z,t)\), using the Fourier convention \(A(t)\propto\int\tilde A(\Omega)e^{-i\Omega t}d\Omega\),

\[\Omega=\omega-\omega_0.\]

Propagation through a transparent homogeneous length \(L\) multiplies the spectrum by \(e^{ik(\omega)L}\). Expand \(k=k_0+k_1\Omega+k_2\Omega^2/2+\cdots\). The constant is an overall phase, the linear term shifts the pulse by group delay \(k_1L\), and the quadratic term gives group-delay dispersion \(D=k_2L\) in seconds squared. The group-delay difference between spectral components separated by \(\Delta\Omega\) is \(D\Delta\Omega\).

Take a Gaussian spectral amplitude \(\tilde A(\Omega)=A_0\exp[-\tau_0^2\Omega^2/2]\), and give it an initial quadratic spectral phase \(\phi_2\Omega^2/2\). The effective quadratic coefficient after propagation is \(\Phi=\phi_2+D\). Fourier transforming the Gaussian at the retarded time \(T=t-k_1L\) gives an envelope proportional to \((\tau_0^2-i\Phi)^{-1/2}\exp[-T^2/(2(\tau_0^2-i\Phi))]\). Its intensity is Gaussian with temporal scale \(\tau=\tau_0\sqrt{1+(\Phi/\tau_0^2)^2}\), where intensity is proportional to \(\exp[-T^2/\tau^2]\). The intensity full width at half maximum is \(2\sqrt{\ln2}\,\tau\); keeping this convention explicit prevents confusing amplitude widths, intensity widths and rms durations.

The intensity spectrum is unchanged by quadratic phase in a lossless element. Temporal broadening reduces the peak at fixed integrated pulse energy by \(\tau_0/\tau\). Its duration depends on \(\Phi^2\), so one measured width at a single setting cannot determine the sign of chirp. Measuring the width after a known added dispersion does break that degeneracy. The full temporal phase contains a quadratic term giving instantaneous frequency that changes approximately linearly through the pulse. A transform-limited Gaussian has \(\Delta t_{\rm FWHM}\Delta\nu_{\rm FWHM}=2\ln2/\pi\simeq0.441\) when frequency is in cycles per second.

Calculate an imperfect compressor setting

Start with a transform-limited duration \(30.0\,{\rm fs}\), so \(\tau_0=18.0\,{\rm fs}\) and \(\tau_0^2=325\,{\rm fs^2}\). A spectral phase of \(+400\,{\rm fs^2}\) broadens this pulse to about \(47.6\,{\rm fs}\), with peak intensity about \(0.630\) of its transform-limited value. Passing it through a component that contributes \(-300\,{\rm fs^2}\) leaves \(\Phi=100\,{\rm fs^2}\) and duration \(31.4\,{\rm fs}\). The same component applied to a pulse initially at \(-400\,{\rm fs^2}\) instead gives \(-700\,{\rm fs^2}\) and substantially more broadening. A quoted dispersion accuracy without the initial sign does not establish compression.

Experimental scans are simplest in squared width. Vary known \(D\) and fit \(\Delta t^2=\Delta t_0^2[1+((D+\phi_2)/\tau_0^2)^2]\). The parabola has a minimum at \(D=-\phi_2\). A non-Gaussian spectrum or a cubic spectral phase can produce pedestals, asymmetric profiles or a minimum width larger than the transform limit inferred from spectrum. In an intensity autocorrelation a Gaussian pulse has a correlation width \(\sqrt2\) times its intensity width. Apply the correct deconvolution for the assumed shape, or measure a spectrally resolved temporal diagnostic when the shape itself is unknown.

Derive pulse-front tilt and the spatially averaged width

Suppose the centre arrival time varies across one transverse coordinate as \(T_c(x)=s x\). The slope \(s\) has units of time per length. A grating or misaligned dispersive optic can correlate frequency, propagation angle and position. For a local pulse of intensity \(\exp[-(t-sx)^2/\tau^2]\) and beam weighting \(\exp[-x^2/w^2]\), a detector integrating the whole beam sees the temporal convolution of the local profile with the distribution of arrival centres. Independent Gaussian variances add, giving measured temporal scale \(\tau_{\rm d}^2=\tau^2+s^2 w^2\). A single-pixel trace at fixed \(x\) remains narrow and shifts in time. A whole-beam trace broadens even when no additional spectral phase changes along any one local ray.

With \(w=0.50\,{\rm mm}\) in this intensity convention and \(s=40\,{\rm fs/mm}\), the extra scale is \(20\,{\rm fs}\). A locally transform-limited \(30.0\,{\rm fs}\) pulse has \(\tau_0=18.0\,{\rm fs}\), so the detector width becomes

\[2\sqrt{\ln2}\sqrt{18.0^2+20^2}=44.8\,{\rm fs}.\]

Changing the aperture alters the broadened trace by changing the sampled transverse weighting. A hard slit does not produce a Gaussian weighting, so the variance addition remains applicable for independent position and local pulse shape but the full width at half maximum need not follow the same Gaussian conversion.

Angular dispersion can create such a delay gradient. Near the reference propagation axis a spectral plane wave carries transverse phase \(k_x(\omega)x\). In the inverse transform the term \((dk_x/d\omega)\Omega x\) shifts the local envelope time by \((dk_x/d\omega)x\), giving \(s=dk_x/d\omega\) under the adopted conventions. Keeping only \(k_x(\omega_0)\) accounts for a tilted carrier phase but misses the tilted pulse front. Mapping cross-correlation delay versus transverse position separates this linear slope from uniform material dispersion.

Predict a compression scan and perturb its detection area

The live temporal traces compare a local pulse and the second trace integrated across a known Gaussian beam, with the same normalised pulse energy. Predict the added dispersion that gives the narrowest local pulse, then turn up the transverse delay slope without moving the compressor. Which curve keeps its width? A larger minimum in an area-integrated scan is compatible with spatial averaging even when a local sample reaches its transform limit. Use a second spatial sampling condition before fitting that extra duration entirely to a higher-order material phase.

Open this experiment online

Check the group delay with two narrow spectral slices

A pulse-width scan determines the magnitude of residual quadratic phase but can hide its sign at a single setting. Split off two narrow spectral slices centred at offsets \(+\Delta\Omega/2\) and \(-\Delta\Omega/2\), and compare their envelope delays against a common reference. To first order the arrival-time difference is \(\Phi\Delta\Omega\). At \(\Phi=+400\,{\rm fs^2}\) and an ordinary-frequency separation of \(5.0\,{\rm THz}\), \(\Delta\Omega=0.0314\,{\rm rad/fs}\), giving a \(12.6\,{\rm fs}\) delay difference. Under the adopted propagation convention the higher-frequency slice arrives later for positive dispersion.

The spectral filters themselves can contribute unequal group delays and broaden their selected pulses. Calibrate their differential delay using a reference path or a reference spectrum with independently characterised phase. Narrowing each slice improves its frequency selectivity while increasing its temporal duration, so a cross-correlation delay fit must retain adequate signal and temporal stability. Measuring several frequency centres permits a slope fit; visible curvature of delay against angular frequency indicates the need for a cubic or higher spectral phase. A camera position offset correlated with selected wavelength can add an apparent slope through pulse-front tilt, so compare at the same physical beam position as well as after spatial integration.

Tutorial on interpreting pulse widths

A \(50\,{\rm fs}\) transform-limited Gaussian has a measured optical bandwidth of \(5.0\,{\rm THz}\). Are the two measurements consistent if both are intensity full widths at half maximum?

Work through the solution

Their product is \(0.250\), below the Gaussian transform-limited value \(0.441\). A \(50\,{\rm fs}\) Gaussian requires about \(8.82\,{\rm THz}\) of ordinary-frequency width. Check whether the reported bandwidth was angular frequency, an amplitude rather than intensity width, or clipped by the spectrometer. Pure spectral dispersion cannot reduce the time-bandwidth product below the transform limit.

The minimum duration occurs at \(D=-250\,{\rm fs^2}\). What is the input quadratic phase, and what additional observation would show significant residual cubic phase?

Work through the solution

The simple model gives

\[\phi_2=+250\,{\rm fs^2}.\]

A minimum much longer than the transform limit for the measured spectrum, or asymmetric temporal satellites that persist near the minimum, motivates a higher-order spectral-phase measurement. Spatial averaging must be controlled separately before attributing every residual feature to cubic dispersion.

Two local cross-correlation peaks differ by \(24\,{\rm fs}\) between transverse positions separated by \(0.60\,{\rm mm}\). What extra rms time spread is expected across the \(w=0.50\,{\rm mm}\) Gaussian beam?

Work through the solution

The slope is \(40\,{\rm fs/mm}\). Since the transverse rms width is \(w/\sqrt2\) for the stated intensity convention, the rms arrival spread is

\[40(0.50)/\sqrt2=14.1\,{\rm fs}.\]

The corresponding Gaussian scale parameter \(s w=20\,{\rm fs}\) is larger by \(\sqrt2\). Match the width convention to the formula used by the detector fit.

Kolner and Nazarathy connect dispersive evolution to temporal imaging.

Compare quadratic phase in temporal propagation with its spatial diffraction counterpart.

Optical fields, instruments and observations · 5. Which polarisation measurements identify an optical element?

A retarder rotates the state of a coherent beam, while unresolved mixtures can reduce its measured degree of polarisation. Derive the passage from Jones fields to a Mueller matrix, reconstruct a simple instrument from measured intensities, and identify claims the available input states cannot support.

Move from a field vector to a coherence matrix

For a monochromatic transverse field write the complex amplitudes as \(\mathbf e=(E_x,E_y)^T\) with time convention \(e^{-i\omega t}\). A deterministic optical element gives

\[\mathbf e_{\rm out}=J\mathbf e_{\rm in}.\]

A detector averaging over unresolved time, frequency or spatial channels instead sees a coherence matrix \(C=\langle\mathbf e\mathbf e^\dagger\rangle\), which remains a \(2\times2\) Hermitian positive semidefinite matrix. A fixed Jones element acts as \(C_{\rm out}=JC_{\rm in}J^\dagger\). An incoherent mixture of distinct elements acts as \(\sum_a p_a J_a C_{\rm in}J_a^\dagger\), generally not representable by one common Jones matrix.

Use the convention \(S_0=C_{xx}+C_{yy}\), \(S_1=C_{xx}-C_{yy}\), \(S_2=2\Re C_{xy}\), \(S_3=2\Im C_{xy}\). This explicitly sets the sign of the circular component independent of competing right-handedness naming conventions. The determinant is \([S_0^2-S_1^2-S_2^2-S_3^2]/4\). Positive eigenvalues require \(P=\sqrt{S_1^2+S_2^2+S_3^2}/S_0\le1\) for nonzero signal. A pure Jones field gives rank one and \(P=1\); a mixture can give smaller \(P\). A fixed lossy polariser can convert an unpolarised input into a fully polarised transmitted field, so attenuation and loss of polarisation are separate observations.

Derive the Mueller matrix of a retarder and an unresolved mixture

Write \(C=\tfrac12\sum_j S_j\tau_j\), with \(\tau_0\) identity, \(\tau_1={\rm diag}(1,-1)\), \(\tau_2\) the real off-diagonal Pauli matrix, and \(\tau_3\) chosen with \(+i\) in its upper off-diagonal entry for the stipulated convention. Since \({\rm tr}(\tau_i\tau_j)=2\delta_{ij}\),

\[S_i={\rm tr}(\tau_i C).\]

Inserting \(JCJ^\dagger\) gives the real Mueller matrix \(M_{ij}=\tfrac12{\rm tr}(\tau_i J\tau_jJ^\dagger)\). A common phase on \(J\) cancels. These intensity measurements cannot determine a spatially uniform optical carrier phase, even if every Mueller entry is known.

For \(J={\rm diag}(1,e^{i\delta})\), \(C_{xy}\) is multiplied by \(e^{-i\delta}\). Thus \(S_0,S_1\) remain fixed while \(S_2'=S_2\cos\delta+S_3\sin\delta\) and \(S_3'=-S_2\sin\delta+S_3\cos\delta\). A \(45^\circ\) linear input has \((S_1,S_2,S_3)/S_0=(0,1,0)\). A quarter-wave retardance sends it to \((0,0,-1)\) in the stated sign convention; a half-wave retardance sends it to \((0,-1,0)\). Measuring only horizontal and vertical powers would return equal readings for all three states. A diagonal analyser and a quarter-wave analyser are needed to distinguish them.

Now suppose half the unresolved beam spots undergo retardance \(+\delta\) and half \(-\delta\). Average their Mueller actions rather than averaging Jones fields. The \(S_2,S_3\) cross terms cancel and both components acquire a factor \(\cos\delta\). For \(\delta=\pi/3\), the diagonal linear input becomes \(P=0.50\), while a horizontal input remains fully polarised. This is anisotropic depolarisation. Resolving the two spots would reveal two fully polarised local states. A coherent recombination of amplitudes with a controlled phase is a different physical experiment and requires adding fields before averaging.

Reconstruct an element from independent incident states

Put four independent known incident Stokes vectors into columns of a matrix \(W\), and the corresponding measured output vectors into \(Y\). A linear optical response obeys \(Y=MW\), so \(M=YW^{-1}\) if \(W\) is nonsingular. Horizontal, vertical, diagonal-linear and a circular state with calibrated sign are one possible independent set. Four purely linear input states have \(S_3=0\), so their columns span at most a three-dimensional subspace. No amount of repetition of only those input states reveals the fourth response column. Additional independent preparations are more informative than merely increasing nominal data volume.

For normalised inputs \(H=(1,1,0,0)\), \(V=(1,-1,0,0)\), \(D=(1,0,1,0)\), \(C_+=(1,0,0,1)\), call measured outputs \(y_H,y_V,y_D,y_C\). The first column is \((y_H+y_V)/2\), the second \((y_H-y_V)/2\), the third \(y_D-M_{:0}\), and the fourth \(y_C-M_{:0}\). These differences assume known equal incident intensities or a correction to a common monitored intensity. A source that dims during one preparation can look like diattenuation. Measure an independent input monitor and repeat the preparations in a different order to distinguish drift from element properties.

Suppose the outputs are \((1,\pm1,0,0)\) for \(H,V\), \((1,0,0.50,0)\) for \(D\), and \((1,0,0,0.50)\) for \(C_+\). The reconstructed matrix is diagonal with entries \(1,1,0.50,0.50\), as in the unresolved equal-retardance example. The matrix establishes the action on Stokes states but does not uniquely identify that microscopic mechanism. A temporal fluctuator and an unresolved spatial mixture can give the same matrix. Change acquisition time, spectral bandwidth or spatial aperture to test which ensemble the detector is averaging.

Recover the retardance of a real plate with an imperfect analyser

Send a unit \(45^\circ\) linear input through the diagonal retarder above. Measuring the two independent output components gives

\[S_2/S_0=\cos\delta,\ S_3/S_0=-\sin\delta.\]

Thus \(\delta=\operatorname{atan2}(-S_3,S_2)\) modulo \(2\pi\). A fit using only the diagonal analyser, and hence only \(\cos\delta\), cannot distinguish opposite retardances and becomes insensitive near a maximum or minimum. The circular analyser supplies the missing quadrature. If the beam is partially depolarised equally in these two components, the angle from their ratio can remain well determined while their magnitude shrinks.

Suppose calibrated output readings give

\[S_2/S_0=0.48,\ S_3/S_0=-0.64,\]

with negligible \(S_1\). The inferred retardance is \(\operatorname{atan2}(0.64,0.48)=53.1^\circ\), while the degree of polarisation is \(0.80\). A single ideal Jones retarder acting on a pure input at one frequency could produce the angle but would preserve unit degree; something in the measured ensemble or instrument reduced it. A pure retarder model fitted only to \(S_2\) would instead infer \(\arccos0.48=61.3^\circ\), wrongly absorbing the contrast loss into its phase.

An analyser with leakage can also reduce the measured Stokes components. A differential pair of orthogonal polarisers with identical leakage fraction \(\eta\) scales its normalised difference by \((1-\eta)/(1+\eta)\). Calibrate each channel using known pure preparations and monitor intensity drifts before assigning all contrast loss to the specimen. Repeating with narrower spectral bandwidth or a smaller beam spot then tests the ensemble dependence of the inferred retardance and depolarisation.

Separate common optical phase from relative retardance

Inserting an isotropic plate can add the same phase to both orthogonal components of every coherent input. Its ideal Mueller matrix is unchanged because \(J\) and \(e^{i\alpha}J\) act identically on the coherence matrix. An interferometer with an external reference arm can nevertheless detect the added phase. This experiment supplies precisely the reference that a standalone polarimeter lacks. The two instruments can thus disagree about whether anything changed while measuring distinct physical observables accurately.

Tutorial on polarimetric inference

An analyser reports intensities \(I_H=0.60,\ I_V=0.40,\ I_D=0.75,\ I_A=0.25\) in common units. Which Stokes entries follow, and what remains unknown?

Work through the solution

The first pair gives

\[S_0=1.00,S_1=0.20;\]

the diagonal pair gives \(S_2=0.50\). No circular analysis was performed, so \(S_3\) remains unknown within \(|S_3|\le\sqrt{1-0.20^2-0.50^2}=0.843\). Assuming a pure state would set its magnitude to that bound but would still not determine its sign. A partially polarised beam can have any smaller magnitude.

An ideal deterministic \(J={\rm diag}(\sqrt{0.8},\sqrt{0.2})\) transmits an unpolarised input with unit incident intensity. What are the output intensity and degree of polarisation?

Work through the solution

The input coherence matrix is half the identity. The transmitted diagonal entries are \(0.40\) and \(0.10\), so \(S_0=0.50,S_1=0.30\) and \(P=0.60\). The element selectively transmits one component of the mixture. It has not generated optical energy; half the incident intensity was lost.

A fitted Mueller matrix maps one measured positive input into \(P=1.08\). How should the inference be checked?

Work through the solution

That output violates positivity for this convention. Check dark subtraction, intensity normalisation, handedness of the circular analyser and conditioning of the preparation and analysis matrices. Collect more independent states with uncertainties and fit within a physical response model. Clipping each output degree to unity separately need not produce one common linear physical Mueller map for the instrument.

Reconstruct a Stokes state numerically from analyser intensities.

TU Delft's optical course covers polarisation states and their measurement.
